Build 0.7.64 · Save format 31 · 7 October 2026

CityBuilderSim

A macroeconomic city simulator: an age pyramid that decides the workforce, a labour market that prices it and in which every worker takes the best-paid post they qualify for, builders who share their crews by what each site can use and are paid at today's wages, with the player's hand on their queue to reorder it, rush it on overtime, cancel an order or pull a building down, seventeen private sectors on one template trading real goods on real markets — two of them selling to the world, one of them to the mills, one hauling the others' freight, one building the cars the households drive and two lifting and refining the oil they burn — and raising their money with shares and bonds that trade on order books, a bank that is a business, pricing a loan from what it costs, keeping its own capital and passing to the city when it fails, a central bank whose balance sheet is the only place money is made, whose dial is the price of it, whose holdings can reach every bond the city sells and whose credibility anchors what people expect prices to do, and so the level every money constant is struck at, a city founded with a name and a money of its own on a world of its own seed — an Earth-sized square of sea, coast and lakes with seven resources lying in finite fields — buying its ground band by band at a price its crowding sets, drawing no more fresh water than its lakes yield, drawn on a map building by building, made to borrow for what it builds, and with a fund it saves in, trades by hand and draws on at a dial, households that each keep their own books including the people the books used to leave out, illness that kills, crime that has reasons, households that eat thirteen separate foods, choose between them by income and ask for them at a price the shops clear on, with a voucher the city can pay toward them, own cars, borrow for them and buy their fuel, and spend what is left over a counter, a road that carries three kinds of traffic and can be relieved but never deleted, beside buses the treasury pays for and riders who weigh the fare against what driving costs them, a trade band that is three quarters freight, a currency with a balance of payments behind it, a vault that spends to defend it and a real rate that moves it, and every screen drawn as pictures that open onto the figures behind them, a history among them that the player can drag, zoom and read their own decisions on. This is what it currently has and does.

01

The month

Everything in the game happens in this order, and most of the hard bugs in its history have been a line that made it into one phase and not the other. The order is genuinely a sequence, so it is numbered — and since 18 September it is also generated: docs/month-order.md lists every top-level statement of Game.nextMonth() and SimulationEngine.simulateMonth() with its line, which is what this list is struck against.

  1. 1
    The month's money is struck, and what falls due is rolled, before the calendar turns
    First the month's money constants are struck at the price level people expect, the level the last month ended on (expectations.strikeLevel(), restrikeMoneyConstants(), 0.7.42): the shelf's floor, the land office's bases, what every building costs and its upkeep, the clinics' fees, tuition, the pension's wage base, the fare, a founding share and the bank's fees and paid-in. So the month lives at one level from here to the next press, and no constant reads a price it sets. Then what falls due is rolled, in the gap between two presses, where a player's own issue lands, so that it settles to its buyers and crosses the audit window exactly as a hand-made issue does. In a December the city's fund takes its dial's share of the year's surplus first (fundYearEnd()), so that no surplus is counted twice; then the rollover reads the principal that falls due next month, nets it against whatever of last year's surplus is still unused, and issues the rest, sized to the cash it has to raise (rollMaturities(), 0.7.13), and strikes how much of the central bank's own maturing paper it will take again at issue. Then the month turns, and as it turns the buildings the player ordered demolished between the presses close (closeDemolished(), 0.7.22), before anything in the month reads the city: their posts, homes and places are gone from its first count. No money moves there; it moved when the order was placed.
  2. 2
    The central bank settles with the treasury, and the promises are paid
    Before anything is priced, because the advances and the remittance are money made and destroyed and have to fall inside the audit's window. With the autopilot on — and a new game founds on it since 0.7.13 — the Taylor rule takes the dial, aimed at the player's target and, since 0.7.52, as strictly as the player holds it. Then the treasury pays the interest on last month's advances, takes last month's profit back as the remittance and the city's fund's withdrawal (by default a twelfth of 3% of all it is worth, from its cash; over the default since 0.7.48, last month's sale first), repays its central bank out of any cash above zero, pays down arrears — since 0.7.55 a line of each payee's cash flow, Arrears paid by the city, where the money had reached the till and no line — and is advanced whatever it is still short (settleTreasury()). Then the central bank takes its own maturing par again, as an add-on to what the city sold between the presses (rollCentralBankAtIssue(), 0.7.15), and the holdings dial trades the city's term paper toward its target (openMarketOperation()), so this month's quotes carry both. Then the students' grant and EI are paid on the people the month opens with, in the month they are credited, and the bank pays its profit tax.
  3. 3
    The world turns and the land office re-prices
    World prices advance, the exchange rate is taken, the cost of living goes into the labour market — since 0.7.42, once the basket is based, half of its move the inflation people expect and half a forty-eighth of the way to the index — and the land office strikes its ground in US dollars and what businesses pay for it in the city's own money. What the city pays outside and what businesses pay inside are both inputs to everything below.
  4. 4
    Business credit is priced, interest billed, property tax charged
    What its money costs the bank goes to the city's floor first, and a failed bank lends nothing. Then the bank's capital rule is read once and handed to every desk that lends this month, the businesses' and the families', its bonds and its book's concentration re-read before it; every business's spread is set on the bank's prime at this month's dial, the landlords' insured mortgages at the insured rate beside it; and the businesses' bond coupons are struck on the same face the interest bills are, with property tax charged beside them. Before the statements run, so the figures each sector banks are net of it.
  5. 5
    Every building pays to stand, and every shipper pays its freight
    1% of build cost a year, charged to every owner in cash and materials and construction points — placed as a real order with the builders, not a line item. Since 0.7.19 the cash is the building's cash cost with its labour at today's builders' wages, and the bill carries the builders' sales tax, which a business that makes taxable supplies claims back and the landlords and the bank bear; the city's comes back to it, since the city collects it. A forty-year stock at 1% a year is about 40% of the city's whole construction output. Then the railway's invoice on the month's trade, for the same reason: an expense on fourteen sets of books and revenue on the fifteenth.
  6. 6
    Fifteen income statements are struck
    Retail, real estate, industry, construction, heavy industry, mining, materials, business services, manufacturing, agriculture, food processing, rail, automotive, luxury retail, restaurants — each at the utilisation the month was traded at, not at today's, and each from the same template. The VAT is settled from the same figures between the two halves, and since 0.7.19 the sales tax a business claims back on the buildings it bought reaches its till as cash back on them. Since 0.7.44 the strike also settles the grocers' supplier credit: the till pays the month's stock bill but for what its suppliers lent against it, and repays what the strike before left owed, out of the takings of the stock that has since sold.
  7. 7
    National accounts, then the household books
    Household spending is consumption, read from the other end, so the people cannot be shown paying a figure the shops were not shown receiving. Consumption is three counters now — the grocers', the boutiques' and the kitchens' — plus the rent. The households' month strikes the bank's account fees, and since 0.7.19 its customers with them, which the branch rule in step 9 reads; and since 0.7.49 it charges the transit fares to the rows that ride and the month's fuel to the rows that drive. The government's part of the accounts carries the schools' and transit's costs since the same version, as step 11 struck them the month before.
  8. 8
    The households buy cars, then spend the rest over a counter
    With the month's money fresh and before the investor can see it: the second-hand market clears first, because nobody pays forty for a car they can have for twelve, then the showroom; then the boutiques, and the kitchens right after, because they are the same kind of purchase out of the same surplus. The cars, the pieces and the meals count as demand for next month's strike.
  9. 9
    The private sector takes its turn
    Loans advance; a bond that falls due is paid from its issuer's till to its holders; matured principal comes back, and a till left short calls its dollars home, sells the other sectors' bonds it holds and goes to the shortfall desk, which lends or sells a bond for it, and what it still cannot pay defaults, a slice the size of the part it cannot pay. Plant is retired and sold to the builders for its material, the bank's branches that this month's account fees no longer cover at last month's cost close (0.7.19), the month's defaults are written off against the bank, the insured part paid by the treasury, and each sector forecasts its own signal six months out, orders only what the city could staff (0.7.18), and funds what it plans in a fixed order: sell shares, spend the till, call money home from abroad, and only then borrow — a bond where one is no dearer than the loan, the bank for the rest, and a landlord's homes on an insured mortgage.
  10. 10
    Construction advances; roads recount; the builders strike their crews, the posts are counted and the labour market reprices
    The sites take the month's points by the crew each building can use (0.7.17), and once the player has a hand on the queue the city's share goes to its own sites in the player's order and a rushed site works its month on overtime (0.7.22; see the builders, section 9). The builders buy the material that work drew on and recognise revenue on the same figure the sites advanced by; each owner then pays what the month's draw cost past what its quote allowed for it, or is refunded (0.7.19), and the hand's month is settled after it — the overtime's premium paid and paid out as wages, a cancelled order's refund, a finished demolition's material and ground (0.7.22). A shop that opens this morning trades this month and puts its trucks on the road this month — roads are recounted before anything reads them. Then the builders strike the share of their posts the work ahead needs and lay the rest off (0.7.17), and only then are the posts recounted and wages walk toward what scarcity says per band, and per licence, against this month's posts and before anybody ages.
  11. 11
    Demographics
    Age; give birth; die of three separate causes — the band's own rate, illness, and violence; migrate, never more than the room the placement has left at the doors standing today (0.7.17); rebuild the households against last month's; commit and record this month's crime; settle health, education and the police; then the commute (0.7.49): the road is told what share of its commuters have no car of their own and what a journey's fuel costs, since 0.7.62 its litres at the price they land at (setCommute(), Motoring.journeyFuel()), the fare draws its riders, the drivers' fuel is drawn — since 0.7.62 off the refiners' tanks first and from abroad for the rest (motoring.drawFuel()), where it had been struck as a bill (setFuelBill()) — and transit's bill with it; advance the ring of the sick.
  12. 12
    The economy and the services sync
    Population, wages, jobs, the fill rate and the utilisation ratios reach every sector — the road ratio per sector, because only a tram can make two businesses on the same street face different jams, and the health ratio the demographics step has just set.
  13. 13
    Cash moves, every market clears, ore is lifted, the paper is paid, the government's books are re-struck
    The utility books what its customers were charged; every market clears and every maker produces in one pass; the mines ask the ground, and since 0.7.62 the oil wells too, each lifting what is left of the oil the city owns (LandManager.extractOil()), the refiners taking their crude and the railway its fuel as buyers. The food vouchers the sale took are paid at once, a promise in the month of the sale (0.7.43); then the tax take comes in and the promises go out a line at a time, since 0.7.49 transit's wages and upkeep among them, after the police's, where until then nobody paid them. The paper sold between the presses settles to its holders, the households taking their share first, and the month's coupons and principal are paid holder by holder. The books are re-struck at the bottom of the month, against the same period's spending and after the coupon is known, because the start-of-month call mixed two months' tax fields. Since 0.7.57 the ground's month closes with them: the forest grows back a 240th of what was cut and the month's flows of ground and ore are cleared (landManager.endMonth()).
  14. 14
    The bank settles, the owners are paid, the shares and the bonds trade
    The bank pays for whatever paper the households did not take and earns a month of the discount on what it holds; then lending and its fees, repayments, the interest by who paid it and its bonds' coupons, what the month asks of its allowance, its staff and its branches — the charter exempt from the operating cost since 0.7.19 — the carry trade, the window and the interest on its reserves at the central bank, and, if it has failed and the city's setting is automatic, its resolution for its shares that month; then the cost of funds is struck once and final, the central bank strikes its month's profit, the savers are paid, then dividends, then the shares trade, then the city's preferred falls due at its anniversaries, then the bonds trade, then whatever is still idle goes abroad, the households' by the same rule as the sectors', and a household whose paper no longer out-yields its deposits sells a little of it back. What lands after the bank's close can still fail it, so it is looked at again, and a bank under its minimum asks the city for preferred.
  15. 15
    The currency moves, and the money is counted
    MoneyAudit strikes pooled after − pooled before = inflows − outflows across every pool in the city and every flow across its border. A cent of residual is a finding — since 0.7.54 a cent, or a part in a trillion of what moved where that is more, because in a city of billions a double's own step passes the cent (MoneyAudit.tolerance()); and a comparison held to a floor of its own is never held tighter than 64 of a double's steps at the size of the figures it compares (MoneyAudit.ULP_STEPS; 8 in 0.7.63, 64 since 0.7.64), because an identity is a signed sum of figures that each carry their own rounding, and a floor of a millionth of a unit is under a double's step from about a billion units up. The hot money moves just before the strike, because it moves a pool; then the balance of payments, the currency and the price index, each reading a month that has finished settling, and straight after the index what people expect prices to do: credibility against the target and the lean the rate showed, expected inflation and the price level next month's constants are struck at (expectations.takeMonth(), 0.7.42), handed on to the investors, the currency and every sticky seller's price (handOnExpectations()). The index has five parts on a trailing year's spending and is chained since 0.7.43. The currency reads the real rate against the world's, ex ante since 0.7.42, and drifts at the credible part of expected inflation (setExpectedDrift()), and on a month it is pushed weaker on a deficit the central bank sells the vault's dollars against the push — a capital transaction booked after the audit, because no pool moves on it. Nothing after the audit may move a pool. Once the month is recorded the history folds any calendar year all of whose months have left its newest six thousand (0.7.55), and the city's map takes the month's buildings (reconcileMap(), 0.7.60). Every twelfth month autosaves at its very end, once the month is recorded (recordMonth()) — since 0.7.52; until then the file was written at the top, after the calendar turned and before anything ran, and a city loaded from it never ran that month — and the game also autosaves before any skip and on quit, both between the presses.

What moved since the last edition. One statement went in and one changed shape, at set places, and the order of the rest held. In 0.7.60 the city's map takes the month's buildings straight after the month is recorded and before the autosave (reconcileMap()), after the month's construction and demolitions, so the map a save writes is the month's. In 0.7.62, at 6d, the drivers' fuel struck as a bill (motoring.setFuelBill()) became fuel drawn as a good (motoring.drawFuel()), and the journey's fuel the road is told before the fare is read at the price it lands at (Motoring.journeyFuel()). Inside statements that were already there, 0.7.55 has the arrears the treasury pays land on each payee's statement and the record fold the history's oldest whole years; 0.7.57 has the government's books close the ground's month; 0.7.59 has the services read the fresh water limit on every path that updates them; and 0.7.62 has the markets clear crude and fuel, the wells ask the ground as the mines do, and the railway draw its fuel off the same tanks. 0.7.55 to 0.7.59, 0.7.61, 0.7.63 and 0.7.64 moved no statement: the generated list is 0.7.54's with its line numbers stripped, bar those two and the fuel the commute's statement reads. In the editions from 0.7.23 to 0.7.54 eight statements went in, two changed shape and one moved, at set places, and the order of the rest held. Six in 0.7.42, the anchor: the month's money constants are struck at its very top, at the expected price level the last month ended on (expectations.strikeLevel(), restrikeMoneyConstants()); the currency is handed its anchored drift before it reprices (foreign.setExpectedDrift()); and straight after the price index the month reads the target, strikes what people expect (expectations.takeMonth()) and hands it on (handOnExpectations()). The wage step became a choice of two in the same version, half on expected inflation once the basket is based, and the index's call took its five parts in 0.7.43. Two in 0.7.49, at 6d: the road is told who has a car of their own and what a journey's fuel costs before the fare (setCommute()), and the drivers' fuel is struck after it (motoring.setFuelBill()). Inside statements that were already there, 0.7.43 has the food vouchers paid after the markets clear, 0.7.44 the strike settle the grocers' supplier credit, 0.7.46 Construction keep the materials its month drew as its books close (Sector.beforeBank()), 0.7.49 the treasury pay transit's bill beside the police's, 0.7.52 the anchor measure the lean against the Standard rule's advice at any strictness, and 0.7.54 the investors search for an order's size, its trim and a landlord's mortgage where they counted them a building at a time. One moved in 0.7.52: the twelfth month's autosave, from the top of the month, straight after the calendar turned, to its very end, after recordMonth(). 0.7.24 to 0.7.41, 0.7.45, 0.7.47, 0.7.48, 0.7.50, 0.7.51, 0.7.53 and 0.7.54 moved no statement: the generated list is the same with its line numbers stripped, bar two of 0.7.53's statements that now hold their counts as long[]. The edition before this one had the builders strike their crews before the posts are counted and migration handed the doors standing (0.7.17), each owner settle the escalation after the work and a business's tax back reach its till (0.7.19), and the demolitions close as the calendar turns, the overtime be read beside the site output and the hand on the queue be settled (0.7.22); the ones before it, the rollover and the fund's December share above the old first step (0.7.13, 0.7.14), the central bank's maturing par after the treasury settles (0.7.15), the bank's capital rule and prices at the top of the month and its provision and second look at the bottom (0.7.7, 0.7.8, 0.7.11, 0.7.12), and the resolution and the preferred after the shares trade (0.7.14).

Standing rule

A flow cannot be reconstructed from the state a month ended in. That is why the save carries income statements, the VAT ledger, the inventory baseline in units, wage history, loss streaks, plots consumed, the unburied backlog, last month's household shapes, the ring of the long sick, six months of prisoners, the railway's quote and what it carried, every sector's fleet and the memory of the commute, the treasury's journal for the month that ended and the one in progress, what the vault sold defending the currency, the bank's running costs and funding blend as it last priced them and the profit it booked after its close, a year of its month statements and of its balance sheets, its allowance and its worst year, every borrower's last quarter, the ledger of the surplus the rollover has netted, the four inputs the debt market's rate was struck from, the crews the builders kept on and the fill they were struck on, each order's contract on its site with the material its quote allowed for, the player's hand on the queue — the city's order of its own sites, the rushes and their months on overtime, the cancels waiting, the stopped shells, the demolitions and the buy-outs, with the overtime wages the next statement charges — the player's own decisions, migration's month and the dead by cause (0.7.27), what the railway was allowed to bill and what went abroad (0.7.29), the fund's ledger and the hand's orders (0.7.39), what people expect and how far they trust the bank (0.7.42), each household's last sale of groceries and the vouchers on it (0.7.43), what the grocers owe their suppliers (0.7.44), the month's trade in units, the balance of payments' month, care treated by kind, each cell's income after its fixed bills and Construction's struck materials (0.7.46), the fund's withdrawal and what it is selling to pay (0.7.48), and transit's bill, the share of commuters with no car and a journey's fuel, and each row's fuel (0.7.49), the world's seed (0.7.56), the city's land on it — its centre, its forty lanes' frontiers, every purchase in the order it was made, the forty offers standing and what has been taken out of its ground (0.7.57) — the water rights its plants pumped before there was a limit (0.7.59), the drivers' fuel as it was drawn (0.7.62), and the graduates waiting for the census to carry their loans and the ratios each sector's month was run at (0.7.63) — none of them derivable by looking at the city.

02

People

The age pyramid is the population. It used to be a display beside a formula; now nothing else decides how many people live here — and since the long sick and the crime batch, nothing dies of only one thing.

Six ages, three ways to die

The band's own annual mortality. Monthly rates are compounded, not divided by twelve.
BandAgesAnnual mortalityNote
Babies0–50.10%almost all of it in the first year
Children6–120.0075%the safest band there is
Teens13–170.02%accidents
Adults18–690.225%averaged over a fifty-two year span — and the entire workforce
Seniors70–842.90%population-weighted from a life table
Elders85–11912.77%plus ageing out at 120

The seniors were one band of fifty years until save format 27, at a flat 4.5%. Senior care asks for a place for everybody over seventy, and in the real world about one in nine of them is in one — 2% of seventy-four-year-olds against 29.6% of the over-85s — and one band cannot hold a fifteenfold gradient, nor a mortality curve that runs from 1.4% a year at seventy to over 30% at ninety-five. The two figures are population-weighted from Canada's 2019 life table, and the split is very nearly aggregate-preserving: total deaths among the over-seventies went from 6.5% a year to about 6.7%, and a seventy-year-old has 14.9 years left against the 15.4 the single band gave. The over-85s keep two household shapes of their own, because Elder living alone is the cell in trouble first when senior care is short.

Children, teens and adults were halved when illness started killing people, so that the new cause of death would be a redistribution rather than a surcharge. A month's deaths are now struck from three rates against the same opening balance — the band's own rate (scaled by care, and by who has no home), illness from the ring of the long sick, and violence from the month's homicides — capped together at 5% a month, with the cap scaling each part alike so the shares survive it. Every one of them is recorded by band, so City History can say what the city has died of since it was founded. Since 0.7.27 the month's dead are summed by cause as well — of age, of illness, killed, and aged out at 120 (PopulationCohorts.getLastDeathsOfAge(), getLastKilled(), getLastAgedOut()) — and saved, so the People page's month reads the same after a load.

Bands drain exponentially rather than on a fixed schedule, so a baby boom smears into a bulge rather than travelling through the city as a wave. Nobody is tracked individually; the bucket only has a mean.

Migration

The city moves toward a target rather than snapping to one. Housing and jobs say how attractive the city is, not how many people are in it.

target = (0.75 × jobs × residentsPerJob + 0.25 × housingCapacity) × seniorCarePull × rentPull × crimePull residentsPerJob = (1 + 0.125) / adultShare // 12.5% labour slack arrivals = min(room, max(0, target − population) × 0.15 × crowding) // room: what the placement has left, since 0.7.17 departures = population × decliningPressure × 0.05

Gross flows, not net, so the screen can say why the number moved. Since 0.7.27 the month is kept whole and saved after the wage history: the draw in its two halves, work and homes (Migration.getLastJobDraw(), getLastHomeDraw()), the pulls, the flows, the leavers by cause (getLastWorkDepartures() beside the broke and crime), both skill mixes and the licences; a save from before reads Not recorded yet until its first month, where a reloaded page had read a city this good draws 0. Crowding interpolates to zero between comfortable households and the point where every flatshare and doubled-up home has already formed — a full city still takes arrivals, just fewer. Since 0.7.17 it reads the households that have a door of their own, so a doubled-up household counts as crowding, Jerus's rule: his year-149 city had doors enough by the count, read 0.63, and had 143,443 households living in somebody else's home because the doors were the wrong size. And a month's arrivals are capped at the room the placement has left (FamilyModel.roomLeft()): the free doors by size and what the doubling valve still has, in households of the arrivals' own mix. It replaced a wall that stopped every arrival while a single household was left with nowhere, and then let a month of 25,000 to 32,000 in at once; in his city, run on for 120 months, arrivals came in 115 months where they had come in 18 (0.7.17). Nobody leaves unless a pay tier's real pay has been declining twelve months straight or has stopped entirely. Four more pushes: a band whose wage is pinned at the floor and still has more workers than posts sheds 2% of the surplus a month — the floor in today's money since 0.7.47 (LabourMarket.isPinned() reads cashMinimumWage()), where it had been compared with the floor in founding money, so that once prices rose no band at the indexed floor ever read as pinned; over the default run that took departures from 65,805 to 1,090,944 and arrivals from 885,622 to 1,694,356 — bankrupt households leave, rent above the target burden deters arrivals, and crime deters arrivals — calibrated off Cullen & Levitt (1999), a tenth of the city's draw gone at twice Canada's crime rate, almost all of it as people not coming rather than people leaving. Arrivals carry a skill mix bought by the wage: at the going rate every arrival has a high-school diploma and nothing more, and every band above it, and since 0.7.18 the band below it, comes only for a premium over the going rate, out of a world that has few to spare. At every band's ceiling 7.5% of a month's arrivals have no diploma, the 2021 Census share of immigrants who landed in 2016–2021 aged 25–54 with no certificate (Ontario's figure, as the Financial Accountability Office of Ontario tabulated it; WageBand.NONE's ceiling, 0.1192). Jerus chose "Some unskilled migrants": "Allow arrivals without a diploma when the unskilled wage is high." In his year-149 city, run on for 120 months, 13,168 came (0.7.18).

Households, pay, and what they remember

Fifteen household shapes, from SENIOR_ALONE and the over-85s' ELDER_ALONE through COUPLE_TWO_CHILDREN to SHARED_ADULTS. Families are built from the adults who work; everyone else has their own books. The mix follows the age pyramid and the job tiers; when homes run short, singles form flatshares first and families double up last.

The households remember. The builder keeps last month's households as far as the people still exist for them — 1% of them re-form each month by design — shrinking each shape by the worst shortage among the bands it needs, rebuilding only the remainder, and refitting tiers to the jobs by iterative proportional fitting. Before it, a city redrew every family from scratch every month and wallets moved with the redraw. Eight seeds: orphans at the end 490–633 → 11–19, hunger 4% → 0%, unemployment 14.8% → 12.2%. Everything a cell holds moves with the people when the shapes re-form — savings, debt, shares, the dollars abroad, the student loan, and since 16 September the cars — and the move is clamped so that a pool cannot hand out more than it holds, because the day it could, four retired cells owed minus 2.7e-29 dollars and the sign of that decided the exchange rate (see how it's checked).

Homes have sizes. A studio takes two, a low-rise flat four, a house six; a household has to fit, largest families into the smallest unit that fits, and a one- or two-person flat refuses a dependant outright. Rent is two markets — see prices. An empty home now costs its owner something to hold, and so does a full one: every building pays 1% of its cost a year to stand.

Six pay tiers, eleven job types — base wages on NS Job Bank medians
TierMonthly
Unskilled$3,460

Seventy-eight ledgers

Eleven working shapes × six pay tiers, four retired rows, three kinds of out-of-work, students, orphans by age band, and the prisoners. Each keeps savings, debt, a lockout clock, shares in each of the sixteen listed companies, dollars held abroad, student debt, its cars, the month's investment income, the dinners it ate out, the share of its people who could pay for care, the city's own paper, since 0.7.12 the businesses' bonds, since 0.7.43 its last sale's baskets — got, asked for and needed — and the vouchers on them, since 0.7.45 the investment income the vouchers' means test smooths over a year, and since 0.7.46 the month's income after its fixed bills — thirty-eight slots a cell in the save (thirty-two at 0.7.12), each keyed by name so a new row never shifts an old one, and each new slot appended to the end so that an older save reads as a city that had none of it, which it did not. Since 0.7.12 each cell also keeps its bonds one by one, and trades its shares and bonds with the other cells as a participant of its own, saved by the cell's name.

Since 0.7.2 a cell whose census count falls under half a household (HouseholdBalance.EMPTY_CELL) is empty: it keeps its count, holds nothing and is paid nothing, and every stock it held folds to its own row first and then to the city — because a cell of 1e-15 households was still being paid, and one line divided a credit by it.

The household books, and a balance sheet

Every cell, opened, shows wages, the banded wage tax, pension contributions and pensions, the health premium, rent, tuition, interest, shopping, healthcare fees, the transit fares, since 0.7.49 the fuel, the bank's account fee, and what is left — plus savings, debt, shares, the city's paper, the businesses' bonds and cars per household, and a waterfall: payslip, rent, fees, food; short, and they spend savings, then sell the city's paper back to the bank's desk, then bring their paper home from abroad, then sell their bonds, then offer their shares on the book at the price at which the dividend they give up yields what borrowing would cost them, then borrow from the bank — since 0.7.7 at a household loan's four costs plus a spread that climbs with what they already owe, never past 36%, and since 0.7.8 only as far as the bank's capital lets it lend — and only then eat less, which makes them ill. A row six months of income in debt that still cannot cover subsistence goes bankrupt: 4% a month discharged, locked out of credit for a year, a quarter of them leaving the city. A row that can be evicted and still cannot pay loses the share of its homes the shortfall covers.

Household money is a page of its own since 0.7.27: each tier's live wage at its head (HouseholdAccounts.wagePerEarner()), the books beside the grid on a wide screen, a verdict that counts every row (it had said every household covered its month over five red rows), the rows outside the families measured against a basket (Household.subsistence(), not a saver's plan: the students had read −$29,448 in Jerus's city), the retired on their own statement, and INCOME PER RESIDENT where PER WORKER had divided the population by the workforce. Until 0.7.27 an opened cell printed the fares under Healthcare and school fees and the account fee under Interest on what they owe; each is a line of its own, and since 0.7.49 an opened cell's fares and fuel are its own row's.

Spending follows income, and since 17 September it follows wealth too. A household plans to spend 80% of what is left above subsistence, which until 0.7.43 it took to the grocer's, so shopping was a budget rather than a headcount; on top of that it spends 0.33% of its net worth a month — four percent a year, the middle of the measured propensity to consume out of wealth — and puts half of the income it does not spend on food over a luxury counter and a quarter of it on a table. That plan still decides what the counter and the table spend out of, and what is left of the month is saved. Dividends and the coupon on its paper abroad are paid home and enter the plan, and so, since 0.7.1, are the coupons on the city's own paper, paid into savings as untaxed investment income, and since 0.7.12 those on the businesses' bonds. A household with no debt, no lockout and more than three months of disposable income in the bank puts 30% of the excess into shares, and sends the rest of its idle savings abroad on the same rule the sectors use. It also takes the city's paper at issue: at the settle the households go first, taking a fifth of an issue for every point it yields over the deposit rate (HOUSEHOLD_PAPER_APPETITE) and never more than half (MAX_HOUSEHOLD_PAPER_SHARE), out of savings past the cushion, and the bank buys the rest. Pensions are pay-as-you-go, and both the contribution rate and the replacement rate are Policy dials. Contributions follow workers and pensions follow pensioners, so an ageing city buys a structural deficit without changing a single policy.

And since 0.7.43 it asks for its groceries at a price. The grocer used to be handed the plan in money, the wealth term in it, so a city asked for fourteen to seventeen times the baskets it could eat and the shelf's price answered a head count. A household asks for what it eats now: a basket a head at most, all of it up to the satiation price, 1.5× the shelf's floor (Retail.SATIATION_MULTIPLE), a little less above it at an elasticity of 0.4 (GROCERY_ELASTICITY, the middle of the measured food-at-home range), and never more than its money buys — everything its plan can fund, and the city's voucher on top (HouseholdBalance.groceryDemandOf()). The city's demand falls as the price rises, so there is one price at which it meets what the shops can hand over, and a shortage is shared out at that price, so the poorest are priced out first (goods and markets).

And since 0.7.3 saving answers the real rate. Everything a household plans above a basket a head — the propensity's share, the wealth term, and the surplus the counter and the table spend out of — is multiplied by one factor a month struck on the real deposit rate, the bank's deposit rate less the inflation the city expects (since 0.7.42; the year's inflation until then): ten points of real return cut it by a tenth, ten points of negative real return raise it by a tenth, and it is held between a half and one and a half. Subsistence never moves, and at a factor of one the plan is the old plan to the bit. On the default seed on 22 September savers ended the run earning 3.63% real and the households planned 0.964 of what they would spend above a basket at zero; over 4,005 months the factor ran from 0.835 to 1.131, median 0.993. On 28 September, with a deposit rate the bank now chooses (see the bank), savers ended the run earning 0.23% real and the households planned 0.998; over 4,002 months the factor ran from 0.921 to 1.060, median 1.000. On 1 October, at 0.7.23, they ended at 0.22% real and planned 0.998 again, and over 4,005 months the factor ran from 0.853 to 1.105, median 1.000; on 5 October, at 0.7.49, they ended at −1.99% real and planned 1.020, and the factor ran from 0.988 to 1.076, median 1.020. Until 0.7.43 what it did to prices was almost nothing, because no price in the index read what anybody would spend; the shelf clears on it now, and what that does is in the central bank.

Why the households were rich

Household net worth ran at 45 months of the city's GDP at month 480 — dead on the real world's sixty to eighty-four — and 5,072 by month 3,840. It did not sit at a wrong level; it diverged. Over one run the foreign coupons came to $2,649bn against $584bn of every wage the city ever paid, because the households kept about 79% of their wealth abroad at the world's 2% and the coupon was rolled where it was earned, so wealth compounded at 1.58% a year mechanically, 193× over 333 years. Three changes were ensembled one at a time: paying the coupon home took the ratio from 5,380 months to 3,412; letting investment income into the plan put it back to 5,465, because investment income is a flow and a bigger city pays more of it; the wealth term took it to 2,737 and gave the best city of the four. A stock that compounds can only be bounded by a drain that reads the stock. And with the coefficient pushed to nine times the empirical range the stock still rose, while delivery fell from 76% of plans to 57% — the households are not hoarding because they are misers; they are hoarding because there is nothing to buy. The cars, the boutiques and the kitchens are the answer to that, and net worth now oscillates (432 → 137 → 128 → 214 months of GDP) instead of climbing.

What a household owns, and borrows for

A car is a household stock, beside savings, debt, shares and the dollars abroad — it follows the people, it is saved per cell by name, a household that leaves takes it — and it is the first one that is not money, so a currency reform does not touch it. It is the first durable a household in this game has ever been able to own: the measurement that decided the shape found an unskilled couple banking $3,160 of a $4,880 month and sitting on $799,100, a hundred and sixty-four months of income, with $17.6bn of household savings in the city against zero household debt. That is not a rich city, it is a city with nothing to buy. So affordability barely binds in a mature city and binds hard in a young one, and the constraint is time: 2% of the households that have never owned one buy a car in a month, a car lasts 180 months and is replaced at once when it dies, one per household is saturation, and a cell buys whole cars — because a money ratio that comes out as a physical quantity has to cross at a grain coarser than the dust, or a reformed city wants an ulp more of a car and the two cities' trade balances part company. A city whose transit could carry everybody tops out at half a car per household, for ever, where its pass is cheap against a car; a metro built after the cars does not remove them, it stops them being replaced, and the fleet decays to the new ceiling over the life of a car.

And since 0.7.49 a car costs something to run. A commute by car burns $2.00 of fuel a journey in the world's money (Motoring.CAR_FUEL_PER_JOURNEY: fifteen kilometres at eight litres a hundred and about $1.65 a litre), forty journeys a month, priced at the exchange rate and imported, like the railway's, and paid by the rows that drive; since 0.7.62 it is 1.2 litres of FUEL a journey, drawn off the refiners' tanks at the market's price before the rest is imported (Motoring.drawFuel()), so with no refinery it is the same $2.00 at the world's price level, and a refinery's fuel stays at home (the private sector). Its wear is still the calendar's 180 months and its insurance is sunk. Until then nothing in the household model cost anything to run. And a household without a car weighs a month's pass against a car's full monthly cost — its payment over its life at the household rate, and a month of fuel — before it buys, and the share who would rather ride — everybody at a pass of half a car's cost or less, nobody at twice it (InfrastructureManager.transitChosen()) — scales how far good transit lowers the ceiling. Jerus: "acquiring car costs 500 a month and transit is 250 then they take transit". A household holds one car, so most commuters in a big city have none of their own — 73% in the 2,400-month research city — and ride where a line reaches them or walk (transport).

And they borrow for it. Jerus: "make it so that citizens the car requirements are lowered and instead they finance with the bank." The rule is pay what you have and borrow the difference: a household hands over every dollar it has spare and a lender finds the rest, with a fifth of the price (CAR_DEPOSIT) as a minimum in cash and half the household's remaining credit room as the most a lender advances — that second number found by DenominationCheck, because every financed household was parked within a whisker of the bankruptcy line. With no credit line the rule reduces exactly to the old cash rule, so it can never refuse a purchase the old one allowed. Since 0.7.7 the bank's one per cent fee on new lending is added to the loan, because a household borrows when it is short of cash. The first draft — a fifth down, four fifths borrowed, compulsory — cut ownership at month 266 from 42% to 11%, because these households are cash-rich and income-poor and the loan moved the constraint onto the thing they have not. Seven of eight seeds are bit-identical over four thousand months; the young city diverges in month eight and has bought twice the cars by month ten. The loan is a bridge, not a note: the grocery waterfall repays it out of spare cash within a month or two. Household debt in this game had been exactly zero in every measured city. It is not any more.

The car goes last. Jerus: "if they are doing bad they cut back expenses, sell their cars or go for cheaper groceries." Two of the three were already in — the plan shrinks by itself, and a poorer cell already buys more grain and less meat — and the car was in nothing: a family sold its shares, brought its paper home, drew its last dollar of credit and went hungry with five months of take-home parked in the drive. There is a second-hand market at home now, and the fleet does not shrink when a household sells, it changes hands. The price is struck the way every market here strikes one: a floor at 15% of new (what a scrapper pays), a ceiling at 70%, a position between them from demand against supply — so a city cannot sell its way out of a general crash: everybody offers, nobody buys, the price is scrap, and the harness asserts it (8,000 offered, 0 sold, pinned at the floor). A household offers when the wage after the fixed bills no longer buys the food and half a year of that gap beats everything it has saved and can still borrow; a one-month test found households going bankrupt with the car still in the drive thirteen times for every one that sold it. Over 4,002 months of the default run when it shipped the trigger fired in two cell-months, both fractions of a household, and over 4,005 on 22 September in none, because the households were too rich to ever sell anything — which is the finding above, and the reason the counter and the kitchens exist. On 28 September the same seed offered 8,121 cars over 4,002 months and 362 of them found a buyer, at between 15% and 59% of a new car; on 1 October it offered 4,059 over 4,005 months and 149 found one, at between 15% and 32%; on 5 October, at 0.7.49, it offered 2,887 and 95 found one, at between 15% and 69%.

03

Outside the families

Until this system the city's money was spread across every adult in a pay tier, working or not, so an unskilled single adult took home $1,262 in a month of 40% unemployment and nobody was ever poor. Four groups now keep their own books beside the families.

labour force = workforce − students − prisoners the pool = labour force − filled posts // an identity, not a second count
RowLives onWhat is true of them
On EI55% of the wage they lost, cappeda claim runs twelve months; the benefit is split among claimants out of a real premium
EI run outsavings, then creditthe only row that can be evicted; four in five of the pool end up here
Lost their homenothinghungry, and 3.7× as likely to be ill and to die
Studentssavings, a grant, and a loan, interest-free unless the player sets a rateout of the labour supply until they graduate; graduates repay over 114 months
Orphansnothing, and they pay no feesone row per age band; no grown-up, so no credit
Prisonersnothing; debts frozenout of the labour force, out of the housing match, out of every offender group

Employment insurance is a real programme. A 1.63% premium off insurable wages funds a 55% benefit capped at Canada's maximum insurable earnings scaled to the city's unskilled median — $1,903 a month for an unskilled claimant, $3,158 for anyone at or above the cap. Both rates are Policy dials. The ring is struck on the inflow of the newly unemployed, cohort by cohort: jobs lost this month join at the wage they lost, each cohort is thinned monthly by hires, deaths and departures, and the twelfth cohort falls off the end into "EI run out". Arrivals who never found work enter at the unskilled wage and get no claim. Since 0.7.17 the builders' idle crews join it too: a builder with less work than crews lays off the posts the work does not need, down to a core crew of a quarter of them, and those it lays off draw EI (the builders). Since 0.7.3 the benefit is paid in the month it is credited: struck again at the top of the month on the pool the month opens with and paid there (Game.payEiBenefits()), so the treasury, the ledger and the audit read one figure. For as long as EI had existed the out of work were credited last month's bill while the treasury paid this month's, and nothing saw it because the households are outside the audit's pools; OutsideCheck closes every Construction Depot in a small city and asserts that the closing month pays the EI of the pool it opened with and the month after pays the bill the closing struck, each credited the month it is paid; three of its lines fail on the old order. A save from 0.7.2 pays one month's EI twice on its first month, once.

Eviction is a level, not a rate. A row that can be evicted and is short loses households × min(1, shortfall / (rent + fees + interest)) of its homes; a quarter of those leave the city and the rest are on the street. Because a cell is an average, its households run out together — evictions arrive as a cliff. For most of the project's life nobody was ever evicted in the ensemble: an out-of-work adult ended a 333-year run holding millions, because the unskilled tier's savings are what they draw on first. It moves with the run. On the default seed on 18 September, with the wealth term and the counters in, 550 households were evicted over 4,002 months and 137 of them left; on 22 September, at 0.7.3, nobody was evicted over 4,005 months, nobody was left on EI at the end, and an out-of-work adult whose claim had run out ended the run with $303,200 put by; on 28 September, at 0.7.15, one household was evicted over 4,002 months and did not leave, 1,378 people were on EI at the end, and an out-of-work adult whose claim had run out ended with $80,600; on 1 October, at 0.7.23, one was evicted over 4,005 months and did not leave, 4,746 people were on EI at the end, and an adult past EI ended with $235,700; and on 5 October, at 0.7.49, nobody was evicted over 4,005 months, 7,135 people were on EI at the end, and an adult past EI ended with $340.1M put by, in a city whose prices had risen 1,213-fold since its founding. Close the biggest employer in a young city and the path works exactly as designed: 549 evicted in the first year, 440 of them in two months, 384 on the street at the worst, 127 still there four years later (measured on 11 September; no harness reproduces it since).

Housing serves each group with its own valve: an out-of-work adult alone counts a full door, a flatsharer a fifth, a doubled-up seeker or someone on the street none — so the rent bill is split over the doors actually paid for. The transit fare is a third fee beside the health fee and the tuition: from the day transit was built until 16 September it was credited to the city and debited to nobody, and nobody saw it because no fixture had ever built a bus. Until 0.7.49 it was split across the rows the way the clinic's fees are, so the retired, the out of work and the students paid for buses they never rode. Since then the road counts its commuters by row — the working rows' grown-ups, and the cars they hold — and the fares fall on the rows that ride and the drivers' fuel, a fourth fee, on the rows that drive, each followed by people within the row (HouseholdAccounts.setCommute()); the rows outside the families pay neither — see transport.

04

Health

Three classes. Health is the effect — a multiplier on output. Healthcare is the service — an employer with a payroll and a cemetery with a finite number of plots. Sickness is the ring of people who have not got better, and it is the one that kills.

Sickness is the fourth utilisation ratio

It sits beside energy, water and roads — and, since 17 September, beside the fifth ratio, the sector's own fleet of vans — multiplies with them, and is carried across a save the same way. The one difference is the whole point:

output = capacity × fill × bEnergy × bWater × bRoad × bHealth × bVans payroll = Σ wage[job type] × posts filled[job type] // no bHealth, no ratio at all; per job type since 0.7.17

Output falls, payroll does not. The employer carries it — a mill with fifty on the books pays fifty wages in a bad flu month and sells four weeks of output in three. The workforce, the pyramid and the wage bill never move. A sick worker still gets paid, still shops, still pays rent. Since 0.7.17 every employer pays each job type's wage on the posts of that type actually filled, and the households receive exactly that: it had been the whole schedule times the sector's average fill, which charged an empty post at the average of the posts beside it, and in Jerus's year-149 city the sectors paid $1,273M a month that no household received, the builders $269M of it. Jerus: "but sick still get paid right? if yes then good." They are: a sick worker holds a filled post.

General-care coverage sets the standing absence rate
CoverageOff sick
None18.0%
Half10.5%
Everybody3.0%

Five things add on top of that baseline and the total is capped at 45%: an outbreak, the unburied (up to 15 points), hunger (up to 8), the share of the city with no home at 3.7 times the baseline, and the injured — half a month off work for every violent crime.

Outbreaks land on top: roughly one every five years, peaking 8–25%, decaying 55% a month, with hospitals taking 60% off the peak but never preventing one. The roll is a pure function of the month number, so an epidemic survives a reload without the generator's state going in the save — you cannot save-scum out of one. Measured over 4,005 months on the default seed, 22 September: 52 outbreaks, the city ill for 289 months, worst month 45% — which is the cap; and the run ends at 15.1% with no outbreak in it at all, nobody unburied and two points of hunger on a baseline of 12.8% that general care covered 35% of. On 18 September the same seed ended at 36.8%, fifteen points of it the unburied; the batches since moved every seed, so the two readings are two cities, not a trend. On 28 September it had 52 outbreaks again, ill for 301 months, worst month 45%, and ended at 19.2%: no outbreak, nobody unburied, five points of hunger on a baseline of 14.1% that general care covered 26% of. On 1 October, at 0.7.23: 52 outbreaks, ill for 324 months, worst month 45%, ending at 20.7% — no outbreak, nobody unburied, 4.2 points of hunger on a baseline of 16.4% that general care covered 10% of. On 5 October, at 0.7.49: 52 outbreaks, ill for 298 months, worst month 45%, ending at 39.7% — no outbreak, fifteen points of the unburied, eight of hunger, on a baseline of 16.5% that general care covered 10% of.

The history keeps the outbreak since 0.7.46 (Health.getOutbreakSeverity()), and City History names an epidemic only while one runs — at least 0.5% of the workforce off sick over what its care explains (YearBook.EPIDEMIC_OUTBREAK, Health.OUTBREAK_FLOOR), three months or more. It had named one on the sick rate, so a city short of care was one epidemic for life: 2,213 months of the 2,400-month research city's.

The people who stay sick

Each band carries a thirteen-month ring of who fell ill when. Past two months sick, a share of them dies each month — 4% for babies and seniors, 8% for the over-85s, 2% for adults, 1% for children and teens. General care is what gets people out of the ring: it cures 50% of the sick a month untreated and 90% fully served. Babies and the retired fall ill twice as often as the city rate until their own care takes the excess away.

What general care stopped doing

It used to be a ×3 swing on the death rate of every teen and adult. That is gone: general care now acts only through the sick rate and through curing the sick. A fully served adult used to die at 0.15% a year; they now die at about 0.232%, base plus a sliver of illness. The halving of the base rate does not make that back at the good end — deliberately, and it is a live lever.

What care does to a life

A multiplier on the band's annual death rate, interpolated geometrically on coverage — unserved is multiplied by the swing, fully served is divided by it, half-served is exactly the base rate. Log space is the right space for a multiplier: halfway between "forty times worse" and "forty times better" is the same.

Annual mortality all-in: the band's rate scaled by care, plus steady-state illness — struck before the seniors were split at 85, and kept as it was struck; the two bands now start from 2.90% and 12.77%, with swings of 1.35× and 1.80×
BandCare typeSwingNo careHalfFull
BabiesChildcare40×7.96%0.77%0.017%
ChildrenChildcare40×0.83%0.120%0.004%
TeensGeneral—0.56%0.133%0.024%
AdultsGeneral—1.28%0.449%0.232%
SeniorsSenior1.35×9.96%5.14%3.35%

Teens and adults have no swing left; the whole of their column is illness. Senior care's swing is 1.35× on the 70-to-85 band and 1.80× on the over-85s, because that is where the care is worth more. Childcare also doubles the birth rate at full coverage — 15 per thousand a year to 30 — and senior care makes the city 30% more attractive to migrants, which is what turns an ageing pyramid into something to manage rather than endure.

Measured on the default seed on 22 September: illness is 32.7% of all deaths — 147,312 of 450,113 over 4,005 months, against 42.7% on 18 September; on 28 September it was 39.9%, 263,361 of 660,761 over 4,002; on 1 October 58.0%, 448,687 of 773,490 over 4,005; and on 5 October, at 0.7.49, 55.2%, 485,059 of 877,961. Two editions ago the same figure read 1.4%, 655 of 46,134, and that difference is not the model: until 17 September the hunger measure was reporting zero in a city that was starving, and the sick rate it feeds was being fed a zero (see what a household eats); and the playtest's default player leaves the city alone for fifty-four months at a stretch, so every figure it reports is a city in crisis from neglect. A player who tops up power, water, streets, clinics and stores once a year runs the same seeds at 7.8% sick against 23.1%. Babies take the worst of it: twice as often ill, 4% a month past two months, on top of childcare's ×40.

The two ways to bury somebody

A cemetery is a stock: plots are consumed permanently and the land never comes back. A crematorium is a rate: almost no land, a great deal of electricity, breaking even only when busy. Households whose monthly surplus would cover a plot over ten years choose burial; the rest choose cremation; overflow runs both ways. A city with neither leaves people where they fell, and the unburied make the living ill — up to 15 points on the sick rate, cleared the month a cemetery opens.

Somebody pays for all of it

Healthcare carries 2,128 jobs across its fourteen templates, more than any other category the city pays for — only the railway's three offer more. The treasury pays the staffed payroll plus maintenance, less what patients and funerals bring back — measured at about 20% cost recovery when the ring was added, 25% on the default seed on 22 September, $5.04M a month, with 246 buried and none cremated in the last month of the run, 30% on 28 September, $5.76M a month, 396 buried and none cremated, 33% on 1 October, $4.28M a month, 400 buried and none cremated, and 12% on 5 October, at 0.7.49, $1.83bn a month in a city whose prices had risen 1,213-fold, with 13,865 lying unburied: a net-deficit business at the founding fees, as designed, and since 0.6.8 the player's to make something else. Fees are charged on people served, not beds built, so an empty ward is paid for and collects nothing — and since 0.6.8 served means treated. Since 0.7.41 every care gauge says served, higher being better: Build's rings and NEEDS YOU's rows the staffed places over the people who need them, unclamped (CityNeeds.careServed()), and the Services cards the month's coverage, held at 100% and cut for the priced out — two figures that can sit far apart in the month after a load (the screen).

Same 600-house city, 360 months, four programmes — measured before the base rates were halved and the sick ring added, and kept because the shape is what it is for
ProgrammePopulationOff sickBirths/moGDP/mo
Nothing1,23233.0%1.5$693k

Everything4,4613.0%7.9$3,305k

The city is founded with a doctor, a nursery, an almshouse, a churchyard and a constabulary — enough for about 1,200 people, sized off the pyramid's own equilibrium shares, and not a building among them. The churchyard's graves last four centuries for a village and six years for a city of eighty thousand.

The price at the door

Jerus: "healthcare should be an adjustable price, all the way to even make it a profitable business or the option to make it an obligatory insurance payment system." Two dials since 0.6.8. TaxPolicy.healthFeeScale multiplies the three care fees — general, childcare and senior; funerals are deliberately unscaled — from nothing to 15× the founding ones, and healthPremiumRate takes up to 10% of every wage, employee side, on the EI premium's own base, into the treasury with nothing balancing it. Between them they make the corner the Promises → Health page names on a chip since 0.7.36, its sentence behind the chip's (i): free at the point of use, paid from taxes; insurance, no fee and a premium; a business, fees past the city's own break-even, which the page strikes live (Healthcare.breakEvenScale(), the gross cost over what the fees would raise at 1×) and marks on the fee's dial; a mix, a fee and a premium; or fee-funded, in part, a fee and the treasury for the rest. Under the chip WHO PAYS FOR CARE draws the month's cost as one bar in its four payers — treatment fees, funeral fees, the premium and the treasury — and the treasury's share is previewed at full service on both sides of a change; a service that cost nothing says so, where the page had read Fees cover 0%. The founding fees themselves are struck at the price level people expect since 0.7.42, like every money constant (the month). The ceiling went from 5 to 15 after the first measurement, because a played city breaks even at ×7 to ×13 — the founding fees were set against founding wages, and the labour market has lifted a clinic's payroll since. The default seed on 22 September breaks even at ×9.85, on 28 September at ×10.73, on 1 October at ×18.84, and on 5 October, at 0.7.49, at ×8.43.

A household that cannot pay goes without care, not without food — Jerus's rule, and what makes the fee a decision. The share of a household's bill that fits in what its plan says it will have after rent, its other bills and a basket for everybody in it — counting income, savings, the paper abroad and the credit still open, the waterfall's own order — is the share of its people the clinics treat, struck against the bill at full service so that a household that skipped last month does not read an empty bill as affordable and swing between served and starving. The rest go untreated in the sick rate, the swings and the births. A household that can cover its bills is served exactly as before, multiplied by exactly 1.0 and asserted at zero tolerance, so at the founding fee the cliff catches only households with nothing, the out of work past EI and elders on a pension: 0 to 247 people a month across the eight seeds, and none on the default seed on 22 September, 28 September or 1 October; on 5 October, at 0.7.49, two a month over the run and none in its last month. Fees at ×5 recovered about two thirds of the cost and priced out no more people than ×1, because the households the cliff catches are out at any positive fee and the working poor can still find $50 a head — a fee that prices a family out of childcare is a fee the dial cannot yet set. HealthCheck's sections 12–16 walk it: the scale against the constants, the break-even, one household both ways, a poor city three times, a rich one at zero tolerance, and the premium to the penny. Since 0.7.12 the poor city's hunger is read over its last year rather than its last month, which was Jerus's change, and read that way the line is red: on 28 September the city charging fifteen times the founding fees was 30.2% hungry over its last year against the free city's 21.8%, 8.4 points against a tolerance of five. The gap has grown since: 77 points on 0.7.17's tree and 39 on 0.7.18's, as 0.7.18's implementer ran them (20.9% against 98.1%, then 14.5% against 53.5%), and on 1 October, at 0.7.23, the free city was 11.1% hungry over its last year and the dear one 51.1%, 40.0 points; on 5 October, at 0.7.49, with hunger counted in baskets since 0.7.43, it was 2.2% against 12.3%, 10.1 points, and the line now reads no hungrier than the free one by more than the price of care, the same five points. Which change of 0.7.17 opened it is not traced. Of the dear city's hungry, as 0.7.12 traced them, a quarter were priced out of care, a fifth were billed care their household row had already paid for, and nearly half had money for food after their fixed bills and still planned less, which is untraced. It ships red on Jerus's word, "Ship with it red, flagged", and has a batch of its own on the list.

05

Crime & police

Crime has reasons. Every adult at liberty is sorted once a month, cell by cell, into a graded list of them — because the cells are the only place the reasons meet: the pool knows who is out of work, the housing match knows who has no door and who is crowded, and the household books know who is going short.

The reasons, in order. Where a group overlaps another, the higher weight counts.
ReasonWeight
No home5
Out of work, EI run out4
Short of money3
Out of work, still on EI2
Crowded — doubled up or flatsharing2
No reason at all0.1
plus too few police, per adult2
pressure = Σ adults × weight + 2 × (1 − coverage) × adults crimes = K × pressure × (1 − 0.90 × coverage1.5) coverage = staffed officers / (360 per 100,000)

K is struck, not typed. It is solved at startup so that a city with Canada's police coverage and the game's own equilibrium adult share commits Canada's 5,585 crimes per 100,000 a year. Full coverage is twice Canada's 180 officers per 100,000 and takes 90% off — drastically, and never to nothing, which was the brief.

A quarter of crimes are violent. Each costs its victim half a month off work, which goes onto the sick rate as an injury term, and 0.115% of them kill — calibrated against Canada's 1.61 homicides per 100,000. The rest is theft, worth a quarter of an unskilled monthly wage each, half taken from households and half from the tills, and handed to the offenders in proportion to the weight their own cell carried. Theft leaves a sector's books through a named stolen line, so the money identity can see it.

Arrests scale with coverage: 9% of crimes at full coverage, pro rata below it. Everyone caught serves six months in a ring of monthly cohorts. A prisoner is out of the labour force, out of the housing match, out of every offender group, has their debts frozen and keeps their own ledger row. Caught and not held is a real state: with no cell free, the arrest is recorded and the person walks. The prisons page sets the city's prisoners beside Canada's, 127 per 100,000 (Crime.CANADA_PRISONERS_PER_100K, 0.7.28).

Four buildings, all paid for by the city like the hospitals and the schools
BuildingCapacityJobsAll-in
Police Station120 officers172$45M
Police Headquarters500 officers750$180M
Jail300 cells264$360M
Penitentiary600 cells590$600M

Measured over eight 333-year seeds: crime runs at 1.8–2.3 times Canada's rate in the years before the first station and settles at about a tenth of it once coverage is full, with the residue coming almost entirely from the long-term unemployed and the crowded. Between 2,100 and 2,900 people are caught over a run and every one of them walks, because the smallest prison in the catalogue is 300 cells for $360M and a city of 15,000 never needs or affords one. Seven to ten people are killed over three centuries; $12.8M to $18.6M is stolen. Those were cities of fifteen thousand. The default seed on 22 September ended at 130,978 people with three police stations, coverage at 46%, crime at 1.22× Canada's, 59,189 caught over the run and every one of them still walking, 334 killed and $891M stolen — the advisor built a station three times in four thousand months and a prison never, so the lockup question below had not gone away, it had grown. On 28 September the same seed ended at 184,736 people with a station and a headquarters, coverage at 62% and crime at 1.19× Canada's, and this time the advisor had built jails: four stood at the end with 344 people inside, and 35,279 of the 79,592 caught over the run were still not held; 593 were killed and $1.50bn stolen. On 1 October, at 0.7.23, it ended at 163,391 people with three stations and nothing else, coverage at 43% and crime at 1.71× Canada's; it built no jail, so all 69,581 caught over the run walked, and 423 were killed and $1.00bn stolen. On 5 October, at 0.7.49, it ended at 185,111 people with one station, coverage at 15% and crime at 2.23× Canada's, too few police the largest of its reasons; all 62,638 caught over the run walked, 961 were killed, and $763bn was stolen, at prices 1,213 times the founding's.

06

Work & output

Seventeen private sectors, each keeping its own books, its own cash reserve, its own debt, its own share register and, since 0.7.12, its own bonds — and, since 11 September, each of them one class extending a single template. Ten of the seventeen have been added since, five of them in the three days to 18 September and the last two, oil and its refinery, in 0.7.62, and the whole point of the template is that each cost an afternoon rather than a week.

Why there is a template

Jerus: "we should make a single template, and every single sector just extends it, and adds or overrides, right? cause in the future there will be over a thousand or near a thousand sectors." Before it, six sectors were five handlers in five shapes, named by hand in about a hundred places — eleven loops in the economy manager, a six-way switch in the books, thirteen enumerations in the interface. A seventh would have been a week of finding them. It is now one class, one constant, one line in the registry. Sixty files changed, fifteen new, eight deleted; save format 21 and a clean break.

Everything a sector has is declared once in the template: its identity (a saved key, a label, the build-menu group its buildings sit under), its buildings (every template in buildings.json names its owner, and the sector reads capacity, jobs, land, book value, power, water and road load off its own stacks), its production (what it makes, what it uses, how many months it stocks), its books (one statement, one shape), its credit, listing and money abroad, its planning, and its save. A sector class is now a declaration of about twenty lines. Mining is the shortest, and the shape a thousand of them should have.

SectorMakesUses
Retailgroceries, priced on a floor of what the stock cost and half again, and above it toward the price that clears the shelf (0.7.43)thirteen foods — local first and imports for the shortfall: bread and bakery goods from the ovens, dairy, eggs, meat, vegetables and fruit from the farms, the five processed goods from the plants; grains and fish only ever off a ship
Real estatehousing — one rent cheque per occupied homehouses and apartment blocks from construction
Industrybread and bakery goodscrops from local fields first, imports for the shortfall — it used to make food out of nothing
Heavy industrysteel, exported or sold to the fabricatorslocal ore first, imported scrap for the shortfall
Miningiron orea deposit, which the player must buy as land
Constructionbuilding work, recognised as it is deliveredmaterials
Materialsconstruction materialpower, water, wages
Business servicessupport, back-office and engineering work, by the seat-month, all of it sold abroadpeople, almost entirely — and an engineering office needs licensed engineers before it will open at all
Manufacturingfabricated steel and machinery — export only until 16 September, when the assembly plants became their first customer at homesteel, local or imported — 61% of a fabricator's revenue
Agriculturecrops for the mills; and since 16 September dairy and eggs, meat, vegetables and fruit for the shelf and the meat worksland, heavily; labour and power barely
Food processingprocessed meats, ready meals, snacks, cooking fats, drinks — a third of the shelf by weightmeat, vegetables and grains at the Meat Works, crops at the other two; the first customer the Livestock Farm ever had at home
Railhaulage — the freight that used to leave inside the trade band, billed to the shipper at home as a fraction of the lorry ratetrack, wagon sets it buys from the Locomotive Works, and fuel imported from the world
