Luxury retaila counter: watches sold at a margin struck against the buyers at the price it chargesluxury goods, all of them imported, deliberately
Restaurantsmeals, one ninetieth of a person-month each, at three to eight times what the food costthe same thirteen foods off the same shelf at the same prices — it imports nothing

Materials is the seventh, and the first sector added under the template. The Construction Materials Plant used to feed the city's yard for free; it is a business now, filling a shed rather than planning to two months of demand, and planning on a thirty-six-month view because a building boom is not a month long. A city that builds hard makes materials dear, and a plant near the builders is the first thing in the game that gets cheaper for the buyer and richer for the maker at once.

The eleventh to the fifteenth

Food processing is the eleventh. Jerus: "now a new sector which makes the ready meals, processed meals, snacks, drinks, and cooking fats?" Those five are 16.3 kg of the reference basket's 50, a third of what a household eats by weight, and every gram of them arrived by ship. Three plants, each decided by a different number: the Meat Works buys meat, vegetables and grains and makes processed meats and ready meals, with the input bill at 40% of revenue; the Snack & Oils Plant turns crops into snacks and cooking fats at 19%; the Bottling Plant turns crops into drinks at 4%. It took four calibration passes and three of them were not calibration: the generic planner sizes a sector by one good and skipped every template that did not make it, so two of the three plants were not refused but invisible; a first plant was valued at the import ceiling its own arrival would end, so processed meat stood at $8.90 the month the works was financed and $7.70 the month it opened, and the sector now prices its own plant with its nameplate in the supply; and the sales tax was not in the estimate, which matters here because an import carries no input credit and a city with no herds imports every gram — month 72 of one run read $35k of revenue, $1k of operating income and $5k of tax.

Then the calibration itself, by revenue per worker, which is the ratio that decides whether a maker survives a city growing up: the first pass ran $6,000–$10,700 a head against the two bakeries' $13,500 and $17,800 and a foundry's $33,600, and a Bottling Plant was six people making a tonne a day; the three are $17,100, $16,800 and $18,000 a head now, payroll 21–22% of revenue at mid-band. And one rule no other sector needs: nobody builds a food plant on exports, because the world's band is narrower than the processing step — raw meat lands at $7.00 and processed meat ships at $5.60 — so a plant is financed only when the city already eats what it would make. What the sector is for, in one measurement: at month 98 of a real city the same Meat Works is worth $16,559 a month on meat at a farm's export floor and $1,678 on meat off a ship. The building did not change; the farms did. Eight seeds: median GDP up 11%, $375.8M to $417.9M a month, population unchanged, the sector ending every seed with $52M–$86M of assets and zero write-downs in seven of eight.

Rail is the twelfth and Automotive the thirteenth, and both are told in transport: the railway is the business that wants to be paid the freight every price in this game has always had inside it, and the assembly plant is the first thing in the city that buys a beam or a machine — ore, steel, fabrication, and now something a person drives away in.

Luxury retail is the fourteenth. Jerus: "lets add restuarants as well as luxury stores, these two will absorb some spending as well", and on the pricing, "supply v demand, thats the most important thing." A Boutique and a Department Store import what they sell — the world has no shortage of watches — and what a city can be short of is a counter, so the margin is what gets struck, on the market's own rule with a mark-up in place of a price: 1.25× the landed cost with nobody in the shop, 4.00× with a queue at the door, and the position between them from what is wanted against what the counters can serve. Until 0.7.19 what is wanted was the pieces the households would take at the floor price, which nobody is ever charged: in Jerus's year-149 city that queue held the margin at 2.646 while only the shoppers who would pay 2.646 bought, and the sector earned 2.5 times any other's profit. Since 0.7.19 it is the pieces they would buy at the margin the shop strikes — Jerus chose "The customers actually served": "The markup reads the shoppers who buy at the price actually charged, not a queue counted at the lowest price" — and since a dearer margin loses buyers, the margin is the one fixed point at which the buyers it leaves put it exactly there (LuxuryRetail.strikeMargin(), by bisection on the households' own demand); the boutiques' planner reads the same buyers. In his city it took the margin from 2.662 to 2.263 (0.7.19). Since 0.7.43 the shop charges a sixth of the way there a month, in logs, rather than the fixed point itself (LuxuryRetail.MARGIN_SPEED), because a shop's price is sticky; the kitchens' margin moves the same way (Restaurants.MARGIN_SPEED). Crowded shops widen the margin, a wide margin makes boutiques worth building, and the investor builds them. What it is for is the hoard: household net worth stops diverging and starts oscillating. It is deliberately an import, so a rich city's spending leaves rather than going round again. It is also a fragile business — on one seed it finishes with 22 write-downs and a 252-month borrowing ban — and the sizing of the two buildings against a city's luxury budget has not been tuned. The swing it showed every other month for a while was a price nobody paid, found by 0.7.12: the shop costed its stock at last month's clearing price, which in a month with no seller at home was half the import price, so its shelf turned round in 98% of months; it reads the import price now, and turns round in 2%.

Restaurants are the fifteenth, and the rule Jerus attached is what makes them a different sector rather than a second boutique: a meal out replaces groceries. So a kitchen imports nothing. A dinner is the same thirteen foods off the same shelf at the same prices, which makes the kitchens a second buyer in a market the city is already short of — where "let the basket get dearer, not bigger" actually bites — and a second door to the food there is, since Retail's binding constraint is coverage rather than stock. A meal is one ninetieth of a person-month, three a day for thirty days, and the hunger measure counts a dinner at what a person-month of food costs, not at what the kitchen charged, because three to eight times the food is wages, rent and somebody else's washing up and none of it is nourishment. The margin is 3.0× to 8.0×, wider than the trade's own 2.9–3.6× because in this city labour runs at four times food — three centuries of farms and food plants made food cheap while the wage schedule did not move — and at most a third of anybody's meals are eaten out. The harness caught three faults nothing else would have: the sector had no demand at all, because a share of a rich household's surplus is a share of zero once the wealth term is in the plan; the kitchens could not pay their staff, because a building pays at 100% and serves at the operating rate, which is about half; and a kitchen that stocked for what it served last month was self-limiting for ever. Eight seeds against 0.6.6: median hunger 36% to 22%, sickness 18.1% to 16.6%, the kitchens feeding 11–26% of each city and the shops selling about 5% fewer baskets, which is the crowding-out working. The sector finishes seven seeds of eight with zero write-downs at 2.00% credit, which is the profile of a business that cannot fail, and that is on the list.

Five ratios, and they multiply

RatioShort of it becauseWhat it does
Energygeneration below drawa step: output scales with the share delivered, and the bill scales with it too
Watersupply below drawsame shape; residents draw 0.3 units a head and nobody bills them
Roadsload past 90% of capacitynon-linear, floored at 35% — a gridlocked city still moves, slowly; per sector since the streams were split, because a tram takes one business's staff out of the jam and leaves the one next door's lorries in it
Healththe workforce is ill or hurtcuts output only; the payroll is paid in full
Vansthe sector has not taken delivery of the lorries its plant needsthe only one a business buys: a fleet is built up at an eighth a month, wears out over ten years, and a firm with none is slowed to 60%, not stopped — it hires haulage; since 0.7.12 it orders only the vans its cash and credit can pay for

On top of those sits the job fill rate, worked out per job type: the allocator spends the workforce across the posts the city offers, and a sector's output is discounted by how much of its establishment it could actually staff, and its payroll is each job type's wage on the posts of that type it filled (0.7.17). Since 0.7.18 workers take the best-paid job they qualify for (labour), so where a short city's bottom rung would out-pay the one above it the two fill as one market, and the empty posts move up the ladder rather than sitting on its bottom rung. The posts the city offers are every building's, bar the builders' crews their work does not need, which they lay off (the builders). The four that arrive from outside are the city's to fix; the fifth is the sector's own capital, and is told with the vehicles in transport. Since 0.7.30 a business's Operations page draws the fill and the five as a cascade whose last step is its operating rate (SectorFlow.THROTTLES), and its note, which had said output was cut by whichever of five was thinnest and left the vans out, names six and says they multiply; a sector with nothing standing reads no plant standing, where it had read Staffed 100% beside a rate in red.

Why it is a ratio basis and not a ratio

A month's income statement is written at the start of the month, off last month's utilisation. The simulation then moves it. Save after that and a reloaded game would price the month at ratios it was never traded at — which showed up the day roads arrived, as $0.51 of income appearing out of a reload. Every statement carries the utilisation it was struck against.

Maintenance is an operating cost, and it is demand

Every building in the city pays 1% of its build cost a year to stand — every sector, every city-maintained category, and the bank's branches — in cash, in materials and in construction points, placed as a real order with the builders rather than written off as a line. Since 0.7.19 the cash part is the building's cash cost with its labour at today's builders' wages, and the bill carries the builders' sales tax: a business that makes taxable supplies claims it back as an input, and the landlords, whose rent is an exempt supply, and the bank's branch bear it, because no rebate reaches a repair; the tax on the city's own repairs is remitted to the city's treasury and so comes home (VAT). On a forty-year stock that is about 40% of the city's whole construction output, permanently. Charging it made the city bigger: population 74,043 → 77,121, GDP $81.7M → $106.2M a month, unemployment 41.5% → 37.6% — because maintenance here is somebody's order book.

Construction recognises revenue as work is delivered: an order adds to unearned revenue and a backlog, each month's points earn their share, and the builders buy their material as they build. Booking it all up front made the sector look enormously profitable in a boom and catastrophic the year after. Until 0.7.17 its payroll had a 25% idle floor, every post kept filled and a quarter of the wages paid with nothing to build; since then it keeps a core crew of a quarter of its posts, paid in full, and lays the rest off onto EI, and the shedding spiral is still possible and still worth warning about (the builders). Since 0.7.22 an order's unearned remainder can go back — a cancelled order of the city's is refunded out of the order book — and the overtime the city pays on a rushed site is earned beside the work on a line of its own, Overtime on rushed sites, and paid out to the crews as wages.

07

Goods & markets

There used to be two bespoke markets, one for food and one for iron, each with its own formula and its own bugs. There is one rule now, and thirty-three goods that obey it or opt out — thirteen of which are the food on a grocer's shelf, three of which are vehicles, two of which the world makes and will not buy back, and, since 0.7.62, two of which are oil: the crude the wells lift and the fuel a refinery makes of it.

World prices are in the world's money and reach the city through the exchange rate; the third number is what moving one unit costs, three quarters of half the wedge between the other two

Fuellitre$1.67$0.70$0.36on the band, held in the refiners' tanks; its import price is a journey's $2.00 of fuel over the journey's 1.2 litres, and the drivers and the railway draw it off the tanks before they import (0.7.62)
supply = flow + stock / 6 // never the raw stockpile position = demand / (demand + supply) price = exportPrice + (importPrice − exportPrice) × position

The symmetric form is load-bearing. demand/supply clamped to 1 prices a perfectly balanced market at the buyer's worst price — one mine feeding one mill used to clear at $399.40 against a $400 ceiling. Here a matched pair splits the band. Supply is flow plus a slice of the stockpile, never the stockpile itself: pricing off inventory alone is circular — low price, withhold, bigger inventory reads as more supply, lower price — and measured that way food collapsed to the floor in every scenario including a genuine shortage.

Neither end of a band is a constant: both are read off the world's own prices every month, through the exchange rate, so a devaluation is import inflation mechanically — and an incentive to substitute. And since 16 September neither end is a single number either: each is the world's own price plus, or less, the cost of moving one unit, so the band a city faces is three quarters freight and one quarter the world's margin, and the freight is a price a railway can win back — see transport. Since 0.7.29 the market reads its wedge apart (GoodsMarket.worldMargin(), freightInBand(), railInWedge(), bandByLorry()), and Infrastructure's Freight page draws it a good at a time. The shops buy local food first and import the shortfall; the mills take local ore first and scrap for the rest. One clearing pass runs every month: flow goods are made, seller-priced goods sold, each traded good priced, offered, bid for and allocated pro rata on both sides, then imported or exported, then the stockable goods made. Every fill is a trade; nothing in these classes moves money. Since 0.7.12 a seller stocks only what its cash and the credit it can still get will pay for, after the bills it cannot avoid; a maker's inputs are the exception and are bought whole, because what a maker produces does not yet read what it bought, and holding its inputs back would make goods out of nothing. The households are not a sector and cannot bid in a strike: they take cars off the makers' shelf at the struck price and import the rest, through the same door the city uses for building material, and a month's purchases are demand for next month's strike. A good with a floor of zero — a van, a wagon set — strikes at exactly zero when nobody here wants one, and a saved zero is restored as zero, because for one build a guard against a missing figure dropped it and a reloaded city read a mid-band price in its place.

What the shops charge

Jerus asked why a city whose central bank held every bond, aimed at 20% and charged nothing barely inflated, and most of the answer was the grocer's shelf: it was cost-plus times a scarcity mark-up of at most 1.6, read off the share of a head count the shops delivered, so no price in the city's basket read anybody's money. Since 0.7.43 it clears on money (Retail.repriceShelf()):

floor = max(the opening price at the expected price level, what the stock cost × 1.5) clearing = the price at which the baskets the households ask for meet what the shops can hand over target = clamp(clearing, floor, 1.5 × floor) ln shelf' = (1 − 1/6) × (ln shelf + ln(1 + expected inflation a month)) + 1/6 × ln target

What the shops can hand over is their coverage times their operating rate, as far as the shelf holds; the clearing price is found by bisection on the households' own demand at a price (people), and a shortage is shared out at it, so the poorest go without first. Sticky in logs, the shelf drifts with what people expect while it closes on what clears it, about six months to clear (CLEAR_SPEED), and one under its floor closes half the gap a month (FLOOR_CATCH_UP). The cap of 1.5× the floor (CLEARING_CAP) is the most a shortage can do: a cap of 2 was measured to run the autopilot at 3.2% a year. Rent moves in the same shape at a lease's pace, a twelfth a month, and the boutiques' and the kitchens' margins at a sixth (prices).

And the suppliers wait for their money (0.7.44; SupplierCredit). A grocer restocks only as far as its cash and its credit reach (0.7.12), and since 0.7.43 it sells only what is on its shelf, so a till that ran dry bought nothing, sold nothing and stayed dry: 11.9% of the autopilot's months after month 240 handed over less than 5% of what was wanted, and in 94% of them the city had the shops. A real grocer's suppliers wait about a month, so these do: what the till cannot cover of the month's stock, up to the stock for a month of the sales the shops expect at what it costs to bring in (Retail.SUPPLIER_CREDIT_MONTHS), is bought on their credit, with no interest and no fee, and repaid at the next strike out of the takings of its sale. It is on both sets of books — the grocer's payables, its suppliers' receivables, the world's share across the audit as a financial flow in and, a month later, out — and the lender reads each as if it were not there. Total stock-outs fell from 11.9% of those months to 0.1%.

What a household eats

Until September 2026 one good called Food did all of this: a unit was a person fed for a month, everything they ate, and every question about a city's diet had that one answer. The shelf is itemised now, and what goes into the basket is decided by income rather than by a dial.

Engel's law is the first half: as a household gets richer the share of its money spent on food falls while the amount rises. Bennett's law is the second: the calories move off starchy staples onto meat, dairy, fish and produce. That is why grains carry a negative income elasticity — a staple is an inferior good, and a household eats less rice as it gets richer, not more.

Neither law is told what to do at the top of the ladder. A basket is scaled by the lesser of what a household can afford and what it can eat, so a poor household is money-limited and goes hungry while a rich one is calorie-limited and buys dearer food instead of more. The falling food share at the top is something the model produces rather than something it is given — which matters, because the first draft typed that share in as a constant and produced households whose calories went down as they got richer.

There is a third axis that is not income at all. Convenience food is bought for time: a working parent buys it at any income, and an out-of-work parent with the same children and the same money does not, because what they are short of is money and what they have is afternoons.

Income is measured in multiples of what bare subsistence costs rather than in dollars, so a currency reform cannot move it and the number means something you can say out loud: this household can afford four times the calories it needs to survive. The shops stock one shelf for the whole city, averaged over its households by headcount rather than by spending — a shelf stocked for money would carry the rich end's fish and fruit for a city of labourers.

The shelf is thirteen pantries

A shop can sell a basket only if it has all of it: what it can serve is the good that runs out first, which is Liebig's barrel on a shelf. That is also how a units bug was caught — shelf room was still sized in units of food when the goods became kilograms, and one person-month is exactly 50 kg of them, so every shop in the city delivered 20% of demand for ever. A city with roads and a city without scored identically until it was found; afterwards, 87% against 31%.

When the shelf was itemised all thirteen were imported. Eleven of them can be made at home now: the ovens bake bread and bakery goods out of crops and export what the city cannot eat; the farms make dairy and eggs, meat, vegetables and fruit; the plants make the five processed goods out of meat, vegetables, grains and crops. Grains and fish still only ever come off a ship. And since 18 September the shelf has a second buyer: the kitchens buy the same thirteen in the reference basket's proportions and sell them cooked, one ninetieth of a person-month a plate.

The hunger measure was reporting zero in a city that was starving

Until 17 September the figure read sold over the shops' own demand, and the shops' demand was already the least of coverage, want and what could be afforded — the shops' performance against a queue the shops had themselves decided they could serve. At month 3,840 of seed 0 the households planned 21,446,946 baskets, the shops could serve 177,760 people, and the line said 76%. A basket is one person-month, so the city wanted a hundred and twenty-two baskets a head and was allowed one. Honest: 58% of people short, 1.4% of the groceries asked for. Not a reporting change: the hungry feed the sick rate, which feeds the labour supply, and it had been fed a zero. Turning it on cost the default seed 11% of its population and 14% of its GDP — revealed, not caused — and showed a cascade nobody had seen, sickness 12.9% to 34.5%, of which hunger is eight points and the unburied fifteen. The basket count is floored to a whole basket, because dividing money by money and keeping the fraction broke the currency-reform twin 1.5e-06 at a time.

Since 0.7.43 hunger is counted in baskets: what each household was handed at the last sale, and the meals it ate out at a ninetieth of a basket each, against a basket a head. It had been the plan times the share delivered, one share for the whole city, so a shortage fell on everybody alike; it falls on the households the price leaves out now. And since 0.7.27 it comes in two halves, both saved: the people who would have gone short with the shelves full, priced out (HouseholdBalance.getHungryAtFullShelves(), getHungerPricedOut()), and the rest, whom the shelves left short (getHungerShortOfStock()), beside the share of what was asked for that the shops handed over (getDeliveredShare()), which had read 1 after every load. In the 2,400-month research city at 0.7.27 GOING SHORT read 42.2%, 49 people could not afford a basket, and the shops handed over 3% of what was planned. The default seed's city at 0.7.49 ended 79% short, the shops handing over 5.7% of the baskets it asked for and filling 44% of what their coverage could serve, running at 44% on roads at 71%: a city its player leaves alone for decades.

And the city can pay toward it (0.7.43). Jerus: "yes price rations, with an optional government assistance, defualt zero". The food assistance dial on Promises → Food (TaxPolicy.getFoodAssistance(), 0 to 100%, nothing by default) is the share of an eligible household's baskets a voucher covers at the shelf price, and the voucher is money it bids with. A household is eligible when a basket a head would take more than half of what it has after its fixed bills and its investment income (FOOD_ASSISTANCE_MEANS_SHARE; that income smoothed over a year since 0.7.45, because a coupon landing in one month took a whole row in and out), which takes in everybody with nothing after their fixed bills. It is paid only on the baskets actually got, into the household's savings at the till, and by the treasury in the same month on a line of its own (TreasuryLine.FOOD_ASSISTANCE), a promise, because the groceries have been eaten.

08

The private sector

Nobody tells businesses what to build. Each of the seventeen sectors measures a different signal, projects it six months out, and expands only if the new capacity would service its own debt.

the brake estimated monthly profit ≥ monthly interest at the real rate × 1.25 the real rate = max(the rate − the inflation the owners expect, a quarter of the rate) // since 0.7.42

A business test, not a credit limit: the lender is willing, the business should not be. Since 0.7.42 it is real (BusinessInvestment.realTestRate(), REAL_HURDLE_FLOOR): the debt is nominal and the profits grow with prices, so a 6% loan in a city expecting 2% costs 4% in what the profits will buy, and the floor keeps expected inflation near the rate from making borrowing free. Alongside it: one order at a time per sector, bar the landlords, who since 0.7.17 may hold twelve months of the builders' work on their sites (the builders); no order larger than would open inside twelve months at the wait it would actually have beside everything on site, where until 0.7.17 it was twelve months of the whole city's construction output, as though the order had the builders to itself; and a 5% headroom target so nothing expands into capacity it already has. Since 0.7.8 a plan is judged at the rate the loan would actually be written at, off the default curve at the leverage the loan would leave the sector at, and since 0.7.12 at the blend of a bond's coupon and the loan's rate where a bond carries part of it; 3,452 plans were declined on price over the default eight when the curve went in. A landlord's homes answer to the lender's own test instead (0.7.11): the building's net operating income must cover the mortgage's payment 1.20× — since 0.7.44 a payment struck at the same real rate of the insured rate, though the mortgage is still written at the insured rate and a refusal names the rate it tested. All three are searched for since 0.7.54 — the largest order inside the wait, the largest slice of it that carries its interest, and the most a landlord's mortgage buys — where each was counted a building at a time, so that the month grew with the order: a ten-billion copy of city2400 took a median of 7.0 s a month, and up to 143 s. The size and the mortgage halve, because their tests are proved to hold on a run from one; the trim asks the old countdown's first 16 slices and then doubles down and halves (Game.COUNTDOWN_SLICES), so an order trimmed by fewer is decided exactly as before, and a refusal still names the rate at the whole order. In every run measured the searches decided what the counts did, that copy's month at a median of 46 ms, and OrderSearchCheck holds them to the countdowns.

And every planner that builds posts asks whether the city could staff it (0.7.18; Sector.staffing()). Jerus: "Construction, Retail, Restaurants, Luxury and the makers check whether they can staff a building before building it (the check four sectors already use), and the 20% it allows can't be jobs nobody can fill." A building passes when the city's spare workers could fill 80% of its posts (MIN_STAFFABLE_TO_ORDER), each band's spare counted as the best-paid-job fill would place it, and none of the rest is in a band nobody can fill. That last is Jerus's reading of his own words, "Truly unfillable only": a band with no spare workers, nobody above it who could step down into it, and no migrants who come for it. Every band has migrants who come for its wage since the same batch, so in play it never binds — 0 of 2,160 staffing questions in a default city — and stands for a band that ever has no way in, which InvestCheck's section 18 causes. An order of several is held to all of them, and the advisor's hold says "the city could staff X% of a Y; it wants 80%". The literal first reading, no posts at all in a band with nobody spare this month, failed every building with a college post in every city without a college, and the default cities ended at 13,808 people against 162,702. Real Estate's homes have no posts and are not asked, and the city's own buildings are the player's; the bank's branch is asked since 0.7.19. In Jerus's year-149 city, run on for 120 months, it held Retail and Restaurants back in 108 of them, Luxury in 116 and Food Processing and Mining in all 120 (0.7.18).

SectorWatches
Real estatejobs plus jobs under construction — never population, which is its own echo
Retailbaskets, since 0.7.43: the households' demand at the shelf's floor, grown with the population over the order's lead time and capped at housing that exists or is being built, against what its shops can hand over — coverage times the operating rate — so a city whose shops cover everybody on paper and hand over 43% of it builds another; until then people against coverage
Industryshop coverage against output; refuses outright if price is below cost
Constructionits own backlog over what the sites are left after the repairs, expanding past nine months of it, and only with a depot the city could staff (0.7.17)
Heavy industryspare ore — mines first, mills after
Miningunmined deposits, so geology gates it and the player controls the gate: since 0.7.57 the iron sites of the world's fields in the ground the city owns, one mine a site, and since 0.7.62 counting Iron Mines only (Game.minesCommitted())
Materialsthe sites' outstanding orders and the repair draw, on a three-year view
Business servicesseats it could actually staff — the world's appetite for the work is unbounded, so the brake is people, not demand
Manufacturingthe price of steel, which is the whole of it — 61% of a fabricator's revenue
Agriculturethe mills' crop bill against the price of the ground a field has to stand on — and refuses while anybody in the city has nowhere to live
Food processingwhat the city already eats of its five goods, less what it can already supply, valued at each good's export floor — never a forecast, and never the export market, because the band is narrower than the processing step; scores every template it owns, not the one that makes its planning good
Railthe freight nobody is carrying — it will not lay a line under 60% used, Materials' lesson relearned the afternoon a spur went up in a town of four hundred houses
Automotivethe parts — one new plant may want at most a quarter of everything the city fabricates, because "spare" was the wrong word and cost a fixture three hundred months
Luxury retailthe buyers at the margin it charges, against the counters — the queue counted at the floor price until 0.7.19
Restaurantsthe queue at the door: meals asked for against the tables there are, and a kitchen values itself, because nothing else can
Oiloil sites the city owns with no well on them and oil left in them: one well a month, a well retired when its oil runs out (0.7.62)
Refininga whole plant's worth of the city's own — fuel the drivers and the railway draw that the refineries standing and on site do not make, or crude its wells lift that no refinery takes — and then whether the fuel clears above its cost at nameplate, its crude priced at what importing it would cost past the wells' spare (0.7.62)

What the investors said, in a kind (0.7.30). Each sector's planner leaves a word a month, its order or what held it, and the screens read the word into one of twelve kinds — building, selling, land, staff, licence, ore, supply, credit, money, enough, none and other (BuildCard.wordKind(), off the word's own text, whole words, the first match winning) — and a sector's investors as one record (BuildCard.sectorInvestors()), which the Sectors list, every business's frame and its Investors page draw, and Build's market cards beside the first gate that building fails. The words are not saved, so after a load or a founding the list says once that nothing is recorded until a month runs (0.7.34).

The eighth, ninth and tenth, and why they are different

The first seven sectors all sold to the city. Business services and manufacturing sell to the world, which is what broke a two-hundred-year plateau: a city whose every customer lives in it can only grow as fast as it grows. Agriculture is the other direction — it sells to the mills, and it is the only sector whose real cost is the ground it stands on.

SectorInOutWhere the money goes
Business servicesalmost entirely people; 250 sq ft of lot a headsupport, back-office and engineering work, by the seat-month, at $5,500 / $8,600 / $14,600payroll 66.3% / 65.2% / 55.2%
Manufacturingsteel, local or importedfabricated steel and machinery, flow not stock; export only until the assembly plants arrivedsteel 61.0%, or wages 49.1% at the Machine Works
Agricultureland heavily, labour and power barelyCROPS, to the millslabour a seventh of a field's sale, power a five-hundredth

The engineering office is gated on a licence, not on money: it will not open without spare licensed engineers for half its gated posts (LICENCE_COVER_TO_OPEN = 0.5 — thirty-nine of them; thirty-eight is one short), and the only way to make one at home is an Institute of Technology. Steel got its first domestic customer and with it an import ceiling of 1.284 against the mills' export floor of .847, a 1.52× wedge that decides whether fabricating here pays at all: 1.55% of build cost a month on imported steel at the ceiling, 3.28% on steel at the mills' floor. The mills got an input cost — they used to make food out of nothing, on margins of 72–77% against every other plant's 28–31%.

The farmland dial

TaxPolicy.FARMLAND_RELIEF assesses farmland at use value rather than development value, on the land half only — barns are still taxed as buildings. It defaults to full relief and says in dollars what the relief costs. Six grain farms on $112m of ground: $151,496 a month with no relief, $11,295 with full relief. Without it a field cannot outbid a warehouse for the same acre, and by month 4,002 at $66 a square foot there are no fields left — ten greenhouses, and the two founding mixed farms.

The sixteenth and seventeenth: oil, and its refinery

Jerus's city bought every litre its drivers and its railway burned abroad: US$27M a month at month 1,851, about fifteen million litres. Since 0.7.62 the city can make it. Oil (sectors.Oil) has Mining's shape: an Oil Well stands on an oil site the city owns — the world's oil lies in fields as its iron does (land) — and lifts 415 tonnes of crude a month, a hundred barrels a day, for $2.0M and 400 points on a two-acre pad with three posts; the sector plans one a month while a site is unworked and oil is left, and retires a well whose oil has run out. With no oil site the order is refused No oil deposit, and the refusal's page buys the cheapest offer holding oil. Refining (sectors.Refining) has heavy industry's: an Oil Refinery, a modular plant of 2,000 barrels a day, takes 8,300 tonnes of crude a month into 8,300,000 litres of fuel, a thousand litres a tonne, with a hundred posts on twenty acres for $60M, and keeps three months of it in its tanks; it buys the wells' crude first and the world's for the rest. The drivers (Motoring.drawFuel(), at 6d, 1.2 litres a journey) and the railway (eighteen litres a tonne hauled) draw on those tanks at the market's price before they import, so the refiners' shelf replaces what the city bought abroad, and with no refinery the bill is what it was, at the world's price level. The refinery is gated as a mill is on spare ore: it is planned only for a whole plant's worth of the city's own — fuel the city draws that the refineries standing and on site do not make, or crude its wells lift that no refinery takes — and then while the investors' estimate clears. Without the gate the playtest's city stood 120 refineries at month 4,002, every one an exporter on imported crude, because a tonne bought at $600 sells abroad as a thousand litres at $0.70 and a refinery's crew and power cost less than the $100 between; with a gate at any fuel uncovered the first stood at month 438 in a town of 800. Crude is a flow good, as ore is: a well ships what it lifts and has no tank. Fuel in the tanks is stock the national accounts count as held, and the refiners' sales to the drivers are consumption beside the shops' (GDP); the money audit's FuelFunded and FuelImports carry only the imported part, the domestic part being on Refining's own statement. In the playtest at 0.7.64 the first well stood at month 1,119 and the first refinery at 2,564, and 79.9% of the drivers' and the railway's fuel over the run came from abroad (OilCheck, how it's checked).

How an expansion is paid for

In a fixed order, every month: sell shares if the register says the company should be raising equity, spend the till, call money home from abroad, and only then borrow. A company under a year old sells half of what it plans; a company with nine good months raises toward its equity target; a company with a bad year raises nothing and borrows nothing. Since 0.7.12 borrowing is two doors: the sector takes the largest bond whose all-in cost is no dearer than the bank's loan and borrows the rest from the bank, and loan and bond together never pass what the bank would lend. A landlord's homes are bought another way since 0.7.11: at least 15% of the cost from its own till and what it holds abroad, its owners asked for new shares for what those lack, and an insured mortgage for the rest (the bank).

Capacity retires, and distress retires it faster

Six consecutive losing months, genuinely spare capacity by a 25% margin, and nothing on site — then the biggest holding is thinned, never past what is actually in use, never more than a quarter in one month. The streak is carried in the save, because forgetting it made scrapping save-scummable.

Separately, a firm that cannot pay sheds plant: twenty-four months of losses and a negative balance after the credit desk has already refused it. Six months killed every founding plant in testing; two years is the runway a real business gets. Since 0.7.12 it may sell any plant that makes something the sector sells, because the rule could once sell only plant making a sector's main good and a Shared Services Centre lost money for 1,151 months. What is retired is sold to the builders as material since 0.7.8 — Jerus: "sold to construction company just in materials, and land to treasury" — so Construction pays for the template's material at the day's price as far as its cash goes and builds with it before it buys, and the plot goes back to the city as it always did. Occupied homes and the bank are never scrapped by their owners, though since 0.7.22 the city may buy a landlord's homes out and pull them down (the builders); the bank closes its own branches, by its own rule (the bank). A mine over a worked-out deposit now closes inside a year instead of borrowing to pay its crew for three — which makes the bust when the ore runs out sharper, and lets the bank survive it. And a distressed landlord can no longer demolish a city out from under it: the railway's bigger business cycle pushed Real Estate into distress deep enough that it sold 5,800 of a city's 13,272 homes in seven months and left 1,137 households with nowhere for a hundred and forty, because the retirement rule asked whether the segment had spare doors and a segment's slack is spread over four sizes. It is guarded, not fixed: nobody sells a door while the housing match says somebody has nowhere, and the proper fix — slack per size — is on the list.

Two banners exist because these fail silently

Builders being laid off — construction with no orders shrinks to fit, and everything you build runs through those crews. Nowhere to build — the private sector asks for land every month, is told no, and waits. Measured at 120 consecutive months in one ordinary run.

09

The builders & the queue

Every building in the city is put up by one sector out of one month's output, and until 0.7.17 that output was split equally by building type on site, so a single depot took as much as two hundred blocks of flats. Three batches made it the crew each building can use (0.7.17), a price that keeps up with the wages that build it (0.7.19), and a queue the player can put a hand on (0.7.22).

Every building gets the crew it can use

Jerus chose "Share building work fairly": "Builders' output split by buildings on site (every site gets a crew), and no more parking points on building types with nothing left to build". The rule it replaced shared the sites' output equally by building type. In his year-149 city four types were on site: the builders' own one-depot order, owed 400 points, took a quarter of the output, about 10,700 points, and carried the other 10,300 as progress on a stack with nothing left on it; the landlords' 219 blocks of flats got the same quarter, about 250 homes a month; and 7.82M points sat parked on stacks with nothing to build.

weight = buildings on site × points a building0.70 // CREW_SCALE_EXPONENT = 1 − Bromilow's B share = the month's site output after the repairs, one rate a unit of weight, each site capped at what it still owes, and what a capped site leaves shared by the same rule

The exponent is Bromilow's time–cost law, T = K × CB, with B about 0.30 on Australian building contracts and 0.32 on Malaysian public projects (Chan 1999): the crew a building can use is its work over its time, which grows as C1−B. So a building twice the cost takes about 1.23 times as long, and a university of 26,000 points has about eighteen times the crew of a depot's 400. Only the exponent is taken; K, the pace a building could go at alone, is not modelled, and nothing caps a site's crew at it. The weight is the whole building, never what is left of it, because a crew that shrinks with its work never finishes: the rule was briefly the points each stack still owed, and in Jerus's city twelve sites sat one building from done for years. Output no site can use is idle, read as the builders' utilisation, and never banked; a save's parked points are cleared on load and move no money — 7,801,561 of them in his city.

It took four readings to get there. One crew a building, whatever its size, gave a lone university beside two hundred blocks of flats one block's share, and it took ninety months where it had taken twenty-one. A crew in proportion to its points gave a founding's six shops of 120 points a four-hundredth of the output between them, beside a coal plant of 120,000 and the founding's other big works, and the shops did not open for a decade; those two are the exponent at nought and at one. Jerus's own hybrid, "every order has atleast one crew, and then spare crews are assigned in ascending or descending order sort of like a pyramid", cleared every small building a large order starved in the checks, but the builders bought depots for the small orders that rushed through and then stood laid off: thirteen banks failed over the eight default cities against four, and unemployment moved half as much again in a month. By the rule he left, "v3 if you think yes, otherwise go for the v that starving small orders is fine", Bromilow's weights ship, and a small order waits behind a large one. One wait is read off this rule by everything that asks how long an order would take (BuildingManager.waitFor()): the planners' lead times and order sizes, the shops' horizon and the build quote, each the order's points over the share it would get beside everything on site.

The builders count repairs and staffing

Jerus chose "Builders count repairs and staffing": "Construction's own planner counts repairs and how well it can staff a depot, both when it adds depots and when it sells them off." The builders' months of queued work are read against what the sites are left after the repairs, which take about 40% of a mature city's construction output, and at their staffed output; a depot is ordered only if the city could staff 80% of it; and retirement keeps what the repairs and the queue need, in staffed output. In his city the depots stood 36% staffed, because a depot is 70% unskilled posts and the unskilled rung was 8.5% filled, and the planner, reading its nameplate against the queue alone, sold its capacity from 232,000 points a month to 56,800 between months 912 and 1,020, after which homes sat flat for nine years. Since 0.7.19 a depot's profit estimate counts the wages of the posts it would fill; it had counted none, and priced a point at what a unit of material costs.

The crews the work needs. Jerus chose "Lay off idle crews". Before the labour market fills the month's posts the builders strike the share of their depots' posts the work ahead needs — the repairs plus every point still owed on site, less what the city's own works department does with no posts, over what the depots would do fully staffed — divided by last month's fill, so that the crews who come are the crews the work needs, and never under 25% of the posts (Construction.IDLE_PAYROLL_FLOOR) nor over all of them. The posts not offered are not jobs: the city counts fewer, the workers who held them are unemployed by the ordinary path and draw EI, and the allocator gives them any other post it has. The posts kept on are paid in full. Until 0.7.17 an idle builder kept every post filled and paid a quarter of the wages; once every employer's bill became what its workers receive, that quarter was what the crews took home, and the batch's first build, which kept it, turned MonetaryCheck's reading of the rate over and tripled the bank failures of the city held at 25%. The planners still count every post, because a builder that laid crews off for want of work is not short of crews for a queue. What it costs: unemployment moves about twice as much from month to month, the 95th percentile of the default eight's monthly move from 0.81 points to 1.93 (0.7.17).

The landlords hold a year of work

Jerus chose "Landlords hold work, not one order": "Landlords may hold orders worth a number of months of building work, instead of one order at a time sized on the whole city's output." One order at a time, sized on twelve months of the whole city's output, was delivered at about 250 homes a month in his city, and the next order was held already building for fifty months. Since 0.7.17 they keep ordering while what their sites owe, the order being weighed included, is at most 12 months of the builders' site output after the repairs (BusinessInvestment.MAX_ORDER_MONTHS, withinMonthsOfWork()), read with every post offered, since builders who have laid crews off hire them back for the order. When their best home does not fit they order the next smaller one that does (Jerus: "Try a smaller home"), and the homes on site count as their supply. Every other sector keeps one order at a time. In his city, run on for 120 months from month 1,792, 0.7.17 added 118,944 homes where 0.7.16 added 38,052, the city grew to 1,517,361 against 1,284,343, and all 47 of its care complexes were finished, the last at month 108.

The price keeps up with the wages

Jerus chose "Builders' prices keep up": "The labour part of building and repair prices rises with wages, materials are paid at the price when they're used, and sales tax is in the builder's quote." Until 0.7.19 the non-material part of every contract and repair bill was the template's cash cost in founding dollars. In his city at month 1,793 a Low-Rise paid the builders 4.25 a point of work against a full depot's wage bill of 6.59 a point, and they lost money in every one of the decade's 120 months, $46.97B in all.

labour = min(cash cost / Pe, points × a depot's founding wage bill a point) × (a depot's wage bill a point today / at founding) work = the rest of the cash cost, at its founding value struck at the expected price level Pe, + labour price = (work + imports + plant × (1 − rM)) / (1 − rB) // rB the builders' sales tax, rM the materials plant's

A Construction Depot's 35 unskilled and 15 diploma posts cost $188.6k a month at the founding ladder for 400 points, 0.47 a point, and that much of a template's cash cost a point is its labour; the rest — overhead, equipment and margin, all but 1,132 of a Low-Rise's 10,196 — stays at its founding value. Since 0.7.42 that founding value is struck every month at the price level people expect, as every money constant is (the month), so the founding labour is read off the cash over that level and put back at today's wages (BuildingManager.nonMaterialCost()): labour moves with wages and the rest with expectations, and neither is counted twice; at a level of one both read as they did. Indexing the whole cash cost by the wage index was measured by hand and rejected: in his city it would have been 13.5 times the cash cost, a windfall rather than a price. The split is the model's own, because Statistics Canada's Building Construction Price Index is an output index and publishes no labour and material weights. It reaches the quote, the repair bills and the planners' costing, and not the book values or the assessments, which carry a building at its cash cost as before.

The material is paid at the price when it is used, by an escalation clause on the pattern of the US Federal Acquisition Regulation's 52.216-4. The owner still pays the whole quote up front, the material priced at the day's rates as an allowance; as the crews draw it, each owner pays its units' share of what the month's draw cost the builders, less its allowance, or is refunded the difference (Game.settleSiteContracts()). The city pays on a treasury line of its own, Material escalation, owed to the builders as arrears past the advances ceiling; a sector pays from its till. And the sales tax is in the quote, grossed up so that the builders keep exactly the work: until 0.7.19 they remitted their rate on all of it out of the work, and in his city 108.6 of their 150.2 of tax fell on material passed through at cost. Who gets it back is the VAT's (tax, policy and GDP).

In his city, run on for 120 months, the builders went from losing $46.97B before tax to earning $9.03B, with 18 loss months where there had been 120; a Low-Rise paid 10.36 a point against a full depot's labour of 6.59, a margin of 36% of what it paid. The first pass took about 30% off the ensembles' cities — the default eight from 166,607 people at the median to 115,002 — because the landlords bore the tax on every home and the bank's first branch carried its upkeep; the rebates on a new rental home and the charter's exemption (the bank) took both back, and the shipped default city ended at 169,978 against 0.7.18's 166,607 (p 0.442).

Sectors › Construction reads the month its statement struck since 0.7.46. The materials row had read the draws since the strike beside a statement of the month before, two different months on one page; Construction now keeps what its month drew, bought from the plant and imported, as its books close (Sector.beforeBank()) and saves it, and the page reads Last month, as on its statement, with Since then, so far under it. A save from before reads not counted yet until a month runs.

The player's hand on the queue

Jerus wanted a construction panel that is "not just a blue loading screen", and then "not only repirotize and cancel but also destroy buildings, like you yourself destroy buildings". On 30 September he chose the mechanics — priority "Both", cancel "Keep the half-built shell", demolish "City's, plus buy-outs (Recommended)" — and since 0.7.22 each is a rule with a source (ConstructionControl). None of it runs unless the player uses it: with no order set and no rush, every wait and every quote is the old one to the bit, and the default playtest's traces did not move. A site is what the model keeps on site, one stack per building type and, since this batch, each of the city's demolitions; the city's sites are the stacks whose every order is the city's. A stack it shares with an investor's order cannot be reordered, rushed or cancelled, because its progress is one pool.

The five hands, with their sources (0.7.22)
HandThe ruleSource
Prioritythe city's share of the crews' rule is split among its own sites top down, in the player's order, each taking what it still owes; nobody else's site gains or loses a point; free; a new city site joins at the bottom, and the order clears itself once none of its sites is leftnone needed: an owner choosing which of its own contracts goes first
Rushone of the city's sites on 50-hour weeks: 1.137 times a normal month's work in its first month, 1.024 in its second, 0.938 in its third and 0.9375 after, less than a normal month; the city pays 0.375 of the crews' wage bill on the site's share, with the builders' tax, and the crews are paid it as wages; owed as arrears past the ceiling; the count resets after a month offBusiness Roundtable, Scheduled Overtime Effect on Construction Projects, Report C-2 (1980), Figure 4; time and a half, Canada Labour Code s. 174
Cancelat the month's end: the month's work done and billed, and the work not done, the material not drawn and the tax on both refunded out of the builders' book; the shell keeps its work, its material and its ground, to be restarted at today's quote or demolishedtermination for convenience, FAR 52.249-2; its settlement costs have no line in the model and none is invented
Demolisha site of 5% of the template's points, priced at that building's own work rate with the tax and crewed by the same rule; the building closes as the next month starts — its staff let go and, if it is a home, its households moved into the homes that stand, whoever does not fit left unhoused rather than deleted — its material is sold to the builders by the 0.7.8 rule when the work is done, and its ground comes back to the citySIGTARP, 26 April 2017: Detroit's average demolition of July 2015, $14,855, over NAHB's 2015 average new home, $289,415, is 5.1%
Buy-outa business's or a landlord's building: the owner is paid its book value, its ground at the land price, and its share of the owner's operating income for the months a replacement would take; its debts stay with it; then the city demolishes itOntario's Expropriations Act, ss. 13(2), 14(1) and 19(1); the 5% residential allowance of s. 18(1)(a)(i) does not apply, because no owner in the model lives in what it owns
What each hand did in the harness's fixture city, as 0.7.22's implementer probed it at about month 38 with the builders' tax at 15% (0.7.22)
HandWhat happened
Cancelan Elementary School ordered at $35.1M, cancelled at 1,023 of its 1,800 points: $15.17M back — $7.73M of work not done, $5.16M of material not drawn, $2.28M of tax; restarting the shell costs $13.68M at today's quote, demolishing it $0.60M
Rusha University on overtime for three months: 1,377, 1,243 and 1,253 points of work against shares of 1,211, 1,213 and 1,335, for a premium of about $230k, $231k and $254k a month with the tax
Buy-outa landlord's block of Low-Rise Apartments: $17.82M to the landlords — $17.12M for the building, $0.15M for the ground and $0.55M of business loss over 10.9 months — and $0.59M to pull it down
Demolishthe city's own Elementary School: $1.05M and about 3.6 months; 48 posts and 600 places close, and its 666 units of material are worth $12.2M at that month's price

Four calls were made while Jerus slept. A demolished building closes as the next month starts, because closing it on the spot made a city reloaded between the presses play a different month from the live one. A month whose normal share would finish the site anyway is not worked on overtime. Short of materials never shows as a site's status, because the model imports whatever the yard, the salvage and the plant do not cover. And the bank's branches can be neither demolished nor bought, since the bank closes them by its own rule. The docs pass's throwaway audit balanced the money to $0.00 through a rush, a cancel, two demolitions and a twenty-house buy-out.

A demolition can pay

The 0.7.8 rule sells a building's material back at the day's market price, so the fixture's school brought $12.2M of material against a $1.05M demolition, and real demolition recovers far less than new material costs. Whether to lower it or keep it as the 0.7.8 rule wanted is on the list (open questions).

The construction page

The hand is played on a page of its own (ui/ConstructionScreen), reached from the construction panel's Open — a panel folded since 0.7.24 to a 44 px tab down the stage's right edge, a crane, the count of sites and UNDER CONSTRUCTION, which a click opens over the stage, open or folded kept between sessions and folded by default — from Construction at the head of Build, and from a build card's N on site, which opens it at that site. Sites carries the builders' gauge — BUILDERS, the month's points after the repairs; OWED ON SITE; the QUEUE in months against the landlords' twelve; and the MATERIAL in the yard — and then a row a site: the city's first, in the player's order with a rank to move, then everyone else's, then the stopped shells; who ordered it, the done of the total, this month's crews and the time left, paid and owed, a status (waiting for crews, stops at month's end, closes as the month starts among them) and the hand. Timeline draws a bar a site to its finish at today's queue. Demolish is a picker — the city's buildings, the stopped shells, and the businesses' and landlords' to buy out — and a staging card that says what would close, what it costs, what comes back, the land and the months, before Demolish or Discard. Every hand that spends money asks first, with the money in the question, and the clock stands still while any question is up. One clock times a site everywhere (Game.siteMonths()), because the docs pass found two: the page read the player's order while the right panel, the build card and the quote read the crews' rule. The inbox gained Overtime now costs more than it gains, the month before a rush's third, and A demolition is done. None of it has been seen on a screen.

10

The owners, the bonds & the exchange

For most of the project's life the corporate sector had no owners: it made money, held it, and nothing ever moved it out. Eighteen companies have a share register now — the seventeen sectors and the bank — the sectors can sell bonds as well as shares since 0.7.12, and both trade on order books where nobody is obliged to take the other side.

The register

Every company is founded with shares issued to the city's households at $1,000 each against its book equity. A company reads its own record before it asks for money: new under a year, good at nine profitable months and not declining, bad at six or fewer or a losing year, normal otherwise. Its equity target starts at 30% of assets and rises with how volatile its income has been, capped at 70%; a new company aims at half. Households subscribe first — a cell with no debt, no lockout and three months of disposable income banked puts 30% of the excess in — and the world takes the rest, but only if the yield clears the world rate plus three points. New shares are sold at one price, the last trade or fair value before one, and never through the book, because a plan needs to know in the month it builds what it raised. The city's fund never subscribes (0.7.14).

Forty percent of a positive month is paid out, from the till, or from the company's own money abroad if the till is short — since 0.7.11 of what the month's net income leaves once the principal it repaid is taken off, Jerus: "Pay out after principal", and since 0.7.12 of that principal net of the new borrowing that rolled it, which is free cash flow to equity, the figure real companies pay dividends from. The bank pays by its own rule (the bank). A company's fair value is the higher of its book equity and the dividend it actually pays capitalised at the world's required return, per share; its price is its last trade, and before its first trade its fair value. The city's own holdings are marked at fair value once a share's last trade is a year old (Exchange.STALE_MARK_MONTHS, cityMark(), 0.7.48), so a share nobody has traded for years does not value the fund at a price nobody would pay now: in the 2,400-month research city Mining's mark went from D$1,644 a share to D$0.59.

One book for each company

Jerus: "Order book for both." Since 0.7.12 every share that changes hands meets its buyer on an order book (OrderBook), one per company: price and then time decide who is filled, a trade is at the resting order's price, nobody trades with themselves, and nobody is obliged to trade — an order with nobody across it rests, and every order still resting is withdrawn at the month's step and posted again from each participant's view of the new month. Each participant posts from the rule it already followed, in this order:

  • The bank's desk, if it has capital: a bid one per cent under fair value for what its capital can carry, an ask one per cent over it for what it holds, and an ask at fair value itself for anything past its two caps — 25% of the bank's equity in any one company and 50% in all of them. Its own shares are capital, not trading: it bids for them only with what it holds over its target and sells new ones only while it is under it.
  • Emigrants sell on the way out, and what nobody bids for stays abroad with them.
  • The world buys when the dividend yield at the best ask clears its own rate plus a three-point premium by a margin, and sells into strength when it falls short, never into a crash, and never in a company under a year old.
  • Companies past their first year and not in a bad one, with more equity than their target and six months of their costs and their debt service in the till bid for their own shares at up to 10% over fair value, a tenth of the company a year at most, and cancel what they buy. What nobody sells them stays in the company: no special dividend is paid by any path since 0.7.12, because paying the unspent money out left the tills bare and brought the failures back.
  • Every household cell is a participant of its own (Jerus: "Each household type trades"): a cell under its cushion offers a tenth of the shortfall at the market, and a cell past it buys four per cent of its excess a month into the best yield over the bank's deposit rate plus a point, the pace its dollars abroad move at.
  • The city's fund last, taking what the others left. Its rule asks at fair value and, since 0.7.48, bids for a share at the desk's ask, one per cent over it (TreasuryFund.RULE_PREMIUM), because at fair value it met nobody. Then the player's hand (0.7.39), at fair value or a price the player names — the best bid, the best ask, the last trade, or fair value in steps of 1% — a buy going no further than the room under the 10% cap, every order the fund has on the company counted as if it filled and the rule's bid making way for the hand's, and its money held out of the cash the rule's bids settle with from the moment it is placed. A waiting order can be cancelled before the step; one that cannot be posted lapses, and the fund's record says why (tax, policy and GDP).

A household short of money sells in the middle of the month, in its waterfall, at the price at which the dividend it gives up costs what borrowing would, so the rich cells' resting bids are where a poor cell's shares go — a transfer between households that no audited pool sees. A share priced at a hundred times its founding price is split a hundred for one, and one that falls to a hundredth is consolidated, and every holding, every resting order and the last trade move by the factor; a cumulative split factor keeps the price history readable per founding share, and since 0.7.48 the history keeps a share's price to six significant figures (HistorySave.SHARE_PRICE_DIGITS), where four decimal places had drawn a consolidated company's line at nothing. On the default seed on 28 September the book traded $39.2bn of shares in 44,049 trades over 4,002 months, a listed company traded in 46% of its months, and the median last trade stood at 0.99 of fair value; on 1 October it traded $92.1bn in 32,065 trades over 4,005 months, a listed company traded in 38% of its months, and the median last trade stood at 0.990; on 5 October, at 0.7.49, $52.6T in 29,333 trades at prices 1,213 times the founding's, a listed company trading in 31.5% of its months, the median last trade at 0.990 again.

The bank was the dealer, until 0.7.12

From the exchange's first night until 0.7.12 the bank's trading desk was the counterparty on every trade, and quoted nothing when the bank was dead. It quoted around fair value, its own book moved the quote, it never sold what it did not hold, and it marked what it held at the lower of the quote and fair value. Its book was capped at a multiple of the bank's equity, which is money — and until 18 September that cap was the one figure in the exchange a currency reform did not divide. It was re-struck each month after the households settled, so for the first half of every month a reformed city's desk believed it had a hundred times the room it had; a household with no savings and a grocery bill it could not meet borrowed in the plain city and sold $1.03 of shares in the reformed one, and from there household debt was 4.2% apart in one month and the exchange rate with it. The bisect blamed the fourteenth sector's inventory term, and the term was right: it had moved GDP, GDP had moved what the desk's book was worth against its limit, and a cancellation that had been landing on one side of zero started landing on the other. A bisect names what perturbed a fault, not what the fault is; a latent member of that family is not a harmless one, it is one nobody has perturbed yet.

mid = fair × clamp(1 − 0.25 × (dealerShares − demand) / scale, 0.50, 5.00) // the dealer's quote, until 0.7.12 bid/ask = mid ∓ half of a 2% spread

The desk foots, since 19 September. Its opened lines on the bank's statement had left out the term that is usually the largest — the change in the mark on what it holds — so a loss sat over sub-lines that did not add to it. Re-marked what it holds (Bank.getMarkChange()) is a line of its own now, and when it is the bulk of a loss the note says why, which is Jerus's question — "explain to me the trading desk, cause a bunch of times it's losing billions": with buyers it could not fill the dealer quoted up to five times fair value to draw sellers, and it marked what it held at the lower of the quote and fair value, so a wave of emigrants selling into a bubble cost it the difference the same day. BankCheck asserts the lines plus the re-mark equal the total, and on a played city they did in 120 of 120 months. Whether a dealer should bid at a quote it does not believe in stopped being a question when the dealer went; the desk marks at the lower of the last trade and fair value. Since 0.7.1 the same desk also buys back the city's paper from the households, at the month's market value off the curve.

What it was worth

When the exchange arrived, eight seeds against the batch before it: GDP $65M → $77M a month, worst unemployment 46% → 37%, exports $104M → $124M, the currency inside 3.5% of parity, dividends to households $3–18bn → $30–56bn a run, every company home-owned in six seeds of eight — and bank failures 10 → 0, because the trading desk was a business and a profitable one, and a sector that has just paid its owners borrows for its next mine.

The firms sell bonds

Jerus: "notes are the loans businesses make to the banks, and bonds are debts that they issue to investors… households can buy them, but banks also buy them as, only if it wants, and yes they are tradeable so they have par value and all." The bank failed 115 times on 0.7.11's eight default seeds, and the cause was concentration: the one bank was every business's only lender, and the sector that broke it owed five to six times its equity. So since 0.7.12 a sector borrows through two doors. A bond (CorporateBond) is a ten-year bullet with a coupon every month, built at par: the underwriter announces the room, every bidder says what it would take at each yield, the coupon is the lowest yield that fills the issue and the rest is a bank loan, and the bank underwrites it for the city's own charge, $12k plus 0.75%. A sector borrowing for a building takes the largest bond whose all-in cost is no dearer than the loan and borrows the rest; the first reading let bonds raise what a capital-short bank had refused, at coupons up to 16%, and failed the bank more, so loan and bond together never pass what the bank would lend. A sector short in the month can be sold a bond by the shortfall desk the same way.

Every bond trades on a book of its own, and the buyers are the ones Jerus named, each by the rule it already had for the nearest thing it holds: the household cells by their rule for the city's paper, wanting a share of each issue that rises with its expected return over what savers are paid; companies that owe nothing by the rule that sends idle cash abroad, never into their own bonds; the world by hot money's; the bank only on the capital it holds over its target, at the yield that makes the bond earn what an equal loan would; and the city's fund. A household short of money sells its bonds after its dollars abroad and before its shares. A bond that falls due is paid from its issuer's till to every holder at face. When a sector's firms default the bondholders lose too, and since 0.7.12 each instrument recovers what it recovers in the world: 75% of a bank loan and 45% of a bond (LOAN_RECOVERY, BOND_RECOVERY, the midpoints of Moody's and S&P's long-run averages), and the eight seeds measured 74.8% and 44.9%. On the default seed on 28 September 2,266 bonds were issued for $71.2bn over the run; 44 were outstanding at the end, $2.36bn, the households holding $1.29bn of it, other companies $914M and the world $126M. On 1 October 1,857 were issued for $62.8bn, and 5 were outstanding at the end, $183M, the households holding $40.3M of it; on 5 October, at 0.7.49, 2,834 were issued for $11.2T, and 151 were outstanding at the end, $1.81T, the households holding $899bn of it, other companies $761bn and the world $155bn.

The player does not trade, bar the fund's hand, which since 0.7.39 has a broker's pages: the fund's Search, every listed company and every bond outstanding as the player types, and a page a security — its price chart, a share's split-adjusted with fair value and the fund's average cost as lines and its orders as flags, a bond's the cash it still pays, a column a year; KEY STATS; THE BOOK; its record; YOUR POSITION; and THE ORDER TICKET, an order chosen, then reviewed, then placed. A company's owners are a card since 0.7.30, ITS OWNERS on its Balance sheet page under Sectors, which the Bank's Capital & owners page draws too: who holds it as a bar — the city's households, abroad, the bank's desk — then LAST TRADE and its month, or fair value where there has been none, FAIR VALUE, YIELD, MARKET CAP, TRADED, DIVIDEND, BOOK and BOUGHT BACK, with the order book's five levels a side behind details in plain figures and the share chart beside them, and the company's regime, payout rule and what it has raised and paid behind its (i). There is a price chart per company, each household cell's shares at the last trade and its bonds, the foreign share of each register on its owners card and the city's shares in foreign hands on Trade, and under Finances a bond market with every issue, who holds it and each bond's own book, drawn ten levels a side since 0.7.32 with the tables twelve deep behind details.

11

Labour & schools

A wage used to be one of six constants, copied in once at startup. Labour is a market now, skill is an axis, and a school is the only way to make a doctor at home.

Four bands, one price each

NONE < DIPLOMA < COLLEGE < UNIVERSITY // substitution runs downward only tightness = posts / qualified supply // per band, at the fill that gives every worker the best-paid post they qualify for (0.7.18) target = minimumWage × ratio[job] × clamp(tightness0.5, 0.70, 4.0) wage += (target − wage) × 0.12 // 90% of a shortage prices in over 18 months

The minimum wage is the base of the whole ladder, so one Policy dial moves every wage in the city, the treasury's own payroll included. Because the unskilled base is the floor, an unskilled glut cannot be priced away — it clears in people instead, which is exactly what a binding minimum wage does. The dial is set in founding money and met in today's: the floor is the dial times the cost of living times the player's nudge (LabourMarket.cashMinimumWage()), which Policy's THE FLOOR and the drawer's Min wage print since 0.7.36 and the People screen's WORK card since 0.7.38; City History's Minimum wage, founding money and the year book's column are the dial itself, because the history keeps neither the cost of living nor the nudge as a series. Since 0.7.36 Policy's Wages page draws the eleven jobs under their bands against that floor, each band's chip off isPinned(), and a staged floor's ghosts where each wage would head (targetWageAt()); and since 0.7.47 a band reads as pinned against the floor in today's money, not the founding one, which is what lets a glut at the indexed floor leave (migration). Wages also chase the cost of living in full, both directions. Until 0.7.42 that was a twenty-fourth of the gap to the price index a month, about two years; since then, once the basket is based, half of a wage's indexing is the inflation people expect and the other half chases the index at a forty-eighth of the gap (EXPECTED_SHARE, DRIFT_PER_MONTH), so the pass-through is still one, with no overshoot, and the tightness curve above stays the Phillips part. A harness has held them to it since 0.6.11: LabourCheck runs steady inflation and asserts every month that the wage index moves by the expected month to EXPECTED_SHARE and by the chase to the rest — one indexation, not two — and sits inside the lag's window, through a currency reform and a reload. When it went in, every one of 144 checkpoint readings on all eight seeds sat at the level the two-year lag implied, and the one-third overshoot measured on 15 September does not reproduce. Students and prisoners are out of the supply while they are away, and the pool is what is left when filled posts are taken off the labour force — an identity, not a second count.

Gated jobs carry a licence premium on top of their band's: posts over licence holders, same elasticity, never below one, the pair capped at 4×. A hospital with no medical school pays its doctors over the graduate band — measured at 1.26× against a band in glut at 0.83× — and that price is what draws licensed arrivals and sends graduates back to school.

Workers take the best-paid job they qualify for (0.7.18; PopulationManager.fillByBand()). Jerus: "Workers take the best-paid job they qualify for, instead of skilled jobs being filled first. Unskilled pay could then never pass diploma pay, but hospitals and schools would lose some staff." The bands used to be filled from the top down, with only a band's surplus passed down the ladder, so a city short of hands put the whole shortage on its bottom rung whatever that rung paid: in his year-149 city every one of 185,132 missing workers was an empty unskilled post, 8.5% staffed at $68.6k a month, more than a doctor's $60.4k, while diploma holders filled diploma posts at $25.5k. The rule people follow is occupational choice with skill downgrading — take the best-paid work you are qualified for, and when the lower rung pays as well the better-qualified move down it and push the shortage up (Beaudry, Green and Sand 2016, Journal of Labor Economics 34(S1)); no number is taken from it, only the rule. A worker may hold any post at or below their band. The bands are filled from the top as before, and a band that still has empty posts and would pay more than the band above it is joined to it, one market at one wage, until nobody holds a lower-paid post while a better-paid one they could take stands empty; the allocator, the wage, migration's chance of work and the planners' spare workers all read the one fill. How many are employed does not change, only which posts stand empty, and where the wages already fall with the ladder nothing is joined. In his city, run on for 120 months, 62,132 graduates took unskilled posts in the first month, the unskilled rung went from 8.5% to 39.3% staffed in that month, its empty posts at the end from 29,110 to 2,106, the unskilled and diploma wages became one at $17.41k, and the city's healthcare went from 91.5% to 99.4% staffed (0.7.18). The default cities carry unemployment, so it rarely binds in them; a default city is short of college workers all its life, at a median college fill of 0.57, and only a school or a dearer college wage brings them.

Nine schools, and a pipeline

StageTakesProducesCourse
Elementary, Middle, Highchildren and teensa diploma, at the narrowest stage's coverage4 + 3 + 5 yr
Community Collegediploma-holderscollege graduates24 mo
Universitydiploma-holdersuniversity graduates48 mo
Medical, Law, Business, Technologyuniversity graduatesa licence for one job type — raises nobody's band84 / 36 / 24 / 48 mo

Three things make an adult study, multiplied not averaged: a free seat, a return (nobody spends four years to earn what they already earn — a medical student's alternative is the best ungated university job), and the money, with tuition a Policy dial between the treasury and the household. Students are in flight: cohorts queue by months remaining, graduate at the front, thin at the adults' own attrition, and are out of the labour supply until they finish — living on savings, a grant worth $525 a month in founding money at the default dial, and since 0.7.19 kept up with prices — since 0.7.42 at the price level people expect — and a loan they begin repaying over 114 months the month they graduate. A university built today is graduates in month 49, not this month. Migration can still bring a licensed professional — some share of arriving graduates already hold one — so a small city imports its doctor and a city of eighty thousand cannot import two hundred at any price. That is the handoff to the schools, by arithmetic rather than by rule.

The school leavers' diplomas are a read of their own since 0.7.28 (Education.getNewDiplomas(), gross, not recorded yet after a load), because the DIPLOMA band's movement nets off every college and university finisher and can read −18 in a month a high school handed diplomas out. And since 0.7.46 the history's Graduates are the month's gains at every level (Education.gainedThisMonth()), where the series had summed movements whose every +1 has its −1 at the band left, and recorded nothing but rounding.

A school above the ladder is asked for the students the city would get and hire since 0.7.51 (CityNeeds.wanted()). Jerus: "universities and education other than elem middl and high schools tend to be overstated and way too early sometimes". Its NEEDS YOU row had read who would come if seats were free — those eligible, times who is willing and the enrolment rate, times the course — and nothing asked whether the city had work for the graduates: a founded city of 118 people with five university posts was told to build a $417M university for 33, and Jerus's city has 11,583 university seats with nobody in them and 1,043 lawyers for 75 law posts.
