The row now wants the smaller of who would come and the posts the degree fills (CityNeeds.hires()): the posts of the band it qualifies for, standing and on site, less the people already qualified for them, plus the posts their holders leave as they age out over one course — for a professional school its licence's posts and the licensed. In the 2,400-month city the college's row reads 0 seats, 1,618 would come and be hired, and the university, medical and law rows that read 24,395, 381 and 162 who would come are gone. Under a class of 25 there is no row, as before, and a first school of its kind is listed only once those would fill half the smallest that teaches it (CityNeeds.FIRST_SCHOOL_SHARE, the businesses' first-plant rule). Build sizes an order for one ahead (the screen), counting the feeder's graduates by the time it opens: the high schools' diplomas for a college or a university, read off saved state (Education.schoolLeavers(), the month's own arithmetic, which a load does not lose as it loses getNewDiplomas()), and the university's cohorts in flight for a professional school. Services › Education's school not built says both sides: that city's university reads not built: 24,395 would come, 133 posts for them.

The price of a place

Jerus: "grants its just a menu where you can choose between a fixed amount, or a percentage of last month's surplus, or a % as it is now of living costs, or a % of tuition. and then another slider which is the interest rate for the student loans … and also make it so that you can tweak the price of tuition as well." Since 0.6.9 the Policy tab's Promises → Schools page — the Tuition page renamed — has carried the students' dials together: on one foot bar, five when it opened and since 0.7.6 a price for each kind of school besides, and since 0.7.36 in the staged tray at the foot of the stage (setting them). The grant is a basis and an amount (TaxPolicy.GrantBasis): a share of the unskilled wage, which was the founding rule and the default until 0.7.19, and which is what "as it is now of living costs" turned out to be; a fixed sum a student, which since 0.7.19 is a real amount kept up with prices and the default for a new city; a share of last month's surplus, as one pool; or a share of each student's own course fee. Each basis has its own ceiling, and all four are struck by one rule, TaxPolicy.grantBill, that the treasury's bill, the save's re-strike, the students' row and the page all call, so a grant on the wrong thing cannot look right on one screen and wrong on another. Since 0.7.19 the grant follows prices (Jerus: "Grant follows prices"): a new city's is $525 a month in founding money times the month's price index until 0.7.42, and since then times the expected price level the month's constants are struck at (TaxPolicy.DEFAULT_FIXED_GRANT, at founding the old default's bill to the cent); a save keeps the basis it was played on ("Old saves keep theirs"), and an older save's fixed amount is read as the real amount that pays the same at the load month's index. The wage share had been paying his year-149 city $1,425M a month, $9.72k a student; on the new basis it would have paid $415M, $2.83k a student. The student loan has a rate, 0 to 15%, nothing by default: the Canadian shape, nothing accruing while they study, charged on a graduate's balance and paid with the instalment, so the balance is only ever principal and falls exactly as it did. The interest is the treasury's own revenue line, Student loan interest; the principal still comes back through the treasury's journal. A prisoner's loan freezes with the rest of their debts, no instalment and no interest. And the price of a place is the player's: tuitionScale, 0 to 5× the founding table, read wherever a fee is read.

Since 0.7.6 it is a price per school. Jerus: "have it so that not only can you raise prices but also raise the price for a specific university, and beside the dial it shows the current space, the current students, and the current cost and revenue." The scale is nine, one for each kind of school (TaxPolicy.tuitionScaleOf(type)), in the shape 0.7.4 gave the income taxes: Every school at once moves all nine, and taking it back unstages every kind's own; each kind has its own dial and four figures off the model — the places its buildings seat, the students in it, what its staff and buildings cost the treasury this month, and the tuition its students paid. Since 0.7.36 the page leads with WHO CAN AFFORD A PLACE, a row a course — a place's fee after the city's share against the best wage of whoever can take it, red at Education.MAX_BURDEN, 60% — each row opening into its own price; then WHO PAYS FOR SCHOOL; THE SCHOOLS THIS MONTH, which foots now, staff and buildings less what households paid being the net cost and the tuition the city waives in grey outside the sum; and WHAT A PLACE IS PRICED AT; then the grant and the loan. A kind with nothing standing says no school and its dial is greyed. The nine ride the end of the policy array, and a shorter array reads them as the one scale it carried. Building it found the trap 0.7.4 had found for the income tax: the month and the load path told every school the first kind's price, which would have put nine prices back to one on every load, and Game.tellTheSchoolsTheirPrices hands each kind its own now.

At the defaults nothing moves, bit for bit. At ×3 the poverty trap the Education header describes is back: a one-university city at five years has 1,718 students and 69% of its diploma-holders willing at ×1, 1,173 and 26% at ×3, and 1,756 and 90% at ×0. The default playtest has never built a school, so -Dplaytest.schools=true builds them, and eight seeds at tuition ×3, the grant half of tuition and loans at 5% all ran clean, the treasury taking $1.1–3.8M a month of interest and fewer people studying. On the way the schools' books were found never to have been restored on the load path — the sixth sighting of that gap, fixed.

12

Tax, policy & GDP

Four base rates since 0.7.4 — on profit, on sales, on wages and on property — and everything else is an offset in rate points from one of them. Resolution happens in one place, clamped, so no screen can quote a rate the city does not collect.

LeverDefaultMaxOffsets
Profit tax15%60%fifteen sectors; the bank is charged retail's rate
Sales tax15%60%fifteen sectors
Wage tax15%60%four education wage bands
Property tax1.5%/yr10%/yrseventeen sectors, each held within ten points either way since 0.7.47, where the others' offsets go to thirty, plus farmland relief; quoted annually, charged monthly

Beside them: the minimum wage, the policy interest rate, tuition's split and since 0.6.9 its price, the pension contribution and replacement rates, the EI premium and benefit rates, the students' grant on its basis and the student loan's rate, since 0.6.8 the health fee's scale and the health premium, since 16 September the transit fare, since 0.7.14 the city's fund's dial, since 0.7.43 food assistance, and since 0.7.48 the fund's withdrawal — each a dial with a stated maximum. The rollover and what happens when the bank fails are the treasury's settings, on Finances (the city's borrowing, the bank), and so are the fund's two dials, the withdrawal leading its Rules & cash page. The fare is a price to a car owner and a charge to everybody else (0.7.49): an owner in reach of a line weighs it against a journey's fuel, and a commuter with no car of their own rides at any fare where a line reaches and a seat is free (transport). It is set per ride, $2.50 by default and $50 at the absurd end, and charged forty journeys a month, because a rider is a person and for its first day it was charged once a month against a constant that says a single journey — Jerus caught it by reading the number. Since 0.7.42 the dial is real, set at founding prices and charged at the level the month's constants are struck at, and since 0.7.47 a currency reform divides its cap by the new unit, so a reformed city's ladder still ends where a founding one's does.

The policy rate reaches 100% since 0.7.2 (DebtManager.MAX_POLICY_RATE 1.00; it stopped at 25% before), a bound that never binds in play and is there so a typo cannot set 2,500%. The slider has chips at 0, 1, 2, 3, 5, 8, 10, 15, 20, 30, 50, 75 and 100. The Taylor rule beside it is printed as the rule says it (ruleRate()), and where the dial's bounds cut it the page says so: "the rule would set −4.60%, the dial stops at 0.00%" was the default seed's last month on 22 September, on −3.1% inflation, on 28 September it read −0.35% on −0.2%, and on 1 October −0.33% on −0.2%; on 5 October, at 0.7.49, the rule asked 2.33% on 1.6% inflation, inside the dial's bounds, so the page had nothing to add. Since 0.7.0 the dial is the price of money, and it has three companions on the same page: the target the rule aims at, a dial since 0.7.4, the holdings dial and the advances ceiling; all four are told in the central bank.

Setting them: four areas, one proposal

The Policy tab is a hub since 0.7.36: four area cards — Taxes, Wages, Money and Promises — each a door with its figure, a small picture and a line, over WHAT IS BITING, alert cards with the month one first bites marked new and a door to where it is undone, and RECENT DECISIONS, the last five tax, promise, central bank and currency decisions. The areas are chips in the head and their pages tabs, and four figures stand over every page, each a door: TAX A MONTH, THE FLOOR in today's money, THE POLICY RATE, and PROMISES, which since 0.7.36 leaves out the tuition the city waives, revenue forgone rather than money out. Promises has six pages since 0.7.45, when food assistance got one of its own: Pensions, Out of work, Food, Health, Schools and Subsidies.

"Income tax" is not one tax. It is three — on profit, on value added, and on every payroll — and until 0.7.4 the three shared one dial, and the screens used to be split by where the modifier lived rather than by what the money is. They are split by the tax now: Everything · Profit · Sales · Wage · Property. Jerus: "sure you can choose sale tax for manufacturing, but what about just increasing sale tax for all at the same time? currently that's a hassle, i want to be able to do that for every type of tax, even wage tax." Since 0.7.4 each of the three has a base of its own, every offset rides its own tax's base, and each tax page's top dial is that base; the old city rate is Every tax at once on the Everything page, which moves all three and shows the three side by side — since 0.7.36 as three chips, the thumb on the profit rate with three named ticks, and three rates in grey once they part. Splitting them found the load path reading the old single rate after the policy array, which would have put a split back together on every load, and the Government tab printing the bank's profit tax at the income rate while the city charged it retail's.

[−] ══════●══════ [+] 20.25% 0.00% to 60.00% · one step is 0.25 points · it is 20.00%

Drag to cross the range; it snaps hard on release, and each button is worth exactly one step. The step is a quarter point everywhere except property, which gets a twentieth — the same step has a very different bite: 20.00% to 20.25% of income is one part in eighty of what income tax raises, 1.00% to 1.25% of assessed value is one part in four.

Since 0.7.6 it is every dial's shape, not the tax pages' own. Jerus: "all the dials in the policy in the promises section, make them a slider with steps, and a + − on the ends, basically i think it's better if you create an object or class of slider". There had been three shapes of one control, and there is one class now, ui/Ladder: a minus, a slider that snaps to the step, a plus, the reading in the accent colour when it is off what the city charges, and an ends line — x to y · one step is z · it is w. The sensitivity is the step and nothing else, because a drag coarser than a click would be two dials in one. The reading follows the thumb and the change waits for the release, because restaging on every pixel redraws the screen under the pointer. On it are every tax dial and the farmland relief, the wage floor and the policy rate, the target, the holdings and the ceiling, which apply at once and keep their chips as shortcuts, the pension's two, EI's two, both health dials, the tuition split, every school's price, the grant and the loan, and the fare, on Infrastructure › Transit since 0.7.28; each kept its key, range, step and formatter exactly, and PolicyCheck did not notice. The standing subsidies are switches and the currency reform is a choice of three, so neither is a ladder.

Since 0.7.36 every dial is a dial card (Levers; the fare's since 0.7.38): its paragraph behind its name's (i), its reading, the ladder — with named marks under the track, the three income rates once parted, the rule's rate, the clinic's break-even, and words where a reading would mislead (Ladder.idle()) — and beside it WHAT IT WOULD DO, each effect before → after over a pair of bars, following the thumb while it is dragged (Ladder.onTrack()), the change still waiting for the release, under one grey line: Struck on this month's books; behaviour is not projected. Each tax page leads with its base on a dial card and WHO PAYS as ranked rows, the bank a profit payer taxed at Retail's rate in arrears, each row opening into its own offset's card.

Changes stage and apply together. A budget is not one rate moved in isolation, so several dials can be moved and applied as one proposal. On the taxes' pages and Schools they stage, since 0.7.36, into a tray at the foot of the stage — a chip a change with its ×, THE BUDGET before → after, and Apply all N or Discard — where until then a foot bar listed them in the order they were made, and a dial dragged back to the live value unstages rather than staging a no-op. Each dial registers its own setter, which is what lets one press apply a city rate, three sector offsets and the farmland relief without a switch on the key. THE BUDGET is the model's, not the screen's (PolicyPreview, 0.7.36): last month's balance plus every line's move — the four taxes and the bank's, which is next month's because it is taxed in arrears, the contributions, both premiums, pensions, EI on the pool, the care fees, tuition, the grant and the loans' interest — each its owner's read of a detached copy of the policy (TaxPolicy.copy()), so a dial at zero previews something and the screen adds nothing up, and PolicyPreviewCheck holds that, applied, the preview is the model: a month on, the EI bill and the bank's tax charged are the previews to the cent. It strikes the same arithmetic twice — once at what the city charges, once at what is staged — and says plainly that both figures are models: comparing a model against last month's actual would make a batch that changed nothing read as a swing. The difference is the answer. Since 0.7.23 each dial a press applies writes a line of its own to the city's decision log at that month, so a proposal of five dials is one flag of five on City History, and a dial set to what it already reads writes nothing (the screen).

The wage page totals what a payslip actually loses — wage tax plus the pension contribution plus the EI premium, and since 0.6.8 the health premium — which no screen in this game had ever added up; since 0.7.36 it is WHAT A PAYSLIP LOSES, a bar of the wage in four parts at the wage rate, each band moved off it with its own total (TaxPolicy.payslipShare()). The premiums and the contribution are read-only there, with a door to Promises, because a second copy of a lever is how two screens start disagreeing about what the city charges.

What the treasury actually did

Earned, surplus, banked (0.7.31). Jerus: "the money one has is barely visible to see as well as ones income", and in his city the line under the cash said +$1.5B a month while the cash grew $2.3B. Three figures, all of them right, are named apart everywhere since: EARNED, the header's — the tax take less the running programmes plus the utilities' net, at today's dials (Game.getIncome()); the budget's SURPLUS; and what the cash BANKED. The Government Overview's FROM EARNED TO BANKED walks the road between them as three tiles with every step named and each a door to where it is decided: from EARNED to the budget, eleven steps since 0.7.49 (Game.getEarnedToBudget()) — land sold and bought, buildings, the fund's transfer, the mortgage insurance's premiums and claims, subsidies, the food vouchers, the central bank's remittance and its interest, and the students' loan interest — with Today's dials, not the month's for what a dial moved between the presses leaves; from the budget to the cash, the paper raised and repaid, every line of the journal below by name, and Not accounted for. The fares were a twelfth step until 0.7.49, in EARNED and the cash and not in the budget. The old statement is behind details.

The Government tab has a bridge from the budget balance — an income measure — to what the treasury's cash actually did between one press and the next, and until 19 September its last row, Everything else the treasury did, was a residual with a name on it: land, buildings, reserves, capital put into the bank, bonds bought back, on any month the player did one of them. Jerus: "it just says 'everything else' — that should be expandable, cause a lot of times that's where a bunch of important things happen." It opened, and since 0.7.31 each of its lines is a step of the bridge. TreasuryJournal records every movement of the city's cash that is neither a budget line nor paper raised or repaid, where it happens and in the player's words — Put capital into the bank, Bought reserves, Bought back a bond, Lent to students, net of repayments, since 0.7.0 Repaid the central bank and Advanced by the central bank (printed), since 0.7.6 land bought out of the vault, since 0.7.14 what the dial paid into the fund and what resolving the bank cost, and since 0.7.22 Sold a demolition's material to the builders — press to press, for the month that ended and the one in progress, both carried in the save. What the lines do not explain prints under them as Not accounted for, and on a month the city borrows it is the first coupon's timing, to the cent. Land and buildings stay on the budget's own line and are deliberately not named twice. Opening the row found that Raised by issuing paper had read $0 on every month the city borrowed, because its counter was cleared before the strike; that the city's own repair bill and the transit fares were on the Government screen and not in the budget's totals — since 0.7.31 neither has been drawn as a budget line (the repairs had made Spending's key add to 111%), the repairs are named under the totals as outside the budget's total and on the bridge, and since 0.7.49 the fares are a budget line of their own, Transit fares, beside Transit, the bill the treasury has paid since then; and, not fixed that night, that the bank had never paid for the city's paper (see debt). The budget gained its own lines in the batches since — the health premium, student loan interest, the central bank's remittance and the interest on its advances, a Subsidies line the Government tab had never had, since 0.7.11 the premiums the landlords pay to insure their mortgages under revenue and the claims the city pays on them under spending, since 0.7.14 Transfer from the fund, since 0.7.43 Food assistance, and since 0.7.49 Transit fares and Transit; since 0.7.19 Material escalation on the city's building contracts and since 0.7.22 Overtime on the city's rushed sites, both discretionary and owed to the builders as arrears past the ceiling, with a cancelled order's refund netted against the capital spending — and since 0.7.6 Land bought opens into what was converted, what came out of the vault and what was bought back. And since 0.7.0 every payment the treasury makes goes through one door, Game.treasuryPays(TreasuryLine, amount), which is what lets the central bank's ceiling decide who is paid.

VAT with input credits

Sales tax is a real value-added tax: exports zero-rated with credits still claimable, imports charged at the buyer's rate and fully creditable, the city exempt both ways, residential rent an exempt supply. One deliberate departure from the real thing — the rate follows the producer, not the product — because under a product rate most of the sector dials would be cosmetic.

And the builders' tax is in their quote since 0.7.19 (the builders), so who finally bears it is the real rules'. A business that makes taxable supplies claims the tax on its buildings back as the work is billed, and on its repairs as an input (CRA RC4022; Excise Tax Act ss. 169(1) and 123(1)). The landlords, whose rent is an exempt supply, claim no credit, but get the tax on a new rental home back by a rebate: all of it on purpose-built rental housing, a building of four homes or more held for rent (RC4231; s. 256.2(3.1)), which the Studio and the Low-Rise Apartments are; and on any other new rental home the new residential rental property rebate, 36% of the tax and at most $6,300 a unit, phased out between $350,000 and $450,000 of value, those figures in founding money at the price level the month's constants are struck at (the month's price index until 0.7.42), so a House, about $454k with its plot at founding, gets about nothing. No rebate reaches a repair. The city's own rebate (RC4034) is the tax coming home, because the city is what collects it, so it has no line; and the bank's branch, an exempt financial service, bears it. Jerus chose "Businesses claim it back", then, after the first measurement, "Both rebates", and on the landlords' repairs still being taxed, "Leave as the law has it". In his year-149 city the landlords' rebate came to $20.13B over the decade, 15.1% of what they built (0.7.19).

Net remittance is allowed to go negative and is not floored: under producer rates a sector buying high and selling low genuinely is owed money, and flooring would quietly turn a rate cut into a partial one. When it happens the screen names who is owed. And the producer pays it — out of its own cash, as it pays the profit tax on its statement's after-tax line. Both used to be collected by the treasury from nobody; closing the money identity found them.

GDP

Measured by expenditure, C + I + G + NX, replacing an income-approach figure that went negative the moment firms started paying interest. Profit is not production. Since 0.7.20 a ratio to it says of GDP, annualised while fewer than twelve months are recorded and of annual GDP after. The screens had already scaled a young city's months to a year; only the words changed, and the Dashboard, which had summed the months there were and called them a year. A founding city's revenue really can be 169.7% of its annualised GDP, as the play-through of 0.7.19 read it in month 7: its revenue is the builders' sales tax on its own orders, large against an economy that is mostly those orders. Since 0.7.31 a loaded city's year is its own at once: every load had reset the record to one month, so a city of centuries read a month scaled up for a year after each load (36.4% for 34.4%; Months recorded 1; the header's GDP tile $0 / yr · first year), and the load path puts the last ten years back from the graph history now (NationalAccounts.seedHistory()). And since 0.7.47 the Government screen's of GDP and TAX TAKE read each line's own last twelve months against the year's output, where a busy month's spending times twelve had read 129.81% of annual GDP; health and schools keep the month times twelve, because the history records their net cost and the rows are gross.

C what the households bought over three counters — the grocers', the boutiques', the kitchens' — and since 0.7.62 the refiners' fuel, + rent paid I construction work put in place + the change in inventory as a VOLUME, priced today: the food shelf at fixed weights, the makers' materials, the boutiques' stockroom, and since 0.7.58 every other good a sector holds at what one costs to bring in G utility payroll + the health service's gross cost + the police's + the schools' + transit's bill // the last two since 0.7.49 NX everything sold abroad − everything landed: scrap, food, materials, machinery, vans, watches, crude, and the drivers' and the railway's fuel

Inventory is measured as a volume rather than a value, because valuing it books every price move as production — a rule that had to be re-learned the day food became thirteen goods, since the obvious way to add kilograms of grain to kilograms of fish is to value each at its own price. The thirteen are aggregated at fixed weights instead, so the figure is still a quantity. The builders' yard is deliberately excluded from the inventory term — work in progress is at contract value and already embodies it, so counting both subtracted the same brick twice — while the materials plant's warehouse is output made and not yet sold, and counts like a mill's. The boutiques' stockroom is the third term, and it was found by a negative month: a Boutique's three months of stock, 960 pieces at $9,000, lands in one month, NX took the landed cost out and nothing said the city still had the goods, so a city that took up shopping reported negative GDP sixty times over a run. Priced at what the shop paid, the arrival nets to zero and the margin is the only thing that scores; sixty negative months became one across eight seeds and 32,040 months. Any good a sector can hold and does not consume within the month belongs in that block the day the good is written. It was learned again in 0.7.58: an ensemble month read GDP of −16,056 because a new railway had bought its twenty sets of rolling stock abroad, 90,460, and nothing said the city still had them, and the farms' crops and every sector's vans were the same gap. Since then every other good a sector holds is the fifth term (NationalAccounts.HELD), its change in units priced at what one costs to bring in, so a fleet bought abroad nets to nothing and its wear reads as use; the refiners' fuel joined it in 0.7.62. The households' cars are named and left out, for the reason below. A household's car is not in C: a household turning money into a thing it owns is a portfolio move, like a share. Land sales appear in the government's books and nowhere in GDP: selling a field moves an asset that already existed, and counting it would let a city manufacture growth by trading land with itself. Education's cost was outside G until 0.7.49, which healthcare's and the police's were not, and the transit payroll joined it on the outside when the modes arrived — paid, it turned out, by nobody. Both are in G since, at what they cost, as a service with no market price is valued; G is struck at the top of the month from the costs the month before set, so they come in a month behind.

And since 0.7.6 it can be drawn in its layers. Jerus: "have it so the gdp graph can be a toggle, and if toggled it switches from line to mountain graph is it? layered, aka showing how much is made up of investments, net exports, government spending, aka breaking it down." The four parts are series of their own — consumption, investment, government, netExports, each with its rule in the year book and deflated the way real GDP is — and a layers chip on City History, the Reports page until 0.7.21, stacks C, I and G from zero with the real GDP line drawn over them, so the gap between the top of the stack and the line is net exports, above it when the city exports more than it lands and below it when not. A stacked area cannot hold a negative layer, and the caption says so. HistoryCheck asserts that the four add up to the month's GDP to the cent. Since 0.7.23 the layers are drawn on City History's new chart, with the rest of its lines (the screen), and since 0.7.37 the four parts are lines of their own on its picker, GDP: consumption to GDP: net exports, a month each in the money of the day.

Standing subsidies

Any sector can be put under a standing policy that covers its losses exactly, uncapped, month after month. It is booked as a capital contribution, not revenue — the sector's cash rises and its income statement still says it lost money. The city goes overdrawn to pay it, and the overdraft — the central bank's advances since 0.7.0 — is priced as debt, so an over-generous policy bills you through the interest rate; and once the advances have reached their ceiling a subsidy is paid only from cash at or above zero, and what it is refused is owed to the sector as arrears.

The city's fund

Jerus: "Rule plus your hand", and "Home only". Since 0.7.14 the treasury has a fund (TreasuryFund), a pool of its own inside the city: a market book of company shares, the bank's among them, and company bonds; a rescue book of a resolved bank's shares, the city's preferred and its warrants (the bank); and its cash. Its holdings live where everyone else's do, as a city holding on each company's register and a city holder on every bond, and it posts on both books by a rule and, separately, by the player's hand. It never subscribes to a new issue and never buys the city's own paper.

The dial takes 0 to 300% of the year's surplus, nothing by default, paid in once a year at December's close and before the rollover nets the same surplus, so that the two cannot spend it twice; a deficit year saves nothing, and past 100% the rest comes out of the treasury's cash but never takes it below a month of its own spending, the liquidity rule Canada keeps and the rollover already reads. Yearly, because a monthly pay-in would save in the good months of a deficit year. The rule is Norway's Government Pension Fund Global: 70% shares and 30% bonds, never more than 10% of any company — a cap that since 0.7.39 stops a hand buy too, at posting, counting every order the fund has on the company as if it filled — rebalanced when shares pass 74% or fall more than four points under 70%, asking at fair value and, since 0.7.48, bidding for a share at the desk's ask, one per cent over it, where at fair value it met nobody; money that does not fit waits as cash, which earns nothing, as the treasury's does. The withdrawal is Norway's fiscal rule since 2017 by default: 3% a year of everything it holds, a twelfth a month, to the budget as Transfer from the fund, from its cash only — the rule never sells to pay it. Since 0.7.48 it is a dial (Jerus: "the city fund, you should be able to click how much to withdraw automatically, even 0 or 10% a month"): 0 to 10% a month in quarter-point steps, one step the default and Norway's rule to the bit (TreasuryFund.WITHDRAWAL_STEP). Over the default the dial spends the fund: what its cash cannot cover is sold from its market book, never the rescue book, pro rata at the step, and paid at the next month's top, and the rule buys nothing meanwhile. It is a full-width card at the top of Rules & cash, with next month's effects and a year on (PolicyPreview.fundWithdrawalAt()). At nothing the default playtest ended 13% smaller as the dial went in, 166,409 people against 191,012, because the transfer is real revenue in that city; at 10% the 2,400-month research city's market book went from D$149.7M to D$16.0M in eight months. The hand places orders at fair value or, since 0.7.39, at a price the player names, good for a month and cancellable before the step, a buy's money held from the rule's bids from the moment it is placed; it may sell any share or bond the fund holds, the rescue book's bank shares included, though not the preferred or its warrants, and each order is a line in the decision log, Order: buy …, its fills and lapses on Activity. Paying in and drawing out are transfers between two government pools and stay off the budget. The fund has its own area under Finances, The city's fund, in a broker's shape since 0.7.39: Portfolio — its worth, its return over the chart's window by Modified Dietz and since it began from its own counters, a chart of its worth against what was put in less taken out, its holdings a row a lot with their average cost and unrealized P&L, and since it began by kind; Search; Activity, every trade, payment and event, newest first; Rules & cash, the withdrawal, the dial, the rescue book's terms, paying in and drawing out, and the orders waiting; and a page a security with YOUR POSITION beside THE ORDER TICKET (the exchange).

What each holding cost is kept since 0.7.39 (FundLedger, FundView), by Canada's adjusted cost base and the average-cost method: one lot a company's market book, the rule's buys and the hand's pooled; one for the rescue book; one a bond; and the income apart. The hand's sale takes the market lot first. A sale realizes its proceeds less the cost it takes out, a maturity its face less cost, a write-down the cost in the share of face it lost, and a rescue the market lot's whole cost, as a loss, since the fund's own bank shares go with everyone's; a split leaves the cost and divides the average. It is bookkeeping only — the default playtest's traces did not move — and an older save is counted at market value in the month it loads, tagged cost from. P&L is green and red, a verdict, and nothing else on the fund's pages is.

At the default dial of nothing the default seed on 28 September still ended with a fund of $116M, fed by the dividends on the bank shares its one resolution had handed it ($239M of the $270M of dividends it took), and paid the budget $659M over the run; on 1 October it ended worth $724M, fed by the dividends on its rescue book ($2.61bn of the $2.86bn of dividends it took), and had paid the budget $3.63bn, $435M short of the rule's 3% because the transfer comes from its cash alone. On eight seeds at a dial of 100% it was paid $15.3T and held about 94% of it as cash, and the cities ended at 174,264 people on average against 166,717 at nothing (0.7.14). On 5 October, at 0.7.49, the default seed's fund ended worth $287.9bn, $222.8bn of it the rescue book from eight resolutions, and had paid the budget $302.8bn at the default withdrawal, $646M short of it; its ledger had realized −$14.1M and stood $24.3bn up on a cost of $225.4bn, its identity off in its worst month by 4.7e-16 of what it held.

13

The city's borrowing

Three instruments with genuinely different shapes, priced off their own cash flows rather than off a formula that assumed a bullet — and since 0.7.1 all three off one curve that sits on the policy dial, held by whoever bought them: the households, the central bank, and the bank for the rest. Since 0.7.13 the treasury can roll what falls due without the player's hand. What lends to everybody else is the bank.

InstrumentDurationGranularityShape
T-Bill3–12 months$1Mzero coupon, sold at a discount, one lump at maturity; the short end of the curve, at the dial
Serial bond1–10 years$10Mmonthly coupon on a declining balance, annual principal slices
Term bond10, 20, 30, 40 or 50 years$100Mlow coupon on full face, whole face at maturity
Why the term bond is solved from present value

The old simple-interest premium made issue-then-repurchase an infinite money button — $2,145 of free cash at ten years, rising to $28,059 at thirty. Solving the face from the bond's present value makes the round trip neutral by construction rather than by a patch.

The dial, and the curve on it

The policy rate is the player's dial on the Policy tab, 0% to 100% since 0.7.2, and since 0.7.0 it is the price of money rather than a floor under somebody else's: the commercial bank's spare cash earns it at the central bank, and its shortfalls pay it and a quarter point at the window (the central bank). The city's own borrowing rate is that dial — never less than what the money costs the bank that buys the paper, plus a point, while the bank and the households hold all of it — walked up by the market's read of the city's books, so a dial at 3% and a debt-free treasury with a bank whose money costs it nothing is a city paying 3%. Until 0.7.7 the bank's strain added up to eighteen points on top of that, and it is gone (the bank). Since 0.7.15 the floor is split by who holds the paper, Jerus's reading of the backstop: "if bank is in a pickle, and central bank is at 100% then your debt isnt priced at tge bank, ira proportional". Until 0.7.0 it was the dial less a two-point discount, a named constant its own comment called the one number in the file that did not describe anything real; with it went a city borrowing below its own central bank's rate.

floor = max(MIN_RATE, policy + (1 − s) × max(0, the bank's cost of funds + MIN_MARGIN − policy)) // s: the central bank's share of all the city's paper, notes included, because the bank holds the notes

At s = 0 that is the floor 0.7.14 had, to the bit; at s = 1 the bank's state no longer touches the city's rate. Because the bank's money costs it almost nothing when it is flush, the floor is often the bank's point rather than the dial: on the default seed on 28 September the dial ended at 0.01% and the floor at 1.00%, and on 1 October the same.

Two independent measures move the rest — debt against annual GDP, and debt against annual tax revenue — each a linear ramp to five points of spread and then flat. The city's own overdraft counts as principal, because a city over a million in the red with no bonds was being quoted the floor, and since 0.7.0 so does what it owes its central bank in advances. The cheapest money the market will ever offer is 0.5%. Because face depends on rate and rate depends on face, every quote walks that fixed point six times. The quote is the arithmetic: what the screen shows is what gets booked, down to the rate before and after and the points the ask itself cost. A letter grade — AAA to CCC — reports the city's position within the market's own band rather than against typed-in thresholds. Since 0.7.14 a reloaded city's rate is the live city's to the bit: the debt market strikes it from four inputs, and one of them, the overdraft, used to be struck again on load from cash that had moved after the month's strike, up to a basis point away, so the save carries the four and the rate.

The curve. Jerus: "the short term rates, aka the one you choose, those should be basically the tbill rate, the others change just as in real life." Since 0.7.1 every price of the city's own paper — the quote, the issue, the buyback, the market value and the borrow pages, which since 0.7.32 draw it a column per term — is read off one function at the paper's maturity, DebtManager.priceAt(debt, months):

rate(months) = floor + credit spread + termPremium(months) − compression(months) termPremium nothing to a year · linear to the ten-year entry · linear between entries · flat past fifty years compression what the central bank's holdings take off it — see the central bank
The term premium, in points over the dial — DebtManager.TERM_PREMIUM_10Y … 50Y, provisional, Jerus's numbers to settle
Maturity10 yr20 yr30 yr40 yr50 yr
Premium0.500.901.151.351.50

A note carries no premium, so the short end is the floor and getRate() — what every screen means by the city's rate, the header's RATE tile's tooltip (the strip's city line until 0.7.21) and the year book's interestRate — stays exactly that; a five-year serial carries 48/108 of the ten-year entry. The credit spread is the same at every maturity, so a hike moves the whole curve in parallel and a city that over-borrows pays more at every point of it, and paper already on the books keeps its coupon and its months, so locking in fifty-year money before a hike is a real decision. Term loans are issued only at ten, twenty, thirty, forty or fifty years (LongTermBond.MATURITIES), at home and in dollars: any other term is refused in words — "Term loans are issued at 10, 20, 30, 40 or 50 years." — and Borrow's term columns, chips abroad, step by ten. Issue and buyback read the same function, so RestructureCheck's round trip is still neutral by construction, and CreditCheck's section 11 walks the rest: a debt-free note at exactly the dial, each maturity the dial plus its entry, the interpolation, a hike and a debt both parallel, a 25-year request refused and booking nothing, and a twenty-year bond bought back the same month for what it raised, bar the issuance cost.

The borrow pages draw it since 0.7.32. Borrow at home draws each term as a column of its rate, struck again for the amount once one is asked, and abroad its terms as chips with the world's rate in their tooltips; Your rate draws THE CURVE at ten maturities, at home and abroad, the dial dashed across it and the thirty-year point taken apart into the short end, the term premium and the central bank's compression, and its (i)s say that the floor is not the dial: the note is the floor plus the spreads. The book prices every piece at what a buyback would pay (DebtManager.marketValue()), a dollar piece on the world's curve; until 0.7.32 the Buy back page priced a dollar piece at the city's short rate, so a ten-year dollar loan read about par where a buyback paid 86.4, and ForeignDebtCheck holds the two equal now. The ask is typed since 0.7.40. Jerus, playing 0.7.39: "there should be an option to raise trillions or tens of trillions right now max is 1B", and of the two ways put to him, "Type it + scaling buttons". The box takes 40B, 2.5T, 750M or 12,000,000, with k, M, B and T in either case and a leading D$, $ or US$ ignored, and says a bad entry in red under it, keeping the old ask; ÷10 and ×10 show once there is an ask; the steps either side scale with it, one unit of its leading digit and a tenth of that, never under a lot; and the presets are the model's — the minimum issue, what falls due within a year, a month's spending, a year of tax and the cash shortfall, and abroad the minimum in dollars and what the dollar paper asks within a year, each shown only when it is above nothing. The ask is written in full and short, D$2,500,000,000,000 · D$2.5T. The model puts no cap on an issue's size, and none was added.

Issuance costs $12k fixed at founding prices, struck at the expected price level since 0.7.42 (Game.FIXED_ISSUE_COST; the anchor), plus a 0.75% underwriting spread (UNDERWRITING_SPREAD), taken off the proceeds and never rolled into the face — otherwise the bond would be worth more than it raised the instant it was signed. Buyback pays cash only and refuses to overdraw, because borrowing at the market rate to retire cheaper paper is strictly worse; since 0.7.1 it pays at the curve's rate for the months the bond has left, and it pays every holder its share, where until 0.7.0 it paid nobody at all and a dollar bond's price left the treasury for nowhere on the books. A city that runs out of cash no longer sells its bank an emergency note; since 0.7.0 its central bank advances the gap, up to a ceiling the player sets (the central bank). When the treasury cannot pay for an order, the Build screen offers two things: since 0.7.10 first a twenty-year bond (Game.BUILD_BOND_YEARS) whose face is solved so that the cash it brings covers the gap (Game.quoteLongBondForCash()), because a building outlives either loan, and then the six-month note it always offered (Game.BUILD_NOTE_MONTHS), each with its rate, face, cash, monthly cost, cost in all and what happens at the end; since 0.7.34 each ends in a button that says what it builds and on which paper, Build 3 · $37.5M over issues the 20-year bond, then builds. And since 0.7.40 the two are one page for the whole run, Build › Funding. Jerus, playing 0.7.39: "when you click build all and you dont have the credit it just builds one not all", and building on credit still opened the old page where land had the new one. Any Build press that costs more than the cash — a card's Build, the order bar, Enter, Build all three or a suggestion — opens it, in the land office's shape with Build lit on the rail: the run's price against the cash with a short by chip, and the two offers as cards side by side, each sized to the run's gap — its invoice, every order priced on the yard the orders before it leave (Game.buildRunInvoice()), less the cash — and each button naming the run, Build 5 · $X or Build 3 orders · $X. An offer books exactly its quote and then places the orders in turn. A run that would stop at an order short of ground, ore or licences borrows only for the orders before it and says so, because the city is never sold a loan for a building it has nowhere to put, and an order not placed stays on its card; BuildCardCheck's ninth section places a run and adds up its receipts, which come to the invoice to the bit. In a probe on 0.7.10, a Standard city buying its water plant on the note alone went D$16.2M overdrawn when the note fell due, and on the sized bond never went below D$0.53M and never drew an advance; NewGameCheck's section 11 asserts the sizing at every maturity.

Who holds the paper

Jerus: "yes households should be able to hold." Since 0.7.1 every domestic bond says who holds it — the principal held by the households and by the central bank, the bank whatever is left — and the split is what makes a bond market rather than a loan. At the settle after an issue the households go first, a fifth of it for every point it yields over the deposit rate and never more than half, out of savings past the cushion; the bank buys the rest and is the residual buyer; the central bank never buys at issue, because buying from its own treasury is printing, which is the advances' line — bar one exception since 0.7.15, the par of its own maturing paper, which it takes again at issue as an add-on (the central bank). Coupons and principal are split by the shares struck before the payment: the bank's the way they always went, the households' into savings as untaxed investment income, the central bank's destroyed and remitted. A household short of money sells its paper back to the bank's desk at the month's market value before its dollars and its shares, and a leaver sells on the way out; since 0.7.15 a central bank buying past what the bank holds takes the households' paper by the same door. Sent home by the playtest's -Dplaytest.borrowAtHome, the households took 38% of each issue at the settle and one at the cap of half, were paid $6–92M of coupons over a run and sold back in 15 to 239 months, every sale a household short of cash (0.7.1). HoldersCheck proves each of those, and fails with a residual if the dollar buyback's audit line is deleted; its ninth section has the central bank at the whole of the paper buying the households' once the bank's is gone.

The discount accretes. A term bond's discount — face less what it raised — used to be booked as one month's interest at the settle: $60M of it in one month on seed 0 sent home, taxed, and 45% of it payable to savers. Since 0.7.1 each bond carries its own discountLeft and earns it straight-line over its life, the bank earning its share a month at a time and carrying what it has not yet earned against its book (Bank.unearnedDiscount), so its equity does not move at the settle.

The bank never paid for the city's paper, from at least 0.4.3 until 0.6.11. Every bond and bill the city sells is sold between two presses, and the month snapshotted what the bank owed for it after the top-of-tick clear, so bank.lend() was handed zero for every domestic issue: the paper went onto the bank's book at face, the coupons and the principal came in, and the cash never went out — equity from nothing on one side, the treasury's money from nowhere on the other. The snapshot is taken before the clear now, and what the bank still owes is carried against its pool in the audit and saved, so a city saved between the issue and the settle still pays. A section of BankCheck causes it — $31,000k of paper sold for $30,490.55k, the bank's cash moving by exactly the ordinary month plus that — and fails four ways on the old order. No seed moved, because the playtest's advisor only ever borrowed the cheaper dollars. What the fix uncovered is why the central bank exists: held at a 10% dial, six seeds' treasuries ran dry, and the emergency notes they had lived on free priced at 27–36% once the bank really paid for them and compounded to $193 quadrillion on seed 0 and NaN on seed 6. A free number had been holding a floor.

Rolling what falls due

Jerus: "the game checks whats going to mature next month, and issues what the treasury is lacking … this doesnt take care of the treasury short of cash, thats the central bank thing". Since 0.7.13 the treasury has a setting with three positions — by hand, same structure and 12-month notes — and a new game founds on same structure; an older save and the bare city the harnesses and the playtest use keep it by hand. It stood on both borrow pages until 0.7.32 and is said once since, on the Finances hub (below), with one line on Borrow and one on the fund pointing at it. Rollover runs at the top of the month, in the gap between presses where a player's own issue lands:

  • What falls due is the principal due next month, piece by piece, at home and abroad, off each instrument's own schedule.
  • Last year's surplus nets it first (Jerus: "Net of last year's surplus"): the last twelve months' budget surplus that no earlier roll has used, capped at the treasury's cash and at what falls due. A deficit year nets nothing, and a ledger makes each year's surplus net once — shared since 0.7.14 with the city's fund, which takes its December share first. Since 0.7.15 the surplus pays the market's part of what falls due before the central bank's par (Jerus: "Surplus pays everyone"), so a city in surplus clears its paper whoever holds it.
  • The rest is issued pro rata, sized to raise the cash it needs (Jerus: "Enough to raise the cash"). In the same structure each piece rolls into its own instrument, term and currency — dollars into dollars abroad, or at home when the window is shut, and a serial's instalment into a new serial of its original term; on 12-month notes everything rolls into a local twelve-month note. Below the smallest issue the model makes nothing is issued and the maturity is paid from cash.
  • A month early, because the proceeds wait a month in the treasury: pre-funding, which Canada's own rule asks for — liquidity for at least a month of net projected cash flows, refinancing included.

It does not cover a treasury short for its spending; the central bank's advances still do that. And rolling for cash capitalises interest: paper sells under its face, so an issue sized to raise the maturity's cash has a larger face than the paper it repays, and the old paper's interest is borrowed into the new principal at every roll. Face for face was measured and Jerus chose cash. The default and autopilot runs were line for line 0.7.12's, because their cities owe nothing at a maturity or net it whole; on a Lean founding seven seeds of eight rolled and repaid, $1.68B of face for $1.26B, and seed 0 spiralled — its twenty-year paper raised 0.34 to 0.59 of its face as the rate climbed to 26.8%, each roll was 1.7 to 2.9 times the face of the last, and it ended owing $476.6T with the central bank owed $370.7T and 2,423 people (0.7.13).

Nothing limits the paper at home

The price of the city's paper stops at the dial plus ten points of spread and the term premium; the bank buys whatever the households leave, funded at a window with no cap; debt service is a promise, paid past the advances ceiling; and only abroad does a window shut. Lean seed 0's $476.6T was that, and so are the spirals of 0.7.14 and 0.7.15 — a held 25% city borrowing from its bank to buy the bank's preferred, and five of eight Insane cities. The holdings dial at 100% makes the central bank the buyer when nobody else is; it does not limit what the city can sell. A market limit — a buyer's refusal, or a debt-service rule at home — is on the list, and Jerus's to design.

Borrowing in somebody else's money

USD versions of all three instruments, treasury only. A foreign bond holds its face and coupon in dollars; every public getter answers in local money at today's rate, so a devaluation makes the debt dearer everywhere in the game at once without a call site knowing. Priced on exports, not GDP — the only dollars a city has ever earned came from selling something abroad: a 2% world base, deliberately below the domestic floor (that is the trap), plus a solvency term on dollar debt as years of exports and a service term on next year's bill, capped at 16%. Because the measure is taken in local money, a devaluation worsens the ratio, raises the premium, and makes the next bond dearer — Mexico 1994 and Argentina 2001 without a special case.

The world's paper has a curve since 0.7.2: foreignCurveRate(months) is the world's base plus the country premium plus the same term-premium table, so a dollar issue prices by maturity and a dollar bond is valued at its remaining months on it — the dial moving no longer moves a dollar bond's value, and the country premium moving does. On the way the dollar quote was found to be a money pump: it scaled the service the city already owed and left the new bond's own out, so a thirty-year dollar round trip netted the city $7.5M over eight rounds. It prices its own coupons into the premium now, by a fixed point (quoteForeignRate(Debt, months), walked by Game.selfPricedForeignRate()), and RestructureCheck's dollar round trip, which fails on 0.7.1, is neutral by construction. A dollar bond bought back is declared leaving the country.

The window shuts on conditions the player can see coming: 14 years of exports owed, service past 45% of exports, dollars owed and nothing sold abroad, or five years after a default. Existing paper still falls due. At issue the player chooses to convert and spend or to hold the dollars as reserves. Default tears every foreign bond off the books, no cash moves, the ratios read as perfect — and nobody lends a defaulter a dollar for five years, with a scar on the price that outlasts the closed window. It also fires on its own when the treasury goes a full year of tax revenue into the red with dollars falling due — and since the central bank advances every shortfall at the top of the month, only on a month whose own bills, a balloon falling due, come to more than a year of revenue; whether the rule should read the advances is on the list. Since 0.7.32 choosing it is a page of Finances' own, Finances › Default abroad, reached from Borrow's dollar page, where until then it lit Trade on the rail: two equal cards, what it wipes and what it costs, with Keep paying first. The scar is on the city's premium abroad alone (countryPremium()), never on its own rate at home, and since 0.7.32 the game's words say so: on what the world lends at.

Since 0.7.13 the land office borrows dollars too: a plot paid for out of the vault that the vault cannot cover opens a funding page offering a twenty-year dollar bond or a six-month dollar note sized to the dollar gap (quoteForeignForCash()), whose dollars are held in the vault and the plot bought from them (land); a young city's first dollar loan for land can shut the window for its next one, by the rule that shuts it on dollars owed with nothing sold abroad. And since 0.7.14 an Insane city is founded owing a twenty-year dollar bond at 3% for the ground it starts on (founding a city).

The debt at a glance

Jerus, of the screens Build's redraw had left behind: "the others are still full of text and the design could be more intuitive and fun". Since 0.7.32 Finances opens on a hub that answers what the city owes, when it falls due and what happens when it does. Five figures stand over every page, each a door with one verdict: TREASURY in NEEDS YOU's colour, amber under a month's tax and red overdrawn; OWED, red only when the market has priced the city out; THE RATE, red at the ceiling; COUPON, by the service bands, felt from 12% of the take and constrained past 25% (CityNeeds.SERVICE_FELT, SERVICE_CONSTRAINED); and NEXT DUE, red inside FALLS DUE's three months and amber inside a year (FALLS_DUE_SOON_MONTHS). The old tab's own thresholds went with it: it had drawn debt red at 60% and 120% of output while the market charged the city AAA. WHEN IT FALLS DUE leads the hub, a column for each calendar year a payment falls in, coupons and principal together (DebtManager.ladder()), stacked by instrument with the dollar part striped, the heaviest year tagged and red past half a year of revenue (CityNeeds.YEAR_WALL), a year a city meets by refinancing before it arrives. Later, every year past the twelfth, is a ghost on the same scale, and since 0.7.34, when it is more than twice the tallest year (FinancesScreen.LATER_BREAK), it is drawn broken, 1.2 tallest years high (LATER_CAP) with two slashes across it and its figure still the whole — in Jerus's city D$721.4B against years of D$2.2B to D$47.9B. Until 0.7.32 the ladder weighed later as a thirteenth year, with a false red alert, and drew its bars a year early. NEXT DUE stands beside it with what fell due last month. Under them are the two settings the treasury runs by itself, each said once: ROLLING WHAT FALLS DUE, with next month's roll as a bar — what last year's surplus nets, the central bank's own roll, each issue as a ghost named for its paper and what the treasury's cash pays (Rollover.Plan.fromCash()), the four adding to what falls due in every setting — and the last roll dated by the month it fell due; and WHEN THE BANK FAILS (the bank). Then the six areas as cards, The position, The book, Borrow, Money, The bond market and The city's fund, each page one picture first, its paragraphs behind an (i) and its tables behind details; Issued is a receipt, D$ and US$ are named throughout, and an offer card ends in →. NEEDS YOU's three Finances rows land where they say whatever page the tab was left on: TREASURY on the hub, FALLS DUE on its ladder and BORROWING on Your rate (SummaryScreen.financesDoor()). ForeignDebtCheck's last section holds the ladder: every payment in the calendar year it is paid in, the dollar part exactly the dollar paper's, the heaviest year never later, a quote's schedule on the ladder as its own, and the roll's four parts adding to what falls due in every setting.

14

The bank

Every loan in the city is the Commercial Bank's money, and since 0.7.7 the bank is run as a business: it prices a loan from what the loan costs it, chooses what it pays its savers, holds a capital target of its own and pays its owners what it does not need. Since 0.7.8 a sector defaults a slice at a time, since 0.7.11 the landlords borrow on insured mortgages, since 0.7.12 a firm that cannot pay ends the way a firm does, since 0.7.14 a bank that fails passes to the city, and since 0.7.19 every deposit reaches it and its branches answer to their customers.

What was asked for

Jerus: "lets make banks realistic, and remember, its a business, it wants to make money." The reading of his 0.7.5 city that came before it found the bank deepening both of that city's crises without starting either: a strained bank added up to eighteen points to every rate in the city, which caused the defaults, the write-offs and the lost equity that strained it further. The plan was three batches — the price, the capital, the tab — anchored on the figures of Canada's big banks, and 0.7.7 to 0.7.9 built them. Of its capital he said "the bank chooses."

The bank is somebody

Every loan in the city — to a sector, to a household, and whatever of the city's own paper the households and the central bank do not hold — is the Commercial Bank's money: one building, 29 jobs a branch, its own tab, and a trading desk that trades on the exchange's books like anybody else; since 0.7.12 it also underwrites every business's bonds, and holds them when it chooses to. Its capacity is the tighter of two limits, what its capital allows and six times everything the city has banked with it, and how much of that its book uses is its strain. Until 0.7.19 the second was six times the savings its branches could reach, 250,000 a branch in founding money; in Jerus's year-149 city $1.24 trillion was banked and its 24 branches reached $6.0B of it, and the bank funded an $86.5B book at the central bank's window while the savings earned the deposit rate on money it could not lend. He chose "Drop it: online banking", and every deposit reaches the bank wherever its counters are. Past its capacity the bank does not refuse, and whatever it lends that its own money and the city's deposits do not fund it borrows at the central bank's window — abroad, until 0.7.0 — and what that money costs goes into the price of whatever it lends, where until 0.7.7 its strain added a premium ramping to 18 points on every rate in the city. A city with no bank is simply a bank with no capacity, and since 0.7.7 it is priced the way any loan is, with the window's money as its funding: Jerus's decision was that a city with no bank yet pays "normal" prices, not a punitive one. Branches used to build themselves through the retail advisor when strain passed 70%, and close when their book had not kept their staff for two years.

Since 0.7.19 a branch answers to its customers. Jerus: "bank branch i think should be customers perhaps, but make it be sustainable even if just account fees, aka one branch maintence and operating costs should be less than the revenue it makes of fees for that specific branch, that should always be true." The first branch is the city's charter ("First branch exempt"): it never closes on the rule, and it pays its staff and its repairs but not its template's operating cost of 190 founding thousands a month ("Exempt first branch"), because a founding village of 59 fee-paying households pays $708 a month for a branch that costs $180,800, and with the upkeep a village's charter ran through its founding capital by month 119 to 128 in six of the eight default cities. Another branch opens through the retail advisor only while there are more than 16,000 customers for each branch standing (Bank.CUSTOMERS_PER_BRANCH, TD's clients per branch: about 16 million through more than 1,000 branches), the month's account fees would cover every branch with it, and the city could staff it; never while the bank is under its minimum. A later branch pays its staff, its repairs and the operating cost, and branches the month's fees do not cover close that month, as many at once as it takes, never the charter. The rule reads last month's cost, so a month whose wages rise can leave a branch short once and the next month closes it (Jerus: "Accept the lag"): in the eight cities that built schools it happened in 11 of the 13,646 months that stood more than one branch, the least cover 0.958. So the fees cover the branches as the rule last read them, not in every month without exception. A default city keeps its charter all its life: at 0.7.19 it ended with 79,513 customers to its one branch, and on 1 October it opened no other in 4,005 months. In Jerus's city, run on for 120 months, the bank owed the window nothing where it had owed $73.2B, and no branch's fee cover fell under 1.120 (0.7.19).

The bank lends to households, for cars and for a short month, and the audit already carried both legs of that: the cash leaving the bank and the debt arriving on the household balance each other with nothing new written. A write-off costs the book, not the cash.

Nothing borrows below what the money costs

For most of the project the bank could be made to lend at a rate below what it was paying for the funds. Three separate mechanisms did it — the city's bond floor, the business desk's spread over a risk-free rate, and a carry trade that borrowed abroad to buy domestic paper — and none of them was wrong on its own terms. The bank simply had no veto.

cost of funds = depositRate × (1 − wholesaleShare) + windowRate × wholesaleShare // the window: the dial and a quarter point since 0.7.7, a point from 0.7.0 any rate = max(riskFree, costOfFunds + MIN_MARGIN) + its own premium // MIN_MARGIN = .01, one point; every rate until 0.7.7, the city's paper since

One rule, three call sites until 0.7.7, and the city's paper alone since. It is blended, not marginal — the first cut used whichever source the last dollar came from, and the floor flipped between 1.9% and 13.6% across twelve consecutive months. And it is struck once, at the close of the month, and carried in the save: the live path priced mid-month and the load path priced at the end, so a city reloaded from disk quoted a different rate than the one it had just been running at — 0.0135 against 0.0133, which is the kind of divergence a harness catches and a player never would.

Sixteen seeds, before and after

Bank failures 5.0 → 0.0 median. Recapitalisation $3.8bn → $0. Closing equity $6.6bn → $9.1bn. Wholesale funding 24–47% → 0–8% of income. Months run at a loss 44–58% → 3–14%. Every seed lifetime-profitable, and the "gentler financing" calibration was not touched to get it.

The obvious next step — make the bank a real intermediary, funding domestic investment out of domestic savings — was measured and abandoned. The bank lends 1.87% of its deposits at home and runs at 31–49% of the capacity it already has. The city's savings are on the order of two hundred thousand times what its productive sector wants to borrow. The bank is demand-limited, not funding-limited, and no amount of deposit-gathering changes that. On the default seed on 28 September it ended with $293bn deposited and $131M lent, 13% of its capacity, and on 1 October with $260.5bn deposited and $1k lent, at 6%; on 5 October, at 0.7.49, it ended with $162.3T deposited and $234.7B lent, at 35%, in a city whose prices had risen 1,213-fold.

What a loan costs

Since 0.7.7 a loan is priced the way a bank prices one, as the sum of four costs (Bank, WHAT A LOAN COSTS): the funds-transfer price, what the next dollar costs it for the loan's term — the policy rate while it holds spare reserves, which would otherwise earn the dial at the central bank, the window's rate while it borrows there, blended by how it is funded, and the curve's term premium for the maturity; the running costs, a trailing year of payroll and upkeep over the larger of its book and what its capital could carry, so a young bank is not charged its whole payroll on an empty book; the expected loss of a sound book through the cycle, BASE_LOSS_RATE 0.4%, about what Canada's big banks provision in a normal year; and the capital charge, the equity the loan ties up at the return the market prices the bank's shares on — 12.5% at the defaults, the world's rate and the three-point premium over the 40% payout (Equity.requiredYield()) — over what that money would cost as debt. Prime is the four for a sound business borrowing for thirty-six months. The carry trade pays the three without the loss; a household pays the four at its own weight plus its spread for the months of income it owes; a business's own risk and a landlord's insured mortgage are below.

prime = funds-transfer price + running costs + expected loss + capital charge // the default seed's last month on 28 September: 0.12 + 1.06 + 0.40 + 2.04 = 3.62% on a dial of 0.01% // ...and on 1 October: 0.12 + 0.86 + 0.40 + 2.04 = 3.43%, each part as the playtest rounds it, on the same dial // ...and on 5 October, at 0.7.49: 2.79 + 0.84 + 0.40 + 1.60 = 5.63% on a dial of 2.68%

The expected loss is a constant because the bank's own record, tried twice, was a disaster twice: a whole sector was one borrower in the model then, so the record was lumpy by construction, and a five-year window priced prime at 77.9% in month 12 of seed 0. In calm years on 0.7.7's eight seeds prime ran 1.7 to 2.0 points over the dial, where Canada's runs 2.2; the cities grew more slowly in their first century and ended about 9% smaller, and the bank failed 4 times against 19.

What savers get, and the fees

Since 0.7.7 the bank chooses what it pays its savers — Jerus: "it can pass on less or more if it wants" — and it chooses by how it is funded: 0.35 of the dial when it is flush with reserves, because its next deposit would only earn the dial at the central bank less the cost of the account, rising toward 0.90 while it borrows at the window, because every deposit it finds saves it the window's rate, and moving a sixth of the way a month, so most of a move reaches savers inside a year and none of it in one step. It never pays them more than the smaller of what its interest margin leaves after payroll and upkeep and the savers' own share of that margin. The second bound was the gate's catch: the bank counts the city's savings as deposits without holding them, the one stated simplification in its header, and held at 10% the first finished build paid its savers every dollar it earned on them and failed 184 times against 0.7.6's 78. Over the eight seeds savers got 0.37 to 0.44 of the dial on average, where real banks pass on about 0.4. On the default seed on 28 September the bank passed on 0.29 of it on average, its margin holding its savers under the rate it chose in 3,157 months of the run, and on 1 October 0.19, held in 3,221 months; on 5 October, at 0.7.49, 0.07, held in 3,856.

It replaced two rules that are history now. From the bank's founding it paid savers 45% of what it earned in interest (DEPOSIT_PASS_THROUGH), and bid higher only when it was short of deposits; and since 0.7.3 it quoted no more than it charged, because a founding bank's first month paid savers 45% of everything it earned, the placement of its own capital included, over $5.7k of deposits, which read as 7,567% a year and would have put the households' spending at its floor in month 3 of every seed. That cap bound in 17 to 236 months of each default run. Both went with 0.7.7.

Fees, since 0.7.7: every housed household pays an account fee of $12 a month at founding prices, struck at the expected price level since 0.7.42 (the anchor) where it followed the price index until then, on its own line of the household books and declared to the audit, and orphans, prisoners and the unhoused pay nothing; and new lending pays 1%, out of a business loan's proceeds, grossed up so that the firm still covers its shortfall, and added to a household's or a car's loan. On 0.7.7's seeds the running costs were 0–7% of the bank's revenue against a real bank's 49–60%, and fees 0–6% against about 39%, because its payroll is tiny against what it lends; that was left as measured. Since 0.7.19 the account fees are also how the bank counts its customers, the households paying the month's fee, and what every branch past the charter must be covered by (above). What the owners put up when a branch opens, $32M at founding prices (Bank.PAID_IN_PER_BRANCH), is struck at the expected level too (Bank.seedConstants()).

Its capital, and what it does with its profit

Since 0.7.8 the bank keeps its capital as a business does:

  • A loss allowance in IFRS 9's two stages: a year's expected loss on a sound borrower and the lifetime loss on one in trouble — a sector's firms past 0.9 of their assets, a family past half its credit ceiling — with write-offs drawn against it first and the provision the month's move in it. Its loans sit on its balance sheet net of it.
  • Its own capital target: the 8% minimum plus the larger of Basel's 2.5-point conservation buffer and the worst year of provisions it has lived through, capped at the whole Basel stack of 8.5 points (MAX_BUFFER) — uncapped, one restructure put the target at 48–108% and stalled a city at 8,348 people. A young bank aims at 10.5% and one that has lived through a crisis at up to 16.5%, and the top of its band is 2.5 points over that.
  • A payout rule: nothing under its target, 45% of its profit after tax inside its band (RBC paid 43%), and past the top of the band the excess as well, a twelfth a month. 0.7.7's bank paid out almost nothing, because its dividend was capped at its cash, and sat on $26.6–50.5bn of equity against a book of $1.7–10bn.
  • Lending that tightens with its capital: free at or over its target; between the minimum and the target a borrower's debt may grow at most 1% × x/(1 − x) a month, where x is how far its capital stands from the minimum to the target — nothing at the minimum, 1% halfway; under the minimum only what keeps its borrowers going, a business's interest reserve and a family's month of interest. The families' credit reads the bank's state for the first time.
  • Its own shares issued only under its target and bought back only with capital over it, and booked as capital, not as trading income — 0.7.7 had booked $8.2bn of them as $8.65bn of desk profit on seed 0 — and its desk buys other companies' shares only while the bank would still hold its target with them on its books at the weight Basel gives a trading book.
  • A leverage ratio since 0.7.11: equity of at least 3% of everything it has lent, whatever that weighs (LEVERAGE_RATIO_MIN, Basel III's), which floors every requirement above — the minimum, the target, the payout, the two spare-capital limits and the lending rule. It came with the insured mortgages, which weigh nothing: without it the bank paid out the equity they never asked of it, grew thin and fell back into its old branch trap, and with it and two other fixes the failures with no loan loss went from 72 to none.

On 0.7.8's eight seeds, in its growth years, the bank held 19.2% against a 16.5% target, earned 13.0% on its equity (RBC 16.3%), paid out 76% of its profit and provisioned 0.41% of its loans a year (RBC 0.43%), and its worst year was 38–109% of its weighted book, so its target sat at MAX_BUFFER in every seed. On the default seed on 28 September, over its 216 growth years of 333, the median year held 63% against a target of 15.5%, earned 6.1% on its equity and paid out 82% of its profit; on 1 October, over 259 growth years, it held 41.6% against 16.5%, earned 18.5% and paid out 99.7%; and on 5 October, at 0.7.49, over 264, it held 49.0% against 16.5%, earned 26.3% and paid out 88.0%.

Business credit

A separate hierarchy, because a business loan must never charge the city's cash. Interest reaches the treasury only through the sector's income statement. Loans are interest-only bullets over 36 months, fixed for all of them at the rate they were written at, originated automatically to cover a hole and, since 0.7.8's pricing, for buildings too; a landlord's homes are on mortgages instead. Until 0.7.7 rates ran the city's rate + 1% to 8% on debt-to-assets, the base never under the bank's cost of funds plus a point, plus a point for each prior write-down up to three — on the default seed on 22 September, with the city's note at 1.00%, the spotless sectors with little debt borrowed at 2.00%, a leveraged landlord with no write-down at 7.40%, and a sector with three or more write-downs at 5.00%. Since 0.7.7 they sit on prime, and since 0.7.8 on the default curve below.

The underwriter leaves a gap. It will lend to 0.9× assets; the default point is 1.5×. They used to be the same number, so the desk parked every borrower exactly on the write-down line. Until 0.7.8 a sector past 1.5× was written down whole to 0.6× and blocked from borrowing — twelve months the first time, twenty-four the second, thirty-six after that, a ban that also stops its investment advisor expanding on credit — and over 4,000 months that pair of changes took write-offs from $25.8bn to $1.35bn. Since 0.7.8 only a sector with nothing left is written down whole, and the rest of its trouble is a slice at a time.

There was no concentration limit, and a fixture found the hole by accident on 17 September: Materials built its first plant, had to buy a fleet of lorries as well as the plant, borrowed $57,939 from a bank with $50,292 of equity — thirty-eight per cent of the whole book in one name — and defaulted in month 70, taking the bank's equity to minus $2,628. Neither half is a bug in the bank: a sector that buys vehicles as well as machines is a riskier borrower, which is the mechanic. And 0.7.2 found the hole again from the other side: on the new path Manufacturing borrowed $74M against a bank whose equity was $30M, for a Fabrication Works that loses money before interest, and was written off at month 166 and its restructured remainder at 178 in four seeds of the eight — all of the rise in the ensemble's bank failures, 8 to 19. From 0.7.8 on, one sector holding 30–73% of the book was under every failure the bank had, and 0.7.12 answered it with a price rather than a limit (below).

A sector is many firms

As 0.7.8 was first built, the bank failed 92 times on the default eight against 0.7.7's 4, and the trace said why: a whole sector was one borrower, and when Real Estate or Manufacturing restructured, the bank lost 60% of everything that sector owed in a single month, 37–185% of its weighted book in its worst year. Jerus was offered three ways on and chose the third, "go for option C": a sector stands for many firms, and only its weakest part goes bust, a slice at a time. How many is Merton's structural model, the basis of Moody's KMV:

PD(L) = N( ln(L / 1.5) / σ ) // a year; L the sector's debt over its assets, 1.5 INSOLVENCY_TRIGGER h = 1 − (1 − PD)1/12 // the month's share of its debt whose firms default σ = ASSET_VOLATILITY 0.25 // the one new number, Jerus's to settle; the literature puts it at 20–35%

So 1.5 is where a firm defaults. The sector's own books carry the common shock and σ is the spread of fortunes among its firms, which averages out over many of them, so the flow is deterministic. Each month the defaulting share of its debt is written off pro rata across its loans and bonds at each instrument's own loss, and the firms keep their plant; its leverage falls, so next month's hazard is smaller. Only a sector with nothing left, or one still past the line that nobody will lend to after a write-down, is written down whole by the old rule, and only that backstop goes on its record, with the surcharge and the ban. The allowance reads the same curve, staged firm by firm. A loan is priced by it: prime, plus a loan's loss given default times PD(L) less what prime already carries, plus a point for each write-down on its record up to three, plus since 0.7.12 the capital its book's concentration asks of it — at the leverage the loan would leave the borrower at, a project's building counted, and read on the borrower's last quarter (STATEMENT_MONTHS 3), as a bank reads statements, while the defaults read the month, because firms fail on what they actually owe. A sound borrower pays prime, one at the watch line 0.11 points over it, one at 1.2 times its assets 4.25 and one at the default point 12.1. Since 0.7.33 a city just loaded quotes each sector with its record and the book's concentration: until then the load path priced business credit before the record was put back and with no concentration charge, so the screens quoted prime and the sector's own risk alone until a month ran — Mining at 3.07% after Continue and 6.38% a month on — though the month re-priced before it lent, so the simulation never lent at the wrong rate. A sector's Cash & debt page draws NEW BORROWING COSTS in the parts its rate was struck from (BusinessDebtManager.quoteParts()) since 0.7.38, as the Bank's ladder does, its white mark the concentration discount alone.

It took four attempts to stand up. Pricing by the curve cut lending past 1.0 by two thirds; Basel's large-exposure rule, with the rest of a big loan syndicated to banks abroad on Jerus's choice, sent 99% of business lending abroad, ran prices to 3.9 times founding and still failed the bank 101 times, and was deleted, because a sector is many firms and Basel's rule is for one firm or a group tied by control, not an industry; reading a borrower on its quarter was the best single fix of the four.
