It shipped at 39 failures, and under every one of them one sector held 30–73% of the book. A failing sector's buildings are sold to the builders for their material (the private sector).

How a firm that cannot pay ends

0.7.12 put a bond beside the loan (the owners, the bonds and the exchange) and found, partway through, Luxury Retail's late collapses running on an interest-free overdraft: a till allowed below zero, owing nobody, for hundreds of months. Everything after that was about how a firm that cannot pay ends, and each answer is a real rule that Jerus chose:

  • Can't pay means default: beside the balance-sheet test the model had, the cash-flow test of Canada's Bankruptcy and Insolvency Act, s. 2. A short month recalls its dollars abroad, sells the other sectors' bonds it holds and borrows, and what it still cannot pay defaults a slice the size of the part it cannot pay.
  • Credit lines stay open: the capital rule rations growth — project loans and new mortgages — and working capital, a short month's cover, the interest reserve and a rollover, is honoured up to the 0.9 ceiling and the default point, as banks did in 2008 and in March 2020. The first cut treated a short month's cover as growth and set off a credit crunch.
  • Interim financing: after the write-down, what is still unpaid is lent as a loan that ranks ahead of all other debt (InterimLoan; Canada's CCAA s. 11.2, and §364 in the United States), and nothing is forgiven on that path; a sector still past the line after the write-down finds nobody to lend and goes to the backstop, and a ban stops growth lending, not what keeps a firm running.
  • Nothing past the default point: no door lends to a sector whose leverage, read on the quarter, is past 1.5.

Concentration is priced, not capped, which was Jerus's choice. The bank holds Basel's IRB capital against each sector's loans, with a correlation that rises with how concentrated its book is across sectors, allocated to each sector by the Euler rule and so into its loan rate; the single-industry multiplier is Basel III's 1.25, set for large financial institutions and kept and flagged. And since 0.7.12 each instrument recovers what it recovers in the world: 75% of a bank loan and 45% of a bond. Against 0.7.11 on eight seeds, failures went 115 → 1 in the default setup and 265 → 9 on autopilot, the money the city put into its bank $134.7B → $3.1B and $228.8B → $2.0B, and the money created by forgiven overdrafts fell from $19.9B partway through the batch to $0.095B, all of it the backstop's. What still failed the bank was the young one on its $32M of founding capital, before month 1,000, when Manufacturing or Automotive went. On the default seed on 28 September 1,535 sector-months defaulted for want of cash over the run, 1,276 of them shells — sectors holding no plant that still owed something — which nothing winds up; on 1 October 663 did, 616 of them shells.

The landlords take a mortgage

A trace of 0.7.10 found what had stopped the cranes. The landlords financed fifty-year buildings on three-year interest-only bullets: on autopilot seed 0, months 2,280 to 2,490, their maturing loans went unrolled, the till fell to minus $6.8B, every new building had to borrow that hole on top of its cost, and nothing was built for about 200 months with homes 37% short. Held at 10%, the test asked gross rent to cover 1.25 times the interest on the whole cost, about 14.5% a year, where a home earned 8–9%. Jerus chose "Mortgages", on "CMHC (Canada)"'s rulebook, and "Insured by the city". Since 0.7.11 a landlord's residential building is bought with at least 15% of its own funds and a mortgage for the rest (Mortgage): at most 85% of the cost, a level payment over 40 years at a rate fixed for 10 and renewed at the day's rate, and the lender's test is the building's net operating income — rent less maintenance and property tax — at least 1.20× the payment; since 0.7.44 it reads that payment at the real rate (BusinessInvestment.realTestRate()), the larger of a quarter of the insured rate and the insured rate less the inflation the landlords expect, as a lender reads a payment that inflation will shrink, while the loan is still written at the insured rate and a refusal names the rate it tested (the private sector). The city insures it: the landlord pays CMHC's premium, 5.00% of the loan and 0.75% more for forty years, added to the loan and paid to the treasury as revenue, and when a landlord is written down the treasury pays the bank what came off insured balances, as a promise. The bank weighs an insured mortgage at nothing and sets nothing aside for it, and prices it at its ten-year funds-transfer price and its running costs, with no expected loss, plus the capital the leverage ratio ties up, 0.41 to 0.64 points at the defaults. Everything else keeps its thirty-six-month loan and its 1.25 interest test, read at the real rate too since 0.7.42.

The stall went: through seed 0's old window homes rose from 35,141 to 36,464 and the till stayed at or above zero. The 10% city stayed small, which is the honest answer at a 10% real rate: the 1.20 test on a forty-year paydown asks net rent of 12.2% of the cost a year. The insurance took in $3.9B of premiums over the eight default seeds against $2.1M of claims, and held at 10% it lost money for the first time, $404M of claims against $56M of premiums. And the bank failed more, 115 times against 42: without the landlords' $6B of fully weighted bullets it was small, $161M of equity at month 2,400 against $1.13B, and the industrial sector that failed it owed five to six times its equity. Jerus shipped it with that open, and the next batch was the bonds. On the default seed on 28 September the landlords wrote 293 mortgages over the run and renewed 879, and the insurance took $185M of premiums against $70k of claims; on 1 October they wrote 370 and renewed 1,110, and it took $208M against $1k; and on 5 October, at 0.7.49, 638 and 1,914, and $16.6B against nothing, with the lender's test declining a landlord's plan in 1,086 months.

When the bank fails

A bank that has lost more than it owns has failed, which is one of the shocks that can start a run on the currency. The path from 278 jobs a branch to 29, a deposit limit from $60k to $250k, honest underwriting and an escalating exclusion once took it from 308 failures in 4,000 months to none in seven seeds of eight; what each batch since has done to that count is in the table below. Until 0.7.14 the city could put capital into a failed bank and got nothing for it, and the hole was declared to the audit as absorbed from outside the city. Jerus, on the owners of a failed bank: "City takes the shares … but later bank can dilute by issuing shares... we dont want commercial to be owned by government fully in the long run". Since 0.7.14 the treasury has a setting, When the bank fails: Automatic, which new games found on, or Wait for my button, which older saves and the harnesses' bare city keep. Either way the resolution (Game.resolveBank()) is the same:

  • every common share passes to the city's rescue book, the households', the world's and the desk's alike, and the old owners get nothing, as under the bail-in regime of Canada's deposit insurer and as with Northern Rock in 2008;
  • the city's preferred and its warrants are cancelled, because the hole took them;
  • the city pays the hole and the capital to reopen at one and a half times the minimum, as a promise: the treasury's cash first, and the central bank advances the rest at the next settle, past the ceiling if it must (Jerus: "Central bank advances it");
  • and the bank reopens the same month.

Nothing enters from outside the city any more; a bank waiting for the button carries its hole at the window. The city's stake falls only as the bank sells new shares (Jerus: "New issues only"), which it does when it is under its target and when it repays the preferred: on 0.7.14's default runs the median stake was 72.8% five years after a resolution, 51.0% at ten and 58.8% at twenty-five. The player can sell the rescue book by hand, since 0.7.39 from the bank's page under the city's fund, whose ticket sells the fund's market-book shares first and then the rescue book, each at its own cost; the page shows both books and their sum, the yield on the rescue's cost, and the city's stake with a door to the Bank tab's owners. At a resolution the fund's own market-book shares in the bank go with everyone else's, for nothing, and its ledger realizes their cost as a loss (the city's fund).

A standing bank under its minimum asks for preferred shares. Jerus wanted "a popup message saying bank wants to issue you shares or something", and since 0.7.14 the inbox carries the bank's offer, on the terms of the United States' 2008 Capital Purchase Program: enough to take it back to its target, but at least 1% and at most 3% of its risk-weighted assets; a cumulative dividend of 5% a year, 9% from the fifth anniversary, paid only out of equity over its target, and no common dividend while any is unpaid; for three years no buybacks and no rise in the common dividend; and warrants on common shares worth 15% of the amount, for ten years. The player accepts or declines, and the bank asks again a quarter later either way (Jerus: "Quarter"); a treasury short of the money is shown the build screen's two funding offers. Each block is repaid at its third anniversary at par with its unpaid dividends, from the bank's capital over its target and the rest by new common sold to the public (Jerus: "Sell new shares to repay"), which dilutes the city's own common, and after the last block the warrants are bought back at fair value. The balance sheet has a third equity line for it, Preferred shares (the city).

On 0.7.14's default runs the city bought 45 blocks for $1.26B and was repaid $1.22B at three years, $996M of it by new shares, with nothing outstanding at the end. Held at 25% the preferred is circular capital: the city borrows from the bank to buy the bank's preferred, every offer is larger than the last, and the eight seeds bought $962T of it with the advances peaking at $20.4 quadrillion; Jerus kept it, "Keep as built", and the limit on the city's own paper would close it. On the default seed on 28 September the bank failed once, in month 442: the city paid $40.9M to resolve it, all of it from its cash, the old owners lost $45.6M at the last price, and the city still held 4.2% of the bank at the end; six preferred offers were accepted for $234M, and none was outstanding at the end. On 1 October it failed once too, and the city took it over in month 664: the resolution cost $36.5M, all of it from the treasury's cash, the old owners lost $69.2M at the last price, and the city still held 62.2% of the bank at the end, never under half; eight preferred offers were accepted for $52.8M, and none was outstanding. On 5 October, at 0.7.49, it failed eight times, the first in month 2,678: the city paid $223.4B to resolve them, all of it from the treasury's cash, the old owners lost $132.5B at the last price, and the city held all of the bank at the end, in a city whose prices had risen 1,213-fold; 64 preferred offers were accepted for $356.6B, and none was outstanding.

0.7.14's runs part from 0.7.13's only at the first month the city puts capital into its bank, where Jerus's rules replace the old playtest's gift: its player had put up to a quarter of the treasury into any bank under its minimum, every month, for nothing. 0.7.15's default and autopilot runs are 0.7.14's to the byte, and 0.7.16's are 0.7.15's. From 0.7.17 to 0.7.19 the batches measured the default eight beside a city that builds schools and one held at 25%, not on autopilot or at 10%: the schools city went 6 → 4 → 4 → 4, and the city held at 25% 43 → 27 → 16 → 8. The 25% city lived on 0.7.18 on the strain of the deposit cap, which opened branches and brought their owners' capital in, and on the window, where a median seed owed $181.9 trillion; 0.7.19 removed both on purpose, and it ended at 428 people at the median against 8,168, its prices 22% lower and its builders losing money in 3,924 of 3,999 months. Jerus: "Let it fail." From 0.7.19 to 0.7.41 the default playtest wrote the same eight trace files to the byte: none of the interface batches moved it. The price model moved them at 0.7.42, 0.7.43 and 0.7.44, the wage floor in today's money at 0.7.47, the fund at 0.7.48 and transit at 0.7.49, each re-baselined and named, so a reading dated 1 October is of the older model and one dated 5 October of 0.7.49's.

The Bank tab

Jerus: "a redesign of the bank UI info, cause when you click on bank you dont even see all the relevant stuff, lets make banks realistic." Since 0.7.9 the tab has answered whether the bank is healthy, and why, without a click, and every figure on it is a model getter, because a dozen on the old tab had been recomputed on the screen and several were wrong: per-branch figures in founding money after a reform, a weight table with no term and no desk that did not foot, 99900.0% capital with nothing lent, an alert on the wrong line. Since 0.7.33 it opens on an Overview, redrawn in Build's style (Jerus: "the others are still full of text and the design could be more intuitive and fun"). The bank's stance is a banner, its word on a chip and Bank.status() whole — healthy, rebuilding, under its minimum, failed, or no bank. THE CAPITAL GAUGE stands on the ratio that binds, the leverage ratio or the risk-weighted one as status() reads it, on its band with the minimum, the target and the top marked, the other measure in a line under it. Beside it a scorecard of eight: profit this month and over twelve, return on equity against the owners' 12.5%, credit losses, amber past twice what a sound book loses (Bank.LOSS_WATCH), net interest margin, costs over revenue, what is lent, deposits, and HOW FULL (Bank.strain()), worded and coloured as NEEDS YOU's THE BANK row, where the capital ratio stood. Under them THE LADDER OF ITS RATES draws every rung in its parts — prime as its money, running costs, expected loss and capital charge; a business as prime and its own risk, its record and the book's concentration (BusinessDebtManager.quoteParts(), the parts its rate was struck from), with its bonds under it; the families; the carry trade; an insured mortgage as its money, running costs and capital; and the city's paper outlined, the market's price — each step a chip in points and each row's sentence behind an (i), and since 0.7.38 a sector shut out of credit is named in red beside its shut N mo, the name still a door to its Cash & debt page. The six pages are cards under it with a year of their line, and each leads with one picture. Profit is a waterfall from the interest, by who paid it, to what the bank kept. The Balance sheet (since 0.7.13) is what it owns against what it owes and its owners' as two bars on one scale, the allowance and the year before as ticks and a failed bank's hole outlined red, with the deposits on a card beside it, a memo because the model does not carry them as the bank's liability. Lending is THE BOOK as one bar by borrower with the bonds in it, so that it foots to what is lent — it had missed $166.4M of bonds in the 600-month city — and a card a business with what it owes the bank and what of its bonds the bank holds, on a leverage scale to the default point. Funding is the deposits and what funds the book as two bars, then the savers, the central bank, what it can carry and the branches. Capital & owners is both capital ratios on their bands with BINDS on one, the payout, the equity's walk, the ITS OWNERS card (the exchange), the rescues and the preferred. History is City History's own chart of its rates — the policy rate, what savers got and prime, recessions as bands — with six small charts on its window, HOW FULL among them with its 100% line, where the old chart set the face of the book against what capital carries weighed and read 94 times over in a city 38% full; since 0.7.38 the central bank's and the bank's decisions are flags on a small lane under it, and it does not pan: it shows the last ten years, and Over the years opens City History. Since 0.7.46 its capital chart is THE LEVERAGE RATIO while that measure binds, once two months of it are recorded. Every old statement and table is under details, verbatim, and every door lands where its line is decided: the policy rate on Policy, Finances' WHEN THE BANK FAILS and Borrow, a sector's Cash & debt, Build's shops and City History.

The balance sheet sets this month against a year ago, every line opening into its detail, and splits equity into what was paid in and what was retained so that the two add up to it to the cent. Retained earnings ended below zero in 27 of 0.7.13's 32 runs, because dividends that return excess capital come out of retained while the city's rescues sit in paid-in, and Jerus kept it so. Since 0.7.14 a third equity line carries the city's preferred, and since 0.7.33 the resolution and the preferred offer are cards at the top of every page, with the bank missing, under its minimum, money from abroad on Funding and the two residuals; a preferred offer to a short treasury shows its two loans as the land office's cards side by side. Since 0.7.19 Funding shows What it can lend against, every deposit and the money from abroad, where it showed what the branches reached, and Its branches by their customers: the customers a branch against the 16,000 one needs, a branch's fees this month against what a branch past the first cost last month, the charter's exemption and the lag. Until 0.7.33 the branch verdict was the rule's and could disagree with the figures beside it, which left the charter's exemption out, so the tab could say a branch would pay beside a fee smaller than the cost printed above it; it is the investors' planner's now (BusinessInvestment.planBank(), a read), Yes only when they would build and otherwise Not yet: and what holds them — the 2,400-month city's page had said yes where the planner said the city could staff 38% of a Commercial Bank and wanted 80%. Its faces print a true minus and no −0.00%, a rate under a tenth of a point to three places, and colour the verdicts and nothing else. BankCheck's thirteenth section asserts the arithmetic the tab reads, and its section 13b, since 0.7.33, that every rung's parts are its rate — a sector's quote to the bit and an insured mortgage to 1e-12 at six dials — that a city just loaded quotes its record and its concentration, and that the branch verdict is the planner's. None of the redrawn tab has been seen on a screen.

15

The central bank

Until 0.7.0 the game had a central bank, and it was the rest of the world. The commercial bank placed its spare cash abroad at the world's 2% and funded its shortfalls abroad; the policy dial only floored the lending rate on top, which is why it stopped mattering past 17%; and money was created only from outside — the founding, exports, capital coming in, the bank's wholesale funding — so the price level had nothing at home to answer to. Five batches, 0.6.11 to 0.7.3, brought the marginal role home, 0.7.15 made it the buyer of last resort for the city's paper, and 0.7.42 to 0.7.45 made what the city believes of it the anchor its prices are struck at. This is what the central bank is, what it holds, what each of its five dials does, how little the rate moved prices until 0.7.42 — measured, and said plainly — and the anchor that has it move them since.

What was asked for

Jerus: "…we implement a central bank, and basically the central bank would buy gbonds or sell gbonds from thin air … the feds sheet would show how much debt it holds, like debt to itself aka money printing, like QE and QT … and that is the M2 supply or what do you think?" Decided the same night: a floor system, households who can hold the city's paper, and an autopilot beside the dial. Each batch was gated on the whole suite and on the eight seeds, and the design note that set them out is the-central-bank.md.

The books

CentralBank is a balance sheet the player reads, on the Money page under Finances. Assets: advances to the commercial bank at the window; advances to the treasury, its overdraft, which is what "printed" means on the screens; the city's paper it holds, at face; and the vault, read from ForeignAccounts rather than moved there, because the treasury still buys and sells reserves and the currency's code has to see them. Liabilities: reserves and currency, which together are M0. Currency is zero — nobody in this city holds cash outside the bank — and kept so that the day somebody does it has a line. Its equity is what the two sides leave.

Money is made and destroyed here and nowhere else. An advance, interest on reserves and the remittance are money issued; a repayment, the window's interest and the advances' interest are money retired. MoneyAudit sees the central bank as a boundary participant, like the world, and declares both under a scope of its own, so the month's issued less retired is the change in M0 to the cent — the difference between money from thin air and a residual. CentralBankCheck section 1 walks a month with every kind of flow in it and asserts exactly that. M0 is a ledger of what this bank has made and not taken back, not a reading of the commercial bank's till, because money reaches the pools without passing through a central bank — exports, capital, the founding — and the two could not both hold the identity.

M2 is what the public holds: the households' deposits, the sectors', the world's, and currency (Game.getM2()). It grows when the bank lends reserves on or the treasury spends printed money, not when the central bank buys from the bank. Both are series, with a column each in the year book. Since 0.7.32 the Money page draws the books as pictures: THE CENTRAL BANK'S BOOK as two bars on one scale, what it holds against what it owes, its equity the difference and a shortfall red; WHAT THE PUBLIC HOLDS, M2 as a bar of its parts; THIS MONTH, the money made and destroyed as bars of their parts and what M0 moved by; and M2 with M0, and the advances, as two small City History charts that open on ten years. On the default seed on 22 September the run ends with M0 at $2.39B, all of it reserves, M2 at $78.0B, the central bank having made $18.5B and destroyed $16.1B since founding and remitted $486M to the treasury — and its equity at minus $2.39B, a loss carried from paying interest on reserves in money it made, which is exactly what printing to pay interest on reserves looks like. On 0.7.2's eight default seeds it ended below zero in every one, at minus $1.1B to minus $3.3B. On 28 September, with the dial's median at 0.44% over the run, the default seed ended with M0 at $1.48M, M2 at $293.4B, $8.08B made and $8.08B destroyed since founding and $426M remitted, and its equity at $32.1M: the vault it counts as its asset, less $1.48M of loss carried. On 1 October, the dial's median 0.46%, it ended with M0 at $4.94M, M2 at $261.0B, $44.1M made and $39.1M destroyed since founding, nothing remitted over the run, and its equity at $13.9M, the vault less $4.94M of loss carried. On 5 October, at 0.7.49, the dial's median 3.18%, it ended with M0 at $173.2B, all of it reserves, M2 at $162.7T in a city whose prices had risen 1,213-fold, $173.2B made and nothing destroyed since founding, nothing remitted, and its equity at $221.7B, the vault less $173.2B of loss carried.

The rate is a floor

The dial is the rate the central bank pays on reserves. The commercial bank's spare cash sits there earning it, where it used to earn the world's 2% abroad, and its shortfalls are borrowed at the window at the dial plus WINDOW_PENALTY — a quarter point since 0.7.7, the Bank of Canada's own spread, and a point before — where they used to cost the city's own paper rate plus two points and a stretch; a failed bank is charged nothing at the window while it is resolved (0.7.1), because its equity is zero and a charge would fail it again. Savers got their share of what reserves earned — a bank whose only income was its reserves paid them 0.675% at a 3% dial and 2.25% at 10%, the first time the dial had ever reached a household — and since 0.7.7 they get the share of the dial the bank chooses (the bank). Nothing lends below what reserves earn and nothing borrows above what the window charges, so the dial is the price of money: the city's note prices at its floor plus its spreads, and MonetaryCheck's section 6 reads the city's rate at exactly the dial at 3, 10, 20 and 40% and the bank's lending rate about one to two points over it — 5.02%, 11.28%, 21.02% and 40.98% on 28 September, 5.00%, 11.27%, 20.99% and 40.93% on 1 October, and 4.99%, 11.27%, 21.00% and 40.94% on 5 October, at 0.7.49. On the way it found a premium counted twice: the carry trade and the bank's deposit bid were priced off a rate that already carried the strain premium, and lendingRate() added it again. The carry trade itself is untouched, and is now the priced alternative rather than the marginal source.

Interest on reserves is paid in money made for it, and that is the one place the floor has no floor of its own. Held at 30% or 50% for a whole run, M0 compounds to 108 to 3×1014 $B while prices stay near one — spare cash earning the rate in new money that becomes spare cash — and the same mechanism took M0 to $1.25 quadrillion in the six broke seeds held at 0.7.1's stop of 25%. It is on the list.

Autopilot. A toggle on the monetary page hands the dial to the Taylor rule each month — the rule the page already prints, which since 0.7.2 is clamped only by the dial's bound at 100% — until the player's hand takes it back; saved, and held in a playtest by -Dplaytest.autopilot; the dial itself is held for a whole run by -Dplaytest.policyRate. With the rule on the dial on all eight seeds, 0.7.2 took bank failures from 27 to 17, the dial peaked at 30.5% and no seed spiralled: with the rule's hand on it, a credible real rate holds. Since 0.7.13 a new game founds on the autopilot; an older save keeps the hand it was played with.

The target is the player's. Jerus: "i want to have the dial not target 0 inflation, set it so that you can choose what is your inflation target". Since 0.7.4 the rule aims at inflationTarget, 2% by default (DEFAULT_INFLATION_TARGET, the constant it had been), in half points from nothing to 10%, and since 0.7.15 to 20% — Jerus: "inflation target can be higher tgan 10%, up yo 20%" (MAX_INFLATION_TARGET). It sits under the autopilot on the monetary page and applies at once, like the ceiling, with chips to 5%, and the rule's sentence is struck live at the target and at another (ruleRate(inflation, target)), so that at 5% inflation the page can say the rule sets 7.5% aiming at 2% and 8.5% aiming at nothing without doing the arithmetic itself — 10.5% until 0.7.42. On target the rule sets the neutral real rate plus the target since 0.7.42: NEUTRAL_RATE's 3% is the neutral nominal rate at the default 2%, a real rate of one point, so a 5% target is met on target at 6% and a 0% target at 1% (DebtManager.ruleRate()). Until then the rule read NEUTRAL_RATE + TAYLOR_WEIGHT × (inflation − target) whatever the target, which at a 20% target sat at the 0% floor until inflation passed 18% — a target the rule could not aim at, and part of the question 0.7.42 answered (below). At 2% the two are the same to the bit. It is saved under its own key, an older save reads 2%, and -Dplaytest.inflationTarget holds it for a run. The colours of the header's INFLATION tile read from it, as the strip's did until 0.7.21 (the currency).

So is how strictly it holds it, since 0.7.52. Jerus, rather than chasing the ensemble's failing criteria: "just a very simple fix, aka beside the target inflation, how strict, very strict then it trys to have it below the target, very loose and the target is a suggestion". A ladder of five steps sits under the target's on THE RULE card, set at once like it (DebtManager.Strictness), and each step is two numbers of the rule. A strict step aims under the target, the target taken as a ceiling and the rule struck at the aim as it would be at a target of the aim: very strict a point under (STRICTEST_AIM, which is Expectations.TOLERANCE, the furthest under the target that still counts as on it, so hitting the aim costs no trust), answering the gap at 2 (STRICTEST_WEIGHT), and never aiming under 0%, because no central bank aims at falling prices — so at a 0.5% target both strict steps aim at 0% and differ only in weight. A loose step lets inflation be inside a band either side of the target, holding the neutral rate there and answering only what is past it, so the rate never jumps at the band's edge: very loose two points each way (LOOSEST_BAND, so its outer point is a miss that trust counts and the bank does not answer), at 1.25 (LOOSEST_WEIGHT) — still over one, so past its band a point of inflation still makes money dearer. Strict, half a point under at 1.75, and Loose, a point each way at 1.375, sit halfway in both numbers, and Standard is the rule as it was, to the bit. One weight serves both sides, so a strict bank also cuts harder under its aim. At the default 2% target and on it, very strict sets 4.00%, strict 3.38% and the other three the neutral 3%; at 5% inflation the five set 10.00%, 8.63%, 7.50%, 5.75% and 4.25%. Trust is still judged on the target, and the lean that spares it is measured against what holding the target takes — the Standard rule's advice and the neutral rate, holdingRate() and neutralRate() — so a looser bank on the autopilot loses trust as far as it falls short, and a stricter one can lean no more than all the way. The card says, at the step under the thumb, what the bank aims at and what the rule would set on target and at this year's inflation (PolicyPreview.ruleAt()): Very strict: aims under 2.0%, at 1.0% · on target it sets 4.00% · at this inflation …; and the rule's reason on the card says what the step aims at once it is off Standard. CentralBankCheck's probe city, the playtest's founding in its seed 5's shape to month 600 and then 240 months with nothing built, ran at 2.04% a year very strict, 2.23% at Standard and 3.29% very loose, the dial at most 5.38%, 4.34% and 11.74%, and trust 0.95, 0.95 and 0.82 on average. Over the same branch and horizon seeds 2, 3 and 4 held the same order; seed 1's loose end ran 0.06 points under Standard, and seed 0, a shrinking city with over a fifth of its workers out of work, ran both ends the other way. Played instead from the founding, 480 months, a boom swamps the dial — very strict's peaked at 56.7% against Standard's 38.0% and very loose's 24.9% — and all three came back down without reaching the stop. It is saved by name under its own key, an older save reads Standard, and moving it is a decision on the central bank's record.

Advances, the ceiling, and who is paid

A treasury below zero at the top of the month is advanced its gap at the policy rate, repays from the first cash above zero before anything else, and may owe up to a ceiling in months of its trailing revenue. The emergency note it used to sell its bank is retired, and a save still carrying one runs it off. The ceiling is a dial since 0.7.2 — advancesCeilingMonths, 0 to MAX_ADVANCES_CEILING 36, default DEFAULT_ADVANCES_MONTHS 6, chips at 3, 6, 12, 24 and 36 months on the monetary page — saved under its own key, so an older save reads six, and held in a playtest by -Dplaytest.advancesMonths.

Past the ceiling the treasury pays by Jerus's rule, asked what a treasury past its ceiling does: "pay promises first, cut the rest." Every payment goes through Game.treasuryPays(TreasuryLine, amount). Promises — pensions, EI, health, the schools, the police and prisons, the city's own services, coupons and principal at home and abroad, the interest on the advances, the student loans already lent — are paid whatever it takes, past the ceiling if they must. Discretionary lines — the subsidies, the students' grants, repairs to the city's own buildings — are paid only from cash at or above zero once the ceiling has bound, and what they are refused is owed as interest-free arrears, by line — rows of the Government Overview's CENTRAL BANK card since 0.7.31 — and paid down first when cash returns. Purchases — land, buildings, capital into the bank, reserves, buybacks — are simply not made. The central bank's profit — the interest it takes on its advances and, since 0.7.1, on the paper it holds, less what reserves cost it — is remitted to the treasury the next month as a revenue line; a loss is carried and recovered before anything is remitted again.

It is the floor the overdraft never had. On 0.7.0, held at a 10% dial, the six seeds that had compounded to $193 quadrillion and NaN ended owing $0.1–2.0B, the audit closed every month, and seed 0's bank failures went from 161 to 16; the arrears grew in a straight line rather than compounding, and none of the five that drew recovered. On 0.7.2, with the currency answering the real rate, none of the six draws at all. The default seed on 22 September never drew an advance: it ended owing nothing against a ceiling of $2.71B, six months of its revenue; nor did it on 28 September, against a ceiling of $3.20B, nor on 1 October, against $2.94B, nor on 5 October, at 0.7.49, against $3.63T.

Since 0.7.15 a city can run on them from its first month. Asked whether an Insane city should play before it borrows, Jerus chose "Play runs on advances", and the refusal 0.7.14 had put on the play clock is gone. An Insane city that never borrows pays its land bond's dollar coupon and its founders' pensions in month one from nothing, ends it $6.47k overdrawn, and is advanced the gap at the next settle; its discretionary lines are refused, because a ceiling of no revenue leaves no room, and its private sector pays tax from month two, so a year on its treasury holds $7.1M. The time skip no longer stops at an empty treasury either ("Skip runs too"); it says what the central bank advanced. A failed bank's resolution is paid through the same door since 0.7.14, the advances taking whatever the treasury's cash does not (the bank).

The holdings dial: QE and QT

Quantity, beside the price. CentralBank.targetShare is the share of the city's outstanding term paper — serials and term loans, not notes — the central bank aims to hold, from nothing to MAX_QE_SHARE — half until 0.7.15, because past half the market it is the market, and all of it since, as a backstop (below); chips every ten points on the monetary page, saved, and held in a playtest by -Dplaytest.qeShare. Once a month, in openMarketOperation() — after the treasury has settled with its central bank and before the market is priced, so this month's quotes carry it — the holding moves toward the dial by at most QE_SPEED, a quarter, of the largest of the dial, the setting before it and the share actually held: four months at most, exactly four to or from nothing, so a sale finishes where a share of the gap never would. It never buys at issue, bar its own maturing par since 0.7.15, and it trades only paper that has already been paid for, pro rata, at the curve's market value — the bank's first, and since 0.7.15 the households' after it; buying is money issued and selling money retired; it carries the paper at face and takes what it paid above or below face into the month's profit, so the remittance carries it. The coupons on its share are destroyed and come back as the remittance: debt to itself. The setting before the dial is saved beside it and restored without moving (restoreTargetShare()), because the first build forgot the pace on every reload.

compression(months) = termPremium(months) × min(1, share of the term paper held / FULL_COMPRESSION_SHARE) × QE_COMPRESSION // FULL_COMPRESSION_SHARE .5 · QE_COMPRESSION 1.0

So at 30% held the fifty-year sits exactly 0.90 points under the table and the note has not moved, and at half the term premium is gone. With the playtest's borrowing sent home and the dial at 30% (0.7.1), the central bank bought exactly 30% of each issue's face in four months from the month after its settle, at 0.99 to 1.05 of face, and took 0.90 points off the fifty-year while it held it. It moved nothing else — the runs match the ones without it month for month until the first purchase and drift apart after, trajectory noise rather than a channel — because the playtest's advisor reads only the short end, which the compression never touches. CentralBankCheck sections 11–15 assert the purchase at QE_SPEED × 30% at face and at the market value, M0 moved by exactly it, compression(240) exact and the note untouched, the coupon destroyed and remitted, the dial to nothing selling back within the speed's months, the pace across a save, and a reform that scales the holdings and not the dial.

The backstop. Jerus: "central bank bond holding can ve 100% if one wants" — "for when banks and investors dont want government bonds, its also f bank is in dire state, to not harm it further". Since 0.7.15 the dial runs to the whole of the term paper (MAX_QE_SHARE 1.0). It buys the bank's paper first and then the households', at the curve's value, through the door a household short of money sells by; the compression stays whole from half, with nothing more past it (FULL_COMPRESSION_SHARE); and the city's floor is split by who holds the paper, so that the more of it the central bank holds, the less the bank's state reaches the city's rate (the city's borrowing). And it rolls what it holds: 0.7.15's first build found that when a piece it held fell due, the rollover sold the replacement to the bank, which funded it at the window and failed, so on Jerus's word, "Build the rollover fix", it takes its maturing par again at issue as a non-competitive add-on at the issue's price, par for par, the Federal Reserve Bank of New York's practice; past its dial it lets what it holds run off, as the Fed did under QT; and at 100% it takes the whole issue when the market's part is nothing ("Yes, the backstop"), which the Fed never does. And last year's surplus pays the market's part of what falls due first and then the central bank's par ("Surplus pays everyone"), so a city in surplus clears its paper whoever holds it; when a surplus pays only part of the par, the paper shrinks under the central bank and it ends that month over its dial, and the holdings step sells the excess to the bank — Jerus: "Leave it (as built)".

The default city never owes paper at home, so the dial cannot be measured on it: its runs, and the autopilot's, are 0.7.14's to the byte. Where the dial bites, on eight seeds a setup: a Lean founding at 50% failed its bank 12 times and ended at 159,372 people on average, and at 100% 11 times and 156,877, paying all its paper off again between months 252 and 1,080 with the central bank rolling in only 10 months across the eight; held at 25%, where the spiral is, the city ended at 4,529 people with the dial at nothing, 5,626 at 50% and 1,047 at 100%, with 43, 105 and 61 failures. That first build's better 25% city at 100% — 9,476 people — was a loop: each rollover sold the bank about three times the maturing face for cash, and the central bank bought it back under face and remitted the gain. Par for par ended it. At 100% the central bank is the buyer when nobody else is; it does not limit what the city can sell. A 0.7.15 save with the dial past half, opened in 0.7.14, loads at half, and Jerus left it so. CentralBankCheck's sections 17 to 20 assert the whole of the paper with the compression whole from half, the floor split by holder, the par taken again at issue, and the surplus paying the market's part first.

The defence spends

Until 0.7.2 the vault damped a push on the currency and not a dollar left it: a capacity that cost nothing, which is why the default player never saw it work. Now the central bank sells. On a month the currency is pushed weaker and the city ran a deficit, it sells the vault's capacity times the month's own deficit — its current plus financial account when negative, in dollars at the month's rate, not the trailing window the push reads — and never more than the vault holds. What damps the push is the realised absorption, what was sold over what was wanted, so a full vault damps what its cover says, one running low damps less each month, and an empty one damps nothing. A push with no deficit that month sells and damps nothing; a rise is never met, because leaning against appreciation is buying dollars, which is a decision, not something a vault does by standing there.

push = trade pressure + ratePressure // ratePressure = −(realRateDifferential + EXPECTED_REVERSION × ln(rate / parity)) × RATE_PULL, held within MAX_RATE_PRESSURE either way; the reversion since 0.7.42 absorption = MAX_ABSORPTION × min(1, cover / COMFORTABLE_COVER) // .85 at six months of imports dollarsSold = min(absorption × deficitUsd, vault) // only on a push to weaken, in a month that ran a deficit realised = dollarsSold / deficitUsd rate ×= 1 + push × (1 − realised) × openness × DRIFT_SPEED rate ×= ((1 + credibility × target) / (1 + world inflation))1/12 // the anchored drift, since 0.7.42: see the currency rate += (parity − rate) × REVERSION

A capital transaction, not money destroyed. The buyer is the world, and the local money it hands back is money the pools had already paid out for the imports: the deposit half of the textbook happened the day the goods were bought. So the vault falls by the dollars, the central bank's equity falls by their local price (vaultSpent(), a line under equity on the Money page, spent defending the currency since founding), M0 does not move, no pool moves and the audit declares nothing, and the month's profit and the remittance are untouched. It is booked at the reprice, after the audit, through dollarsSold(), with the month and the lifetime kept apart (getDefendedThisMonth(), getDefendedLifetime()). The first build retired the money instead and took seed 0's M0 to minus $255M, a bank that had destroyed more than it ever made. Trade's reserves page says what was sold this month and since founding, and since 0.7.35 The currency page's WHAT IS MOVING IT draws the forces, the defence among them (the currency).

What it did with the founders' billion, when a city was founded on one (0.7.2), was spend it on structural deficits, not crises. On the eight seeds every one sold more than half of it and six all but 1%, over 22 to 225 sale months, half of it gone by month 628 to 3,747 — while the playtest's advisor buys it back with the treasury's cash, so that one seed sold US$1.29B out of a US$1B vault. MAX_ABSORPTION .85 at a COMFORTABLE_COVER of six months spends about 0.85 months of cover per deficit-sized month, so a vault at six months of cover meets the first deficit-sized month at full strength and damps less every month after, about half as hard five months in. On the default seed on 22 September the defence sold US$1.06B in 174 months, the most US$62.3M in month 725; half the founders' dollars were gone by month 726 and all but a hundredth by month 1,774, the vault ended at US$134k, and $531M of the central bank's equity went on it. Since 0.7.10 a city is founded on US$25M, not a billion (founding a city), and the default playtest's vault was half gone by month 141 against 739 and empty by month 374 against 987, the defence spending US$202M against US$1,189M, while the worst currency month fell from 3.34 to 1.54 local to the dollar (0.7.10). On 28 September the defence sold US$24.9M in 63 months, the most US$3.7M in month 66; the vault was at its lowest, US$973k, in month 973, half the founders' dollars were gone by month 753, $17.3M of the central bank's equity went on it, and the advisor's purchases left US$53.1M in the vault at the end. On 1 October it sold US$39.2M in 114 months, the most US$9.0M in month 3,447; the vault was at its lowest, US$2k, in month 132, half the founders' dollars were gone by month 40 and all but a hundredth by month 51, $31.7M of the central bank's equity went on it, and the vault ended at US$33.0M. On 5 October, at 0.7.49, it sold US$475.3M in 74 months, the most US$68.3M in month 2,121; the vault was empty in month 273, half the founders' dollars were gone by month 38 and all but a hundredth by month 49, $24.3B of the central bank's equity went on it, and the vault ended at US$489.8M. Whether the defence should answer only a crisis is Jerus's.

The demand channel

For the rate to bite at home a household's saving has to answer the real return, and since 0.7.3 it does. What a household spends above a basket a head is scaled by one factor a month, struck on the real deposit rate — the bank's deposit rate less the inflation savers expect, Game.realDepositRate(), and less the year's inflation until 0.7.42 — before the households plan. Deposits and not the curve, because the marginal saver's return is what the bank pays on every balance every month. Subsistence never moves; the propensity's share, the wealth term and the surplus the counter and the table spend out of all scale by it, so a hike does not hand what the grocer lost to the counter — scaling the want alone had pushed the surplus up, and a hike raised spending. At a factor of one every line is the old plan to the bit. Nothing is saved; the load path strikes it again. The monetary page says it in words: "savers earn X% real, so households spend Y% of what they would at zero." Since 0.7.47 its preview of a dial not yet applied reads the same expected inflation (PolicyPreview.realDepositRateAt()), where it had gone on subtracting the year's: in the 2,400-month city it previewed −3.28% at the dial in force against the month's −1.705%, and previews −1.699% now.

spendFactor = clamp(1 − SAVING_RESPONSE × realDepositRate, SPEND_FLOOR, SPEND_CEILING) realDepositRate = the bank's deposit rate − expected inflation // the year's inflation until 0.7.42 // SAVING_RESPONSE 1.0, provisional, Jerus's number · SPEND_FLOOR 0.5 · SPEND_CEILING 1.5

So the chain runs from the dial, to the deposit rate the bank chooses as a share of it (until 0.7.7, a share of what its book and its reserves earned), to the real deposit rate, to the factor, to the plan, to the shelf, which since 0.7.43 clears on what the plan will spend. MonetaryCheck section 6 — the assertion that had waited since the first batch — holds one founding at 3, 10, 20 and 40% from month 25 to 60 and asserts that inflation falls with the rate: each row no higher than the one before within MEASUREMENT_NOISE, and the 3% row at least TRANSMISSION_FLOOR, a point a year, above the 40% row. The noise was 0.05 points a year until 0.7.11, when a ten-year fixed mortgage that locks in the month it is written put the rows held a month late up to 0.053 apart, and on Jerus's word it is 0.10; on 28 September they differ by 0.000 to 0.035, on 1 October by 0.000 to 0.066, and on 5 October by 0.000 to 0.056. 0.7.42 alone put them 0.165 apart and was left red against the 0.100; 0.7.43's shelf brought them back under it on the model's own change. It went green at 1.032 points on 0.7.3, where 0.7.2 read 0.996 and would have failed, read 1.428 on 0.7.7, and read 1.304 points on 28 September. Since then its founding has been made to build a hospital through the window, so that its builders keep their crews (0.7.17), and to put up its bakery, diner and boutique (0.7.18), and on 1 October it reads 4.960 points, with the city at a 3% dial inflating 3.82% a year: a different town from the one 28 September measured, so the two readings are two towns, not a trend. On 5 October, at 0.7.49, on the same town, it reads 8.209 points: at a 3% dial it inflates 6.16% a year and at 40% it deflates 2.05%, and the dear dial's real deposit rate is +4.59% against −3.48% at the cheap one. The anchor and a shelf that clears on money are what moved it (below).

The channel was in, and until 0.7.42 it moved very little

Most of that point was the currency's: the rate moved inflation by making the currency dearer and imports cheaper, which it did before the channel existed. The channel fired every month and reached almost nothing, for four reasons. Weak pass-through. Until 0.7.7 the bank paid savers DEPOSIT_PASS_THROUGH, 45%, of what it earned, not a rate, so the deposit rate carried about a quarter of the dial — 11% at a 40% dial — and the factor ran only from 0.99 to 0.88 across the whole range. Since 0.7.7 the bank passes on a share of the dial it chooses, and on 28 September the same founding's deposit rate ran from nothing at a 3% dial to 13.21% at 40%, and the factor from 1.000 to 0.858; on 1 October the re-caused founding's ran from nothing to 6.52%, and the factor from 1.083 to 0.930. Nothing to act on in the index. Food was four fifths of it, and the shelf did not answer money at the margin: at the founding it sits at its floor on every row, and in a grown city the want is thirty times what the shops can serve, so the scarcity mark-up reads the shops' coverage and a tenth less want moves no price. The counter and the table were not in the index, and that is where discretionary money goes. Saved money compounds: at a held high rate savings earn interest paid in new money, and the wealth term spends a fixed share of a stock growing faster than the factor cuts it. On 0.7.3's default eight the factor's median was 0.994; held at 10, 30 and 50% the six broke seeds swung 1.25×, 1.75× and 2.24× where they had swung 1.24×, 1.90× and 2.01×, and their index ended higher at 30 and 50%. A held high rate does not deflate through demand.

A demand channel needs a price that answers to demand. The assertion was green on the transmission as a whole, not on this channel, and the box said the next rate that bit would be a shelf priced by scarcity against money rather than coverage. That is what 0.7.43 built, on the anchor 0.7.42 laid (below): the shelf clears on what the households would spend at each price, the index has five parts with the counter and the table in it, and a held high rate no longer feeds the savers' trap — on sixteen seeds a dial held at 15% lowers inflation by 2.85 points against its twin, where on 0.7.41 it raised it.

The anchor: what people expect

Jerus: "even if you set central bank to hold 100% of bonds, and set target inflation at 20%, and set the rates at 0% forever, other than at the beginning, you barely get any inflation, is it cause of the type of inflation?" It was. Until 0.7.41 the model made only cost-push inflation — import prices through the rate, and wages chasing the index into rent — and every push ended as a one-off level step, pulled back by money constants fixed in founding money: the shelf's opening price, the price of ground, the cash in every building's cost, the fees. No price in the index read money, spending or wealth; with a 20% target the rule sat at the 0% floor until inflation passed 18%; and under the autopilot a city ran at about 0% a year whatever the dial said. A real economy's anchor is not a constant. It is what people expect, and what they expect depends on whether they believe the bank. Four versions answered it, 0.7.42 to 0.7.45, each built by a fresh implementer on a design that was measured before it was fixed.

Expected inflation (Expectations, 0.7.42) is the target weighted by the bank's credibility plus recent inflation weighted by the rest, never under minus one per cent (EXPECTED_FLOOR), recent inflation being the year's rate smoothed a twelfth of the way a month (ADAPT_MONTHS). Credibility runs from a quarter to 0.95, and a new city, like a save from before the anchor, starts believing the bank at 0.80 (KMIN, KMAX, KSEED). It is hard to win and slow to lose while the bank fights. With smoothed inflation within a point of the target (TOLERANCE) it climbs a sixtieth of the way to 0.95 a month, about five years to most of it (GAIN_MONTHS); off target it falls toward a quarter at up to a twenty-fourth of the way a month (LOSS_MONTHS), in proportion to the miss past the point, full at four points more (MISS_SCALE), and only as far as the bank is not leaning against it: a dial at the rule's advice, or past it, keeps every bit of credibility a supply shock would otherwise cost — since 0.7.52 the Standard rule's advice, however strict the bank, so a looser bank loses trust as far as it falls short of it. A floor of a tenth, lost to shocks whatever the rate, was measured to lock the founding at 10% a year. The expected price level compounds at expected inflation from 1.0 in the month the basket is based; before that everyone expects the target, the level is founding's and credibility waits. The level is drift-only: a save from before 0.7.42 seeds it at 1.0, because its constants are still in founding money, and a catch-up to the index was measured to be a unit root with the floor.

smoothed += (inflation − smoothed) / ADAPT_MONTHS // 12 credibility += (KMAX − credibility) / GAIN_MONTHS // on target: smoothed within TOLERANCE, a point credibility −= (credibility − KMIN) / LOSS_MONTHS × min(1, (miss − TOLERANCE) / MISS_SCALE) × (1 − lean) expected = max(EXPECTED_FLOOR, credibility × target + (1 − credibility) × smoothed) level ×= (1 + expected)1/12 // from 1.0 when the basket is based

What follows it. Every money constant that prices something is struck at the expected level at the top of each month, never at the index, so that no constant reads a price it sets (Game.restrikeMoneyConstants()): the shelf's opening price, the price of ground, every template's cash cost and upkeep, the care and burial fees, the tuition table, the pension's wage base, a founding share's par, the bank's paid-in capital and its account fee, and the fare; and what Game strikes for itself — the fixed grant, the new-home rebates and the issue fee — reads the same level. The numerical guards and grains follow the currency's unit alone, and anything priced in world money is the world's. Wages take half their indexing from expected inflation and half from chasing the index (labour). The real rates are ex ante, the rate less expected inflation: the differential the currency reads, the deposit rate the households save on, the investors' hurdle and, since 0.7.44, the landlords' lender (the private sector). The currency drifts at the credible part of the target, and investors expect a currency away from parity to come back (the currency). And since 0.7.43 every sticky seller's price drifts at expected inflation: the shelf, the two rents and the kitchens' and the boutiques' margins.

The shelf answers money since 0.7.43, which is the demand channel's missing half. Groceries are asked for at a price, at most a basket a head, inelastic, wanted whole up to half again over the floor and capped by the household's money; the shops' price moves a sixth of the way a month, in logs, toward the price that clears that demand against what their coverage lets them hand over, between a floor and a cap half again over it (goods and markets); and the index has five parts, the meals and the luxuries where discretionary money goes among them (the index). Hunger is counted in baskets, and a food assistance dial, nothing by default, pays vouchers to the households the shelf would price out (promises).

What it did, measured on sixteen seeds under five policies, against a score written as code before the first number was seen. Under the autopilot at 2%, inflation from month 240 ran at 0.05% a year on 0.7.41, no seed of the sixteen within a point of the target, and at 2.28% on 0.7.44, from 2.02% to 2.40%, all sixteen within it; 0.7.49's sixteen ran at 2.22%. One import shock, the world's prices up 30% at month 600, is a level step that settles: 12.36% over its twin at the peak, and in its second decade 2.87% a year, 0.41 points over its twin. A dial held at 15% from month 600 now lowers inflation, by 2.85 points against its twin, at a cost of 2.25 points of unemployment, where on 0.7.41 it fed the savers' interest trap and raised it. Every seed took off, earlier than before, and the money audit closed in every month of every run. Three criteria still fail, each for a reason that was measured. A 0% dial held from month 600 in a city meant to be slack ran at 7.07% a year, because the city at month 600 was taking off; held from month 1,800, measured on 0.7.43, it ran at 3.16%. A 0% dial held in a hot young city gives a persistent burst and never an accelerating rate, because the wage curve has no growth term and credibility floors at a quarter. And a boom can still hold year-on-year inflation over 10% for 12 or 13 months, against a limit under 12, as a shelf runs from its floor to its cap. All three are Jerus's to confirm (open questions).

On the screen since 0.7.45, the header's INFLATION tile reads expect x% · trust 80% under the rate, exp x% · 80% where it is tight, and NEEDS YOU has a PRICES row, amber in a month credibility fell and red when it fell under half (CityNeeds.TRUST_RED), its reading the miss and how far the dial sits from the rule's advice — the Standard rule's, and named so off Standard, since 0.7.52; the row reads the move, not the level, so that it does not stay red through the recovery a player buys by leaning against the miss. Policy › Money draws THE ANCHOR beside THE CURRENCY'S DRIFT, and the hub flags Trust is falling. City History keeps expected inflation, credibility and the expected level among the 23 series 0.7.45 added. ExpectationsCheck holds the anchor in nine sections: before the basket is based; credibility won at GAIN_MONTHS on target; a miss nobody leans against costing it and a lean keeping it; the floor; the level compounding at expected inflation; the wages half and half; the money constants at the expected level; the currency's level for a real gap and its anchored drift; and the anchor through a save and a save from before it.
