The central bank's month is not saved, so after a reload the Money page's this month lines read nothing until the month turns; that is on the list too.

16

Prices, the currency & the world

The city has a price level of five parts, chained every ten years, a currency with a name that drifts at what the city believes of its bank, a vault of dollars, a balance of payments, hot money that comes for the spread, a rent market that no longer follows wages, and — since the surplus had nowhere to go — somewhere for its savings to sit.

A price index, and prices that ration

A fixed basket, priced every month, is the city's price level, and it does not re-weight to hide inflation. Until 0.7.43 it was the shelf price and rent, weighted by what households spent in a base month two years after the city started shopping, and fixed for good. Since 0.7.43 it has five parts (PriceIndex): groceries, rent, meals, luxuries and the services, the services being a fee index of its own over the care fees, tuition, the fare and the bank's account fee, each weighed as its fees were paid. The weights are what the households spent over the trailing twelve months (WEIGHT_MONTHS), luxury held to 15% of the basket and its excess spread over the rest pro rata (LUXURY_WEIGHT_CAP) — uncapped it would have been 63% in the 2,400-month city — and a part nobody bought holds its last price. Every 120 months (REBASE_MONTHS) the basket is struck again on the year before and chained to the old one at the level it reached, so the level runs on unbroken and a year's rate runs through the link; a one-month rebase was measured to give food a weight of nothing in a crash month. A save from before 0.7.43 waits for its first month to link its basket, at the level its own old basket strikes. The basket is first fixed only after 24 months of real shopping with both food and rent in it (PriceIndex.SETTLING_MONTHS), about month 37 in a Standard city, because a city based earlier had a basket that was 96% rent and never saw food prices; and the first year-on-year rate comes a year of readings after that, about month 50. Until then every screen and the central bank read inflation as zero. The play-through of 0.7.19 read the price card's no year yet at month 13 as a bug; since 0.7.20 it counts down, rate in ~37 mo, a floor while the basket is not fixed and exact once it is, and Jerus chose to "Keep the countdown" over fixing the basket sooner or adding a provisional reading. The shelf price was cost-plus and rose to 1.6× at total shortage until 0.7.43; since then it is a floor and a clearing price half again over it at most, moving a sixth of the way a month in logs, so a short city spends more at the shops for fewer goods by what its households would pay rather than by a count of heads (goods and markets), and People's WHO GOES SHORT, AND WHY says who went short and whether for money or for stock.

The index remembers its own high and low water-marks, and the month each was set. An end-of-run figure says where a city finished, not what it lived through: a run that ends at 0.98 having been at 1.31 and 0.62 is not the same city as one that never moved, and until the marks were kept there was no way to tell them apart from the outside. The pair and the swing between them are carried in the save; a city saved before the marks existed opens with both sitting on today's level, which is the only honest thing to say about a past nobody recorded.

Rent is two markets: family homes and studios, each with a cost floor from the cheapest template that serves that segment, at today's cash and materials prices spread over a landlord's yield of 5.8%, times that segment's households per front door, unbounded both ways, closing a twelfth of the gap a month because a lease is twelve months — in logs since 0.7.43, and drifting at expected inflation as it goes, so a landlord who expects prices to rise writes the next lease dearer with the market standing still (Retail.stickyPrice(), the one form every seller in the city prices by). Taking one floor across the whole portfolio and applying it to both legs was what turned rent into a constant — every building earning the same $445 a head whatever it cost, and a city that built 138 low-rise blocks and no studios with 1,847 households wanting one. Struck per segment, studio rent went $445 to $706 and studio doors over 4,000 months went from zero to 19,920. Land is deliberately not in the floor: land price is a capitalised rent, an output of this market, and feeding it back in grew a number because it grew. Overbuild and rent falls below cost and the landlords eat it.

The city's dollar, and what moves it

The local currency was the Danzik dollar, D$, until 0.7.10, and since then it is the city's own: named after the city by default — a city founded as Arden pays in the Arden dollar, A$, ARD — or by hand, with a three-letter code that is never USD, and a save from before loads as Danzik, in the Danzik dollar, D$, DZD. This page writes D$ for it, against the US dollar. Since 0.7.2 its rate moves on three things: pressure from the balance of payments; the real rate differential, Game.realRateDifferential() — the policy rate less the inflation the city expects, its year's inflation until 0.7.42, against the world's base rate less the world's realised inflation — at RATE_PULL 4 a point, so money moves toward the higher real return; and a pull toward relative parity (REVERSION, a fifteen-year half-life) that says where the rate should end up. The drift that moved it every month by the whole of the inflation differential, rate *= 1 + (local − world) / 12, is deleted. With imports repricing through the rate, wages chasing the whole index and the shelf cost-plus, a gain of one on that link meant a city whose prices rose 40% a year saw its currency fall 40% a year by rule, whatever its central bank did; Jerus's 0.6.9 city, 399× its founding prices in twenty-five years, was that loop run for 306 months. The inflation differential still reaches the currency, through parity, which rises with local prices, and through the trade balance a cheap or dear currency earns — but not month by month, by rule. A spiral needs an actual outflow to continue, and a credible real rate stops it: in CurrencyCheck, ten months of 40% inflation with the real gap at nothing move the rate to 1.0067, the parity pull alone, where the drift would have taken it to 1.3649. The world has its own inflation, a persistent moving average drawn low in 1–8%, deterministic, with its level pulled back to trend so that 333-year runs stay usable.

Since 0.7.42 a drift is back, in the one form with no inflation in it: every month the rate moves by the credible part of the city's target against the world's realised inflation, (1 + credibility × target) / (1 + world inflation) a year (Game.anchoredDrift()), and by nothing before the basket is based (the anchor). And investors expect a currency away from parity to come back, at 0.15 of the log gap a year (ForeignAccounts.EXPECTED_REVERSION): the rate's pressure is the real gap less what a strong currency is expected to give back, so a sustained real gap moves the currency to a level, e−gap/0.15 of parity (uipLevel()), a three-point gap 22% from it, rather than a drift for as long as the gap lasts. Both were measured before they were fixed: with the expected inflation in the real rate, a 0% dial under a 2% expectation is a two-point negative gap for good, and without the reversion it compounded into a currency falling every month; with no drift at all the currency ended at 0.43 to 0.46 of parity and growth halved; and a drift at the whole of expected inflation ran at 41–66% a year with the dial held at 0%. The reversion also bounds what new money leaks abroad.

current account exports − imports − interest paid abroad − foreign savers' interest financial account what the city's owners send abroad, less what comes home position reserves − foreign debt // revaluation lives here, not on any statement real rate gap (policy − expected inflation) − (world base − world inflation) // RATE_PULL 4 a point; the city's year's inflation until 0.7.42 its pressure −(real rate gap + EXPECTED_REVERSION × ln(rate / parity)) × RATE_PULL anchored drift (1 + credibility × target) / (1 + world inflation) − 1, a year hot money spread = max(depositRate, cityRate) − worldRate − countryPremium // at most MAX_SPREAD

The caps on the channel are numerical guards now: MAX_RATE_PRESSURE went from .8 to 4.0 and binds only at a hundred-point real gap, and the hot money's appetite, CapitalFlows.MAX_SPREAD, from .06 to .25, measured, until 0.7.7 took the strain premium out of every rate, against the city's rate net of it (getRateBeforeStrain(), gone with it), because what a strained bank charges is not a return. RATE_PULL went from 6 to 4, the one retune the batch allowed itself: at six the loop's gain was 1.44 against the deleted drift's 1.0, and one seed whose dial sat at 0.08% went to the guard with its prices at 115× founding; at four the gain is 0.96, and no default seed goes near a guard. The cost is that a credible real rate buys two thirds of the support it did. The channel is only as good as the hand on the dial. Since 0.7.35 Trade's currency page draws the forces as the model previews them, WHAT IS MOVING IT, NEXT MONTH: THE PRESSURE as bars either side of a line, stronger to the left — the trade term, the real rate and the two together — with the vault's defence and the openness under it (trade ≥ output (100%) when the model's clamp holds), and WHICH COMES TO, the month's push, the anchored drift since 0.7.45 and the pull back to parity, adding to next month's move exactly (ForeignAccounts.previewPush(), previewDrift(), previewPull(), previewMove(); ForeignCheck asserts the sum to 1e-12), in the money blues and never a verdict's colour, with where the real rate alone would hold the rate and what the anchor hands it beside them. What the reprice was handed rides the foreign accounts' saved array, the defence at slots 25–27 and the real differential with its two inflations at 28–30, so the page survives a reload.

And since 0.7.3 there is no guard a player will meet. ForeignAccounts.MAX_RATE and MIN_RATE were 100 and .01 and pinned the currency — the last acts of Jerus's year book were a currency at its guard with imports no longer rising — and they are 1e9 and 1e-9 now, scaled by a reform: a guard against a rate run to nothing or to infinity, never a price the game expects to see. Past a hundredth of a cent the rate turns round and says what a US cent costs — the strip's second line did, and since 0.7.21 the RATE tile's (i) does — US¢1 = D$1,000 at a rate of 100,000, instead of printing 0.00¢, and every page prints a rate through one formatter (Money.fxRate()). On the default seed on 22 September its dearest dollar was 1.000 local, in month 2; it spent no month weaker than twice parity and none at a guard, prices averaged +0.05% a year, and the run ended with the rate at 0.482 local to the dollar, 53% under a parity of 1.016. On 28 September its dearest dollar was again 1.000 local in month 2, with no month weaker than twice parity and none at a guard; prices averaged +0.04% a year, and the rate ended at 0.632, 37% under a parity of 1.006. On 1 October its dearest dollar was 1.428 local, in month 137, with no month weaker than twice parity and none at a guard; prices averaged +0.02% a year, and the rate ended at 0.571, 39% under a parity of 0.929. On 5 October, at 0.7.49, with the anchor, its dearest dollar was 813.952 local, in month 3,985: the currency drifted with prices that rose 1,213-fold, spent no month weaker than twice parity and none at a guard, prices averaged +2.15% a year, and the rate ended at 806.260, 23% under a parity of 1,053.126.

The vault

The founders leave one. Jerus: "of the 3.5B you start with, 1B is in usd in the reserve, so you only see 2.5B start with" — because 99% of players would never buy reserves. From 0.6.10 to 0.7.9 a new city opened with D$2.5B in the treasury and US$1B in the vault, and since 0.7.10 it opens with D$100M and US$25M, the IMF's old three months of imports for a town of eight to ten thousand (founding a city); the dollars are bought on day one at the opening rate and booked as the purchase they are (Game.FOUNDING_CASH, FOUNDING_RESERVE_USD), and Trade's reserves page says where they came from among WHAT ELSE MOVED THE VAULT: the founders left US$25M in it. It is kept in dollars. Checking the founding reserve found it in the wrong currency: it was a local figure at the price paid, so a hundredfold fall turned a US$1B vault into US$10M and its import cover fell with it, while the dollar debt beside it was revalued every month. The stock is dollars now, its local value the dollars times the rate, cover no longer moves with the currency, and the month's move is a revaluation line, not cash and not an audit flow. The treasury still buys and sells reserves, and since 0.7.6 the land office can be paid straight out of the vault (land); the central bank reads the vault as its asset and, since 0.7.2, spends it defending the currency (the central bank).

The reserves page is in 0.7.35's shape. WHOSE IT IS draws the vault as one bar with the dollar paper and the parked money eaten out of it, what is left the city's own (Game.getOwnReserves()), and the treasury's position, the vault less the paper, which was called the net position. HOW LONG IT WOULD LAST is months of imports on a band, red under three (ForeignAccounts.THIN_COVER, the model's constant since 0.7.35, where the world prices a currency for a crisis) and amber under six (COMFORTABLE_COVER), with what three and six months would need; since 0.7.38 one formatter says it on Trade, Finances and the drawer alike, under 0.1 months rather than 0.0, over 10 years from 120 months and none with an empty vault (Money.coverMonths()), and the drawer colours it on the same two lines. BUY OR SELL FOREIGN MONEY is on 0.7.34's action button in the money blue, to 3 months and to 6 months among its steps, with what it would do to the cover before the press.

A reserve defends a currency; it does not hold one down. Jerus's rule, chosen from three after the founding reserve caused the very year book it was meant to prevent. The vault used to damp the pressure on the rate both ways, so a deep one muted the surplus a weak currency earns and the support of the policy rate — the two forces that pull a currency out of a spiral — and with the founders' dollars in it three of eight seeds reproduced Jerus's city, two of them past 140× their founding prices with the currency at its guard. It damps only a push weaker now, and the eight went to a median price swing of 1.60×, none past 2.81×, with sudden stops 8 to 0.

And the header carries them. From 0.6.10 to 0.7.20 a strip did, three panels between the population and the cash: prices against founding over inflation year on year; the rate both ways in the currency's own names, US$1 = D$x over D$1 = US¢y, grey at or above parity and down to 5% under it, amber more than 5% below it and red past 25%; and since 0.7.4 the price of money, Jerus: "on the top of the UI the bank rate, the central bank rate, both should be shown, as well as the rate you borrow in" — three lines at the caption's weight, central the dial, bank the bank's lending rate on it, reading no bank or failed in red when the bank lends nothing, and city what the treasury borrows at. The inflation readout was grey within three points of the 2% target, amber outside it and red past 10% either way, and from 0.7.15 read from the player's target. Since 0.7.21 two of the header's tiles carry them, six tiles then and five since 0.7.24 (the screen). INFLATION reads the rate against the player's target, or rate in ~N mo before the first rate comes; it is green within three points of the target either side, amber further off, and red more than five points over it (STRIP_INFLATION_OVER_TARGET, Jerus: "Red at target + 5 points") or with prices falling faster than 10% a year, whatever the target — so at the default target inflation of 7 to 10% reads red, where until 0.7.15 it read amber; and since 0.7.45 the line under it is the anchor, expect 2.2% · trust 82%, plain at rest, amber in a month trust fell and red when it fell under half, NEEDS YOU's PRICES row (the anchor). RATE shows the dial, with what a US dollar costs under it, and since 0.7.35 on the one parity rule the drawer's THE CURRENCY row and the Trade tab read too: grey within 25% of parity either side, amber from 25% and red from 50% (ForeignAccounts.PARITY_WATCH, PARITY_FAR, parityLevel()), grey when pinned. Until then it was grey near or above parity, amber more than 5% weaker and red past 25%, so a currency 25–50% stronger than parity turns it amber where it was grey, and 5–25% weaker reads grey where it read amber; the bank's prime and the city's rate are on its tooltip, and the three rates, the currency both ways and parity behind its (i). Every figure comes through a getter, and the header recomputes nothing.

The surplus, and where it goes

A city that exports steel used to run a surplus of 30–50% of GDP with nowhere for it to go: no household bought anything abroad, nobody was paid a dividend, and no saver could own a foreign asset. The currency simply appreciated — about 10% a year, 1.20 down to 0.44 over 250 months — until every exporter in the city was dead, twenty times a run.

Outward investment is the door. A sector with an idle till buys the world's paper when the world pays more than the bank — forty times the spread, up to nine tenths of its wealth, moving 4% of the gap a month out and 10% home — and brings it home to fund a plan, pay a dividend or clear an overdraft. Households run the identical rule on their own savings. The coupon is rolled where it is earned, an income credit matched by a financial debit, so a rentier's surplus does not appreciate the currency, and the currency is priced on the whole balance rather than the trade half. The median run went from 33% below parity to 3.5%; exports went $33M to $124M a month. Since 0.7.12 a sector that owes nothing also puts a share of its wealth into the other sectors' bonds by the same rule, never its own, and still most of it goes abroad: after 0.7.12's eight default runs the companies held $6.7T abroad against $1.7T in their tills.

And since 17 September the households spend abroad too, through three doors the surplus did not have. A car the city did not make is an import at the ceiling; a watch always is, deliberately, so that wanting luxuries is money that leaves rather than going round again, and import substitution, if it ever comes, is something a player achieves; and every tonne the railway hauls burns fuel priced in the world's money, charged as a real import before there was any oil good, so that a reform could not forget it. A fourth door since 0.7.49: every journey a household drives to work burns $2.00 of fuel in the world's money at the rate (Motoring.CAR_FUEL_PER_JOURNEY), forty a month, an import Trade named as the households' fuel (transport). Since 0.7.62 both are FUEL, a good the city can make: the drivers and the railway draw it off the refiners' tanks first and import the rest, so with no refinery the bill is what it was at the world's price level, and a refinery narrows the door by what it makes (the private sector); Trade lists fuel among its goods, the households and the railway among its buyers. A meal is the exception — the food in it was already counted when it crossed the border, and the margin is wages and rent here.

Hot money comes for the spread net of the risk, at 8% of the gap a month, and leaves at 20%, straight into and out of the bank's deposits — which are its lending capacity, so the boom and the sudden stop are procyclical by construction. A run needs a fragile position (a material stock of it with under 25% backed by reserves) and a shock: the currency down 12% in a year, the bank insolvent, or a recent default. Always painful, never fatal.

Who owns the bank is a curve of financial deepening: a city with no savings has no domestic investors, a city with $124B on deposit owns its own banks. The bank's capital follows that curve. Its wholesale funding followed it too, which is what stopped a $156B book declaring its entire funding cost as interest paid abroad, until 0.7.0 moved the funding to the central bank's window, where the interest is paid at home and declared as money destroyed.

The policy rate, and dropping zeros

The policy rate is the player's dial, with an advisory Taylor rule beside it saying what it would do and why, obeying the Taylor principle — and since 0.7.0 an autopilot that hands the rule the dial; the dial itself, and everything it now moves, is the central bank. The playtest works the dial a quarter of the way toward the advice each time, because a rate that sat at 3% for three centuries would leave the whole monetary half untested. And once the city's own price index passes ten times founding, a currency reform unlocks: divide everything by 10, 100 or 1,000, the player's choice, never automatic. Every money constant in the game is an instance field seeded from its constant and redenominated with everything else, and since 0.7.42 struck every month as its founding figure over the unit times the expected price level, so a reform needs nothing new: the unit and the level are both in the product (the anchor). Foreign prices are the exception, quoted in someone else's money and reached through the rate. Since 0.7.47 a reform divides the month's transit bill and fares too, and since 0.7.49 the households' fuel; and the fare dial is read in founding money through the unit, so a reform no longer moves who rides, its cap being the founding cap in the new unit (TaxPolicy.maxTransitFare()) — in the 2,400-month city divided by a thousand, a month on, 41,394.6 riders and 11,077 households eligible for vouchers, the unreformed city's figures exactly, where they had read 43,627 and 49,778. The reform instant is bit-exact and conserves money; the following month is a few percent adrift in the discrete decisions, which is filed and has a plan.

17

Founding a city

Until 0.7.10 every city was the same city at month zero: Danzik, founded on D$2.5B and US$1B, with no reason ever to borrow. A player founds a city now — its name, its money, what it starts with and the world's inflation — and the default start is small enough that the city borrows for what it builds.

Jerus: "lets reduce the cash the city starts with, both the foreign usd and the starting cash, what do you think makes sense? cause the city should borrow right". A trace of 0.7.9 found the treasury never going below D$2.467B in thirty years: the young city spent $0.1–2M a month and took in $0.4–5M, owed nothing for about ninety years, and held a vault of 270 or more months of its imports. The endowment had been sized on 9 September to buy the opening, a coal power plant, and nothing since had asked it to do less. Since 0.7.10 a city is founded on D$100M and US$25M, Jerus's choice from the options put to him (Game.FOUNDING_CASH, FOUNDING_RESERVE_USD), sized so that the treasury could pay for the playtest's founding village and one of the first big works, and the city would borrow for the rest, the way towns pay for long-lived works, on bonds repaid over the asset's life — Britain's New Towns were built on sixty-year loans from the Exchequer, not on endowments. The vault follows the IMF's old rule of three months of imports. The endowment is still a game number, and the code says so.

The village is the playtest's, not the player's. The playtest places a founding village by hand before its advisor takes over — sixty houses, five shops, two farms and a depot — and the play-through of 0.7.19 found that a player's city gets none of it: after Found the city the treasury still held D$100.0M, and month 3 had one house and thirteen people. A player's city starts with the treasury and the vault, and builds the rest. The sticker is not the invoice. What a new city pays for a building is the building and the material beyond its yard at the world's price, and since 0.7.19 the builders' sales tax on both: the water plant's D$65.8M sticker was invoiced D$109.6M and is D$129.0M with the tax. So D$100M pays for the village, D$38.8M, and one of the wind farm, D$36.5M, the school, D$35.3M, or the police station, D$52.9M; the option Jerus picked had been described on stickers, and he kept D$100M once it was described on invoices, so the water plant is a bond from day one. That is why the build screen offers a twenty-year bond sized to the gap (the city's borrowing): as 0.7.10 measured it, a Standard city a year on was D$26.1M short of the water plant, issued D$40.3M of face at 4.22%, raised D$26.13M, paid about D$56k a month, and had the plant in 57 months; on 1 October the same fixture was D$58.3M short, issued D$113.8M of face at 7.19%, raised D$58.4M, paid D$253k a month and had the plant in 137 months; and on 5 October, at 0.7.49, it was D$61.8M short, issued D$137.6M of face at 9.54% on a 3.45% coupon, raised D$61.8M, paid D$395k a month and had the plant in 146 months, with the audit closing every one (NewGameCheck, section 11). Since 0.7.42 every price on the page is struck at the price level the city expects, which at its founding is founding's, so the invoices a new city is quoted are the ones above.

Found a city

Jerus, the same afternoon: "a small thing at the start of the game where you choose the name of the city and the currency … and perhaps the settings to choose the starting cash and usd cash and offworld inflation rate … (with option to just start at default)". From 0.7.10 Start New Game opened Found a city (ui/FoundingScreen): the city's name; its money, named after the city or, with a tick, by hand, a name and three letters that are never USD; the money it is founded with; the world's inflation, moved there from Settings because it acts only at founding; and Found with defaults, which founded straight away. Each start showed what it would buy. Jerus, on the play-through: "even the screen shouldnt say what the money could buy, the start screen should be real simple". Since 0.7.20 a start shows only what the founders leave, and since 0.7.21 New city on the main menu opens one panel over its dimmed skyline: the name, in a field that opens on Danzik; one line for its money, "Its money: the Danzik dollar, D$ · name it yourself", where the link opens the name and its three letters in place; WHAT IT STARTS WITH, four cards, each its money in figures and one line on how hard it is, never what it buys, Standard marked default, and Set your own amounts for Custom; THE WORLD'S INFLATION, a row of five, with "A faster world makes your money stronger. It can't be changed later." and the rest behind an (i); and Found the city and Back. Found with defaults is gone, because the page opens on the defaults. None of 0.7.21's panel has been seen on a screen.

Since 0.7.56 the panel founds a world as well. THE WORLD, between the money and the inflation, is a seed: a whole number in a field in the figures' face, rolled when the page opens and again by a dice beside it, from 1 to 999,999,999, over "The same number makes the same coast, lakes and ore." Any whole number typed is a world; anything else keeps Found the city dark with "Type the world as a whole number, like 4127, or roll the dice." The defaults, the presets, the harnesses and the playtest found on world 4127 (Founding.DEFAULT_WORLD_SEED), so that they stay deterministic. What a seed makes is in land. The panel grew to about 710 pixels for it, under the 768-pixel window it is laid out for, and the row has not been seen on a screen either.

The starts, in the order the page lists them (Founding)
StartTreasuryVaultWhat it is
InsaneD$0US$0"Nothing. The ground is owed on a 20-year bond." Since 0.7.14: the ground it stands on owed abroad, a twenty-year dollar bond at 3% for US$2,100k

StandardD$100MUS$25M"The usual start." The default, sized on the playtest's village and one of the first big works at their invoices

The choice is a record on the city (Founding), set once, saved under eight keys and since 0.7.56 a ninth, the world's seed, and never changed; the currency is a value per city, read through game.getCurrency(), because one process holds several cities — the harnesses', and the playtest's reloaded twin. The window's title and the save lists name the city, and every screen that prints its money names it. A save from before 0.7.10 loads as Danzik, in the Danzik dollar, founded on D$2.5B and US$1B, which is what it was; and one from before 0.7.56 is given a world made from its name, its founding treasury, its ground and its month (Founding.derivedWorldSeed()), the same at every load, which its next save keeps. A new game also founds on the autopilot and with the rollover on same structure since 0.7.13, with the bank's resolution automatic since 0.7.14, believing its central bank at 0.80 with prices expected at founding's since 0.7.42 (the anchor), and with its fund's withdrawal at one step, Norway's 3% a year, since 0.7.48; an older save keeps what it was played with, and one from before the anchor or the dial reads those two defaults. Building the screen found two bugs on the founding path: a new game after a load kept the loaded world's inflation, and a new city's first year was back-cast at the previous city's rate.

Insane

Jerus: "on game start, add a new difficulty called Insane, which is just you start with 0 cash, 0 vault, and a 20y bond 3% for the initial land cost … if easier the starting debt is abroad in usd". Since 0.7.14 it is the first start on the page: D$0, US$0, and a twenty-year dollar bond at 3% owed abroad for the starting land — 3,000,000 square feet at the land market's opening price of US$0.70 a square foot, US$2,100k. No cash moves, because the founders' land was bought on it, and on the world's curve it is worth US$375k on day 0. On day 0, as FundCheck's section 12 prices it, a city with no revenue and no output pays the full spread: the founding card quoted the village's twenty-year bond at 13.90%, $85,500k of face to raise $32,978k, the six-month note at 13.00%, and the window abroad shut, until 0.7.20 took every figure off the founding screen. Since 0.7.21 Insane's card reads nothing for its money, and what is owed abroad for its ground is in its tooltip. 0.7.14 made the clock refuse to run until such a city had borrowed (Jerus: "Borrow first"); since 0.7.15 it runs on the central bank's advances from its first month instead (the central bank).

Of 0.7.14's eight Insane seeds, three became cities of 148,000, 206,000 and 148,000 people, and five spiralled on the city's own paper, ending with 3,686 to 10,118 people and $420T to $6,416T of it; the playtest's player borrows exactly the village's invoice, so every seed ran on the advances from month 3. Lean is hard too: D$25M covered three quarters of the village then, and two-thirds since the tax went into the invoices; every seed borrows by month 4 to 14, and on 0.7.10 half of them ran out of cash around month 140 and lived on the advances for 450 to 500 months.

What the smaller start did

As 0.7.10 measured it, making the founding a choice changed nothing else: founded Wealthy, the city reproduced three old ensembles byte for byte, all 24 seed-runs. The default playtest never borrows on D$100M; its treasury bottoms out at D$61–67M in its first two years and then grows. What the default run does show is the vault, half gone by month 141 against 739 and empty by month 374 against 987. Under the autopilot the bank failed 76 times against 49, and all of the rise was the vault's: D$100M with the old US$1B vault gave the old counts, and D$2.5B with US$25M the new ones, because without dollars to steady the currency a rule-held rate sees more imported inflation in the city's middle years. Held at 10%, every seed now runs dry in years 185 to 253, where three of eight did before: a city held at that rate loses money for centuries, and the D$2.4B had been paying for it. And 42 fixtures in 25 harnesses had been living on the old treasury without saying so — ten went red and three were green only because they ran on a short calendar — and each funds itself explicitly now, with no premise and no tolerance moved.

18

Land

A city stands on a world of its own: an Earth-sized square made from one number, its sea, coast and lakes and seven resources in finite fields, every one of them there to be seen. It starts with thirty blocks of that world's dry ground, allocated so that a power station and a water plant leave two spare — land is taught on turn one — and buys the rest band by band from forty offers that are never rerolled.

The world

Since 0.7.56 every city stands on a world of its own (World): a flat square the size of the Earth, 368 cells a side of 61.44 km, 511.2 million km² against the Earth's 510.1, with the city founded at its middle. It is made from one number, the seed chosen on the founding page (founding a city), and the same seed makes the same world on every machine: every value is a SplitMix64 hash of the seed and the place, and every sine and logarithm is StrictMath's. It is measured in plots of 30 m, nested by powers of two — a tile is 32 plots, 0.96 km; a district 256, 7.68 km, which is what the map keeps (below); a cell 2,048 — and its ground is value noise in octaves: sea below a level each world finds for itself, so that 71% of it is sea, as the Earth's is; lakes on 3.7% of the land and forest on 31%, the measured shares of each (Verpoorter 2014; FAO 2020); and beaches at the shore. The founding site is the first point, spiralling out from the cells 25–75% land nearest the middle, that is dry at its centre and 600 m round, at least 60% land within 5 km, has the sea within 2.1 km but not within 1.35, and has an iron field within 2 km: a coast, with a lake beside it and a river that runs from a lake 64 to 90 plots away past the site to the sea. A world is built the first time it is asked for, in about half a second, and WorldCheck holds three of them to the design (how it's checked). Two of the design's levels moved before it shipped: its percentiles had been taken at lattice points of every fine octave, where the noise is a bare lattice value, and made 5.0% of the land lakes and 24% forest, so the lake level went from 0.695 to 0.7092 and the forest's from 0.595 to 0.5676, the shares being the intent.

Seven resources lie in it, in finite fields, and every one of them can be seen. Iron, oil, stone, coal, copper and uranium lie in fields (Resource, Deposit): a cell holds a Poisson count of them by its land, each field a place with a number of sites — most small and a few huge, the floor of (1 − u)−2/3 capped at 512, 2.52 on average — and whole tonnes that add up exactly to the cell's own total, which is drawn first; a field centred in the sea is drawn again, up to eight times, bar oil, because offshore oil is real. Forest is the forest of the terrain. Nothing has to be surveyed or found: the fields are in the world from the seed, the land office counts every one centred in an offer when it lists it, and the map draws them. The world's totals are counted once, in about 30 ms, and kept with the city, so that for each resource the world = what no city owns + what the city has left + what it has taken out, to the tonne through every purchase and every tonne lifted (LandCheck). What the city takes out is worked out in the order it bought the ground, the centre first, and forest grows back a 240th a month, though nothing cuts timber yet.

The seven, as the world lays them (Resource); an offer's price counts only those with a good
ResourceFields a km² of landA siteA site holdsIn an offer's price
Iron ore0.20.0372 km²15 Mt1/350 of its world price, the $0.40 a tonne the office always charged against the floor of $140; a site is an Iron Mine's own ground, and a km² of land carries 7.59 Mt, the old listing's ore on a quarter of its sites
Oil0.020.0405 km²150,000 t5% of crude's since 0.7.62, by iron's rule of about a year and a half of a working site's profit; a site is a well's ten-acre spacing and thirty years of its 415 t a month
Stone0.010.1 km²13.5 Mtfree until it has an industry; a quarry 50 m deep
Coal0.0021 km²6.5 Mtfree; a seam 5 m thick
Copper0.00050.5 km²300,000 tfree; the metal in 120 Mt of ore a km² at 0.5%
Uranium0.00020.1 km²200 tfree; 1 Mt of ore a km² at 0.2%
Forest——13,700 m³ a km²free; the terrain's forest, 31% of the land (FAO 2020)

A centre, four sides and forty offers

The centre is a square round the founding site holding exactly the dry ground the city was founded with — 3,000,000 sq ft, about 0.29 km² in all on the default world — sized ring by ring out from the site so that it holds that ground to the square foot (CityLand, 0.7.57), and it never moves: nothing rolls into it. What the city owns in square feet is its dry ground; the water inside its land is its own too, the lake its water plants draw on and the sea a desalination plant needs (utilities). The four sides are each ten lanes fanning out from the site, wedges read across the side, and the city owns a point when it lies inside the centre or inside its lane's frontier; forty lanes bought evenly make a square with straight edges. An offer is a lane's next band, from its frontier out, so there are ten a side and forty in all (LandMarket.OFFERS_A_SIDE), every one touching the city, and they are never rerolled: an offer stands at its size, its contents and its price until it is bought, and buying it moves that lane's frontier out and lists the band beyond while the other 39 stay as they are. Its size is a multiple of one block or of 1% of the city's whole area, whichever is more (LandMarket.unitKm2()), the multiple drawn from the offer's id as the parcels' sizes always were — 55% between 1 and 2.5, 30% between 2.5 and 5, and 15% tracts of 5 to 18, a mean of 3.81 — so a new city sees the sizes it always saw and a big one is not offered scraps: by the design's count ten billion people is 411 to 444 purchases. What an offer holds is measured once, as it is listed, from 64 by 64 samples of its band — its km² in all, dry, fresh, sea and forest — with every field whose centre lies in it. One over 70% sea stays listed, because sea is cheap, but is tagged MOSTLY SEA and is never the best.

Until 0.7.57 nine plots were on offer at any time (LandMarket.LISTING_SIZE; earlier editions said ten), generated deterministically from the plot's own id, so parcel 47 was the same plot in every session and in every reload. Sizes were multiples of a floor that rose with how much the city already owned; 15% of plots were enormous tracts, and the tract was what made the listing worth reading, which is why the offers keep it. The lanes are about to go. A block grid lined up with the map's districts, each offer a rectangle of whole blocks against the city's edge and still ten places a side, is designed and not built (7 October), so the wedges are described here only as far as a player meets them.

Whole fields

Jerus: "Yes whole iron fields as one offer, yes that means significant investment." A field — every one of its sites and all of its tonnes — belongs to the one piece of ground that holds the field's centre, the centre or one band of one lane, wherever its sites lie, so the centre and the forty lanes' bands hold every field exactly once and the world's totals stay exact whoever lists what when. It was the design's rule at 0.7.57; from 0.7.58 to 0.7.63 a field was shared site by site among the ground its sites lay under, so that a new city could buy a single site of its founding field for about US$5.3M; since 0.7.64 it is whole again, for every resource alike. On the default world the founding field is 35 sites and 449 Mt, its centre 26 plots out in the lane South 3, and a new city is offered no iron in its first ring: pushed out to it, South 3 lists the field whole, one offer of US$179.9M against a founding treasury of D$100M, and the land office's funding page sizes its bond to it. An offer's row says so — "Iron ore: 35 sites, 449 Mt in the ground: every field of it centred in this offer, whole, paid for in its price." — and the map's hover names whose a field is, since its sites can lie under another offer. The playtest's player buys its iron the same way since 0.7.64: when an Iron Mine would pay and every iron site the city owns has a mine, the cheapest offer holding iron, out of the cash when that covers it and on the funding page's twenty-year bond when the player's test for borrowing passes (LongPlaytest.ironWhenNeeded()). Its first was East 7 at month 485, a one-site field 33 plots out, 12.8 Mt for US$6.16M out of the cash, where at 0.7.62 it had bought a shared site in month 32.

Two prices, moved by different things

what the world asks US$0.70 a sq ft × the world's price level × the crowding premium // since 0.7.55 an offer's price that on its dry ground, 45% of it on fresh water and 8% on sea, + its fields' tonnes × their share of the world price × the world's price level, to US$5k, fixed at listing what the city pays the offer's dollars at the day's rate what businesses pay the local base × the crowding premium × a scarcity multiplier from 0.65 to 1.90 on allocated/available // the local base struck at the expected price level since 0.7.42 (LandMarket.getBasePricePerSqFt()); the dollar base is the world's the crowding premium 1 + 119 × s, s = 1 / (1 + (4,000 / d)5.3), d = people a km² of the land the city owns

The multiplier passes 1.0 at about 61% built — below that the city sells land at a loss, which is the intended cost of over-annexing. The earlier version multiplied by utilisation, which sat between 86% and 100% for 2,400 straight months, so "scarcity" was decoration. From 9 September the city was founded with a $3.5B endowment — D$2.5B in the treasury and US$1B in the vault from 0.6.10 — enough for a coal plant at 41% of it, a works department at 400 points a month, a shed of material, and a bank standing on day one. Since 0.7.10 it is D$100M and US$25M, and the big works are borrowed for (founding a city).

The premium is crowding, not size (0.7.55). Jerus, of the scale study's proposal: "Yes for land price do that". Until then it was 1 + .008 a block owned + .05 a thousand residents, linear in the city's size: a copy of his city ten times over saw its rents rise 3.9 times and lost 18% of its people in thirty months, and at ten billion people a square foot would have cost about 19,500 times what it cost him. A city's size is not what makes its ground dear; how hard it presses on it is. So the premium reads the city's people a square kilometre of the land it owns (LandMarket.peoplePerKm2()), through a curve fitted to the three cities held so that each was quoted on the day about what its size had given it — city600 3.67 to 3.74, city2400 35.07 to 35.99, Jerus's 103.39 to 104.00: 1.65 at 1,500 a km², 60.5 at the midpoint of 4,000, 117 at 8,000, and never past 120, where the size premium had no ceiling (CROWDING_MIDPOINT, CROWDING_STEEPNESS, CROWDING_CEILING). A copy of a city K times over, K times the people on K times the land, is quoted exactly what the city is. And the dollar price follows the dollar's own inflation (Jerus: "usd inflation not domestic inflation"): the base times the world's price level (WorldEconomy.getPriceLevel()) times the premium, so the city's own inflation reaches it only through the rate — in Jerus's old city the dollar price rose 14.4% on the day, US prices having risen 1.137 times since his founding. What businesses pay keeps the domestic anchor, because ground inside the city is a domestic thing bought with domestic money: one price would have put every swing of the rate into every business's ground, and would have cut his businesses' by 47% on the day, his currency standing about 1.9 times above parity. The two prices meet at parity, so the city's margin on land is the currency's distance from parity and the scarcity, nothing else, and the land office names the price's parts. Crowding is a consequence and not a cause, as 0.7.58 measured: a city kept short of ground is crowded and quoted dear, but with the premium held at one the playtest's city grew slower, not faster.

Land is bought in dollars

Jerus: "when you buy land, make it so that it costs USD not domestic currency, and basically how it would work is a little toggle at the top to choose, when you buy land, to use up your USD reserves or to convert cash into usd exactly to buy the land, and the default is that you convert." Since 0.7.6 the land office prices every parcel in US dollars — the same base and premiums, so at the founding rate of 1.00 every number is what it was — fixed when it is listed, and the treasury pays usd × rate on the day it buys: a weak currency makes land dear and a strong one cheap. What businesses pay the city stays local money, struck exactly as before, so the city's margin on land carries the currency, and a currency reform no longer clears the office's board. There are two ways to pay, a chip pair at the top of the office. Converting, the default: the treasury buys exactly the dollars and hands them over in one movement, and the vault ends where it began. From the vault: the dollars leave it and no local money moves, a capital transaction like the defence's, and the treasury's journal carries the land back against the budget's land line. A vault too short for the plot spends what it holds and converts the rest, and the receipt says so. On the default seed on 28 September the advisor paid US$10.2B abroad for land over 151 months, none of it out of the vault, and it cost D$6.15B at the day's rates, 39.6% less than it would have at the founding rate; on 1 October it paid US$10.6B over 149 months, none of it from the vault, for D$5.95B, 44.1% less; and on 5 October, at 0.7.49, US$14.7B over 166 months, none of it from the vault, for D$3.99T, 26,961% more than at the founding rate, its currency having drifted with prices that rose 1,213-fold; and at 0.7.64, on the world's land, US$33.1B over 141 months, none of it from the vault, for D$3.91T, 11,704.5% more, prices having risen 775-fold.

Since 0.7.13 the office speaks the paying currency and square kilometres — Jerus: "for the land … instead of blocks, say km^2, and add a button to buy multiple". A plot's large price is in local money when converting and in US dollars from the vault, with the other as a caption beside it; areas read in km² on the exact international foot, to three figures, so a block is 0.00929 km², and so do the build screen's Not enough land page and the time-skip report. Since 0.7.26 the office leads with square feet, to three figures (9.68M sq ft), with the km² after them, and the left panel's LAND header reads N free · D$x/sq ft, only its Owned and Free lines in km²; since 0.7.61 it reads square kilometres again — the city's size, each offer's and its price a dry km². A plot the city cannot afford no longer refuses: its button opens a funding page — converting, the twenty-year bond sized to the gap and the six-month note; from the vault, a twenty-year dollar bond or a six-month dollar note sized to the dollar gap, whose dollars are held in the vault, or the old top-up from cash as a third card; with the window abroad shut, a red card across two columns with the reason, and only what remains. Since 0.7.26 the page is the office's own, Land office › Funding, with the rail lit, a summary bar of what is held against the gap and a short by pill, and the offers as cards (Pieces.offerCard(), which Build › Funding took up in 0.7.40), and it goes back to the office when a month lands and nothing needs funding. And Buy the next N plots, five by default, buys the office's first N, cheapest ground a square foot first, and since 0.7.61 is Buy the best N, from every side; because a listed offer's price is frozen, buying them at once costs exactly what buying them one by one does. Since 0.7.34 every Buy is a button that says its price in the money the toggle pays in, the card's width: Buy · D$1.0B; outlined, Buy on credit · D$1.0B, when only a loan pays; from the vault and short, Buy · US$200.3M with the vault is short: ways to pay under it; Buy the next 5 the same in its line, and not drawn with nothing listed; and the funding page's offers Buy 5 plots · D$9.8B over issues the 20-year bond, then buys — or issues it abroad, then buys from the vault, or takes what the vault has and converts the rest. A double-click buys one plot: until 0.7.34 the second click bought the card that had slid into the first one's place. Converting for land has never pushed the currency, which is on the list. Since 0.7.22 the player's hand on the queue reaches the ground too (the builders): a stopped shell and a demolition on site hold their ground, a finished demolition's ground comes back to the city as free land, and a buy-out pays the owner for its ground at the price businesses pay the city, recorded as a buyback. Land purchases are not on City History's lane of decisions, as build orders are not; a big one the treasury borrowed for shows as the borrowing.

Ground is what a mature city runs out of — land sits at 88–90% for the last decade of most long runs, and the 2,400-month playtest city measured at 0.7.26 was 95% used or more in 120 months of 120 with no sector waiting, which is why no screen colours the share used any more — and the newest sectors are the hungriest for it. A Rail Terminal is 5,500,000 square feet, nearly three times a Coal Power Plant and the largest footprint in the catalogue; a Livestock Farm is 120 acres, the dearest ground of any farm at $108k an acre; a Metro Line is 30,000 square feet for thirty thousand journeys a month, five hundred times a gravel road's capacity per acre. That ladder is the whole reason the modes exist — see transport. Every sector's planner assumes the ground is free and none of them can see they are bidding for the same land; that is on the list.

The office redrawn

Jerus, of the screens still to be done after Build: "the others are still full of text and the design could be more intuitive and fun". The land office was the first redrawn in Build's style (0.7.26), and it answers top down whether the city has room to grow and which ground to buy. THE GROUND is four cells — the ground free, who is waiting, what the world asks and what investors pay — over the ground as one bar, the free ground solid and the next N plots as numbered ghosts, on a scale of the two together so that the bar always reads. GROUND FREE takes NEEDS YOU's GROUND row's colour, amber at a block or less and red at none (CityNeeds.ground()), and so do Build's LAND FREE and the left panel, whose Land alert shows only at none: never the share used, which sits at 95–100% for centuries with nothing wrong, so a red USED at 90% went. What the world asks is the going rate, the listing's median a square foot (LandMarket.goingUsdPerSqFt()), and who is waiting is read from the sectors the month found blocked (Game.getLandBlockedSectors()), where it had matched the words no land and missed a sector whose ground went between its plan and its purchase. ON OFFER, until 0.7.61, was the nine as a three-by-three of wide cards — from 0.7.57 the best nine of the forty — the next N numbered with a pink edge, each with tags of its own: BEST VALUE on the first, the cheapest ground a square foot with any ore inside its price, which is not bestValue(); MOST ORE on richestDeposit(), since 0.7.57 richest(IRON); ORE and NEW; and one card can carry BEST VALUE and MOST ORE. Prices are neutral, red only when no way pays without debt. Under them four cards say what the ground is worth — the margin, the ground on top of the build, the ore and who is waiting — and behind details the world's price of ground over the city's life. The receipt is in the screens' money, for US$101.8M, converting D$72.0M of cash at D$0.7072, where it printed US$101,800k. Nothing the office said is gone: what is not on a card is behind an (i). LandCheck's last section, since 0.7.26, holds the model reads it rests on: the going rate the median, the upper middle on an even count and nothing with nothing listed; the GROUND row's level its free ground's, word for word the row NEEDS YOU lists, a city 90% used still fine; and the receipt.

The office round the map

Since 0.7.61 the office is laid round the city's map. The hero row is the map, 600 by 400, the city's land and its forty offers hatched over the world, with Expand to lay it over the whole window (below); and beside it THE CITY: its size in km² — in Jerus's old city 107.8 km² · 89.6 dry · 0 fresh · 18.2 sea — and its share of the world's iron, of the world's 1.12 Pt of iron, the city owns 0.0000452%; the four sides as chips, each with its cheapest dry ground a km² and BEST VALUE on the best's side; and the chosen side's ten offers as rows of 28 pixels, each its lane, its km², its dry ground, fresh water and sea as one bar, its deposits as 8 · 136 Mt, its price in the money the toggle pays in, its price a dry km², one tag — BEST VALUE, then NEW, MOST ORE and MOSTLY SEA, the first that holds — and Buy, or Fund when only a loan pays. A row lights its band on the map, and a click on the map picks a side or an offer. Under them THE GROUND and its ghosts, Buy the best N, the worth cards — the ore card is Ore and oil — the details and the funding page are as they were, the funding page sized to the offer: converting, its bond is quoted for exactly what the chosen offers cost at today's rate less the treasury's cash (Game.landCashGap()), and from the vault for what the vault is short of their dollars, and once it is issued the Buy buys them. The 0.7.26 value bar went for want of room in a row. Build buys from the same office (Game.bestOffer()). LAND FREE reads in km², with Buy the best land · 3.4 km² · US$12.1M › under it: the most dry ground a dollar among the offers the city can afford that are not mostly sea, the nearer on a tie. The no-land page's Buy the best takes the cheapest offer that covers the shortfall with bare ground, no ore to pay for, and says so — "West 10, bare ground with no ore to pay for, covers the 0.0929 km² short" — or, with none, the best value, and asks again; a building short of ground has not asked for ore, and on the literal rule, the cheapest offer that covers it, the playtest's village was sold two sites of iron for US$11.4M where bare ground cost a tenth of that, and stood still for two hundred months. The no-deposit page's Buy the cheapest takes the cheapest offer holding the ore, since 0.7.64, as the playtest's player does, where from 0.7.61 it took the most sites a dollar; and the no-coast page the cheapest offer with sea. Short of cash, each opens the office's funding page sized to that offer.

Iron is a pooled resource. Until 0.7.57 a parcel carried a deposit count and a tonnage the office drew for it, and roughly 28% of ore parcels carried more than one site; since then the sites and the tonnes are the world's fields in the ground the city owns. The sites cap how many mines can stand, one a site, and every mine draws from one reserve, what the city's fields held less what has been taken out (LandManager.getIronReserveTonnes()), worked out in the order the ground was bought. Ore in the ground is worth a fraction of what it fetches once it is out — the value is in getting it out, which is why it sells at 1/350 of the world's floor — and a deposit that is worked out closes its mine inside a year, which is what makes the bust when a mining city runs dry a real event rather than a slow leak. Oil is the same since 0.7.62, a well a site.

The map of what the city has

Since 0.7.60 the city keeps a map (CityMap), and since 0.7.61 it is on screen. What it stores is not a picture but a count: each type's buildings in each 7.68 km district of the city's land, with the district's free plots and the iron and oil sites the city owns in it. Each month, once the month's construction and demolitions are done, each type's change goes into the first district with room — nearest the founding site first, but farms, utilities, mines, the railway and the car plants farthest first — removals the other way round, and nothing placed ever moves; mines and wells stand on the city's own sites of their resource, the first not worked out in the order the ground was bought. The map is drawn the first time anything asks for it, so a city nobody looks at pays nothing, and it is saved beside the city as slot-NN-map.bin, deflated, with its stamp in the save; a load whose sidecar is missing or stale draws it again, and the picture shifts once. What it draws is what the city has, no more and no less (0.7.64). Jerus: "a brand new city shows that it has a few houses and a shop when it doesnt". A new default city holds one building, the Commercial Bank it is founded with (Game.foundingBank()), and no road, where 0.7.60's map had drawn homes from its people and workplaces from its jobs, the bank as a shop, beside two highways of the world's. Every building the model has is drawn once, as its own type, on whole plots sized to its type's land — the square root of its plots across and the rest down, a House one plot, Low-Rise Apartments two by three, an Iron Mine six by seven, a Livestock Farm 23 by 23 (BuildingVisual.footprint()) — turned to face its road, a mine on its own site; and the model's roads are laid as their own plots by kind, a Gravel Road 46, a Paved Road 26 and an Elevated Highway 7, and no others. A tile is painted from the seed, its ground, what is owned, what it was dealt and its neighbours' roads (TilePainter), so it looks the same every time it is drawn: buildings of sixteen plots or more first, then the roads, grown from ports the next tile agrees on and bridging fresh water but never the sea, then the rest, each from its own hashed place beside a road. One that no free box on its tile can hold is drawn smaller, and counted: 7 of 2,340 in Jerus's live city at month 416, and at most a tenth on the densest screen MapCheck draws, 9.30% of it. A year on, a new city's map is its eleven houses, its store, its diner and its bank.

On screen it is one canvas (ui/MapView, its arithmetic in the model as MapFrame, LandMap and MapTiles so that MapCheck holds it): small in the land office and, with Expand, over the whole window, where a drag pans, the wheel zooms by 1.25 a notch at the pointer from the whole world to 16 pixels a plot, + and − zoom, 0 fits and Esc closes, with a legend and a hover card — the ground, whose it is, an offer's size and price, the fields under it and their tonnes, the building or road. It draws at the mockup's levels: painted tiles from 3.2 pixels a plot, at 4 pixels a plot and at 8 past 6; blocks of four plots from 1.4; blocks of eight below that while the view holds no more than 1,024 tiles; and past that the districts as tinted nodes over the world's ground. Tiles paint a few a frame, at most 8 ms of it, the middle of the view first and another level's picture standing in; a month or a purchase stamps the tiles in view again and repaints only those whose stamp moved; and its caches together stay within 48 MB at the design's size, 47.8 measured. The first draw is made off the screen's thread, on a copy of the city's land and counts, and taken back on it caught up to the month (Game.mapDraft(), adoptMap()). Its cost follows the screen, never the population: a 198-tile screen paints and rasters in no more than 80 ms with Jerus's old city as it is and at 9,814, 10,000 and 19,629 times it — five and ten billion people — 59.5 ms at worst and each within 1.5 times the five-billion copy's, and a month's change at five and ten billion costs no more than 5 ms. Room is counted plot by plot, about 8 ms a district, and only in the districts a purchase touches, but a large save loaded without its sidecar counts every district once. The view has not yet been seen in the game's own window; what has been looked at is the painter's renders.

Older cities on the world

A save from before 0.7.57, format 30 or older, is put on a world once, as it loads (LandConversion). Its seed, if it has none of its own, is made from its name, its founding treasury, its ground and its month (founding a city); its site is found as a new city's is, with one test more — the square holding its ground must be at least 80% dry, with the sea within half its side and 2 km — over the 64 coastal cells nearest the middle, failing all of which the driest site found; its centre holds exactly the dry ground it owned; its iron is its own, the sites it had or at least its mines standing and on order, and exactly its tonnes, none invented and none yet taken out, with a field laid at least 1 km out if the world put no iron in the centre; and its nine parcels go, for forty offers at today's prices. The three research cities convert in about a second each: city600's 3.03 km² of dry ground in a centre of 3.20 (95% dry), city2400's 33.29 in 40.98 (81%), and Jerus's old city's 89.63 in 107.83, 83% dry and 18.2 km² of it sea, its 155 iron sites under 21 mines and 508 Mt; each saved, loaded and played a month on comes back the same. Since 0.7.59 a save with no water rights is given what its fresh plants already pump less what its lakes yield, so that loading idles nothing it had — Jerus's old city 300,000 units, having no lake in its centre, city2400 960,000 and city600 none (utilities). A save whose square feet disagree with its land — a copy of a city K times over, or ground set by hand in a harness — has its land drawn again to hold them, its iron and what was taken out kept. Saves made from 0.7.58 to 0.7.63 keep their offers and purchases as they were listed, sites shared, and are not converted: there are only the last days' saves, and nothing marks which offers were shared, so a field split between a bought band and one listed since can count twice, or the rest of it never be offered.

19

The catalogue

Seventy-six buildings in buildings.json, hot-editable next to the game, every one on a sourced real capital cost at the size the game actually builds it. Edit a number, restart, look — no rebuild. Ids are permanent, because saves are keyed by them; the next is 76. Eighteen were added in the three days to 18 September, and three since the last edition: the Desalination Plant (0.7.59), the Oil Well and the Oil Refinery (0.7.62).

CategoryTemplatesWhat it does
Residential3House (six), Studio Apartments (two), Low-Rise (four) — a home has a size and a household has to fit it
Commercial3Convenience Store, Small Grocery — coverage is how many people can shop — and the Commercial Bank
Industrial5Industrial Bakery and Bakery, which buy crops and bake bread and bakery goods out of them (until September 2026 they were a Textile Mill and a Food Processing Plant making a good called Food out of nothing); and the eleventh sector's Meat Works, Snack & Oils Plant and Bottling Plant — five, six and four posts, among the smallest investor-built job steps in the catalogue
Heavy industry6Steel Foundry and Steel Mini-Mill, plus manufacturing's Fabrication Shop, Fabrication Works and Machine Works — the first domestic customer steel ever had — and since 0.7.62 the seventeenth sector's Oil Refinery, 8,300 t of crude a month into 8.3 million litres of fuel
Business services3Contact Centre, Shared Services Centre, Engineering Services Office — seats sold abroad; the last needs licensed engineers before it will open
Agriculture4Mixed Farm, Grain Farm, Greenhouse Complex and, since 16 September, the Livestock Farm — 13.8, 55, 3.4 and 120 acres; the glass is the one that survives expensive ground, and the herd is the dearest of the farms per acre — $108k against the Grain Farm's $22k — and sells 6.5% of its build cost a month against the Grain Farm's 18%
Mining2Iron Mine — sixty jobs, a quarry lifting 30,000 t/yr — and since 0.7.62 the sixteenth sector's Oil Well, three posts lifting 415 t of crude a month; each stands on a site of its own resource that the city owns
Construction2Construction Depot, and the Construction Materials Plant — the seventh sector's only building
Electricity2Coal Power Plant — 280 MW at $1.43B, far more than a city of 74,000 needs — and a Wind Farm, the first small plant beside it
Water2Water Treatment Plant, which treats no more than the city's lakes and river yield since 0.7.59, and the Desalination Plant, the same water from the sea at twice the cost and 10,880 kW
Infrastructure6Gravel Road, Paved Road, Elevated Highway — each the cheapest per trip across a band of land prices — and since 16 September the Bus Network, Light Rail Line and Metro Line, the same ladder two rungs on: 2,500, 8,000 and 30,000 journeys a month, and the first things in this category that ever had a payroll
Rail3Rail Spur, Freight Line, Rail Terminal — 50,000, 200,000 and 500,000 tonnes a month across the city boundary at $140M, $520M and $1.24B, owned by the twelfth sector and financed like a steel mill; a terminal is 1,482 jobs on 5,500,000 square feet, the largest hiring commitment and the largest footprint in the game
Automotive4Vehicle Works and Assembly Plant (120 and 500 cars a month), Commercial Vehicle Plant (150 vans), Locomotive Works (four wagon sets) — all fed on fabricated steel and machinery, and anchored on heavy manufacturing's revenue per head rather than the food plants', because the first draft never cleared the interest test
Luxury2Boutique and Department Store — 320 and 4,200 pieces a month over a counter, importing every one of them
Hospitality2Diner and Restaurant — 13,500 and 40,500 meals a month, each buying the Convenience Store's basket scaled to the person-months its meals come to, to the kilogram
Healthcare14childcare, general, senior and death care, on Build since 0.7.24 as four rings, the picked ring's buildings as cards
Education9three basic stages, college, university, and four professional schools that each license one job
Safety4two police buildings and two prisons — the city pays for all four

Every building declares a cash cost, construction points, materials, maintenance, capacity, lot footprint in square feet, electricity and water draw, road load, its job mix across eleven types, and — since the template — which sector owns it and what it makes and uses. A healthcare building also declares what it treats, a school what it teaches, a safety building whether it is a station or a cell block, a transit line the journeys it can carry and a rail building the tonnes. The road load is one number and it is read as three: fitted to the catalogue itself, 0.726 × jobs + 0.878 × homes + 0.0386 × tonnes a month, so a mine keeps its 420 and learns that 68% of it is ore, a university is all commuters, and a shop's freight is a van at a back door. Money throughout is in thousands: a cash cost of 30 is $30,000, at founding prices, and since 0.7.42 struck every month at the price level the city expects (the anchor). Every building prices itself — sticker and all-in, from the same method that charges, since 0.7.19 with its labour at today's builders' wages and their sales tax in it — on a card that explains it from its own numbers.

The Build tab files it differently. The table's categories are BuildingType's, and since 0.7.24 Build files the catalogue in fourteen of its own (BuildAdvice.categories()): the city's five — Utilities (electricity and water), Roads & transit, Healthcare, Education and Safety — then, after a rule, the market's nine — Homes, Shops, Industry (industrial, heavy industry, mining and construction), Offices, Farms, Rail, Vehicles, Luxury shops and Restaurants. Five were renamed as Jerus chose, "Yes, rename them": Residential is Homes, Commercial Shops, Industrial Industry, Infrastructure Roads & transit and Business services Offices, on Build's strip alone. Since 0.7.25 the market's pages draw their buildings in their owning sectors' groups: Industry's seven, nine since 0.7.62 — food mills, food processing, steel, fabrication and machinery, iron, oil, refining, building materials and the builders — and Shops' two, the groceries and the bank's branches. BuildAdviceCheck's first section holds that every building sits in exactly one of the fourteen.

One card for every building

Jerus: "also the build card for every building, i think the card itself needs a redesign dont you think?" Since 0.7.25 every one of the 73, 76 since 0.7.62, is one card, 300 pixels wide (BuildCard), the city's five and the market's nine alike: the head, an icon square in the colour of who builds it, you have N and what is on site with its wait and a link to the site; the tags; a hero that says what the building gives the city in its own verb and unit, with a detail line; $X all in · sticker $Y, always both; two bars scaled within its group — the first the price a unit of what it gives, or for the makers, farms, mines and vehicle plants how many months of value added at today's prices it takes to pay for itself, an office's months of its exports, and the second the land a unit on market cards, the posts it could not fill per 10,000 served on the city's, and of 100 posts how many the city could not staff on offices — with no track in a group of one; the investors' line, on market cards; the needs line — posts, the city could staff N%, the land and its share of what is free, runs $X/mo; the stepper with +10 and +100; the quote and its verdict; and the (i) with its stat cover. A tag goes only to the strictly best on a bar, and only in a group of two or more. Each group's note reads the sector's own figures — the doors, the shops' coverage, the counters, the kitchens, the rail's trade, each maker group's demand and nameplate. The quote warns of a missing deposit, no iron deposit and since 0.7.62 no oil deposit, a missing coast, no sea owned to draw (0.7.59), or a licence, nobody licensed, before the click.

The investors' line says on site when an investor's order is on the site, and otherwise the sector's word for the month, read since 0.7.30 into one of twelve kinds (the private sector), then in amber this one: and the first gate this building fails now, in the order the build path asks them — the deposit, the licence, the staffing test, the land, a loss in the estimate; a click opens the owner's Investors page. The words are the month's and are not saved, so after a load or a founding the line says nothing was recorded until a month runs. Build is a button that asks to be pressed since 0.7.34 — Jerus: "everywhere you have build, like the build button, it should be more intuitive aka like an actual button that is basically asking to be pressed, cause currently its a tiny text". It is the card's width under the stepper and says the order, Build 3 · $37.5M, the count and the quote's all-in total; outlined, Build 3 on credit · $37.5M, when the treasury is short, a press opening Build › Funding (the city's borrowing); grey and saying why when it cannot go ahead — No land free or Short 476k sq ft of land, No iron deposit or No oil deposit, No sea to draw, Nobody licensed to work in it — with the way out under it and a press opening the page that explains; and at none Build · choose how many, where a press chooses one and prices it and never orders. A double-click is one press. It had been a small grey button beside +100, disabled at none. Since 0.7.41 a city card's figures are served, supply over demand, under one verdict (the screen). BuildCardCheck holds the cards in ten sections, without a screen: every building has a card and a unit; the hero and the bars are the model's; the tags; the notes; the investors' line and its gates, each caused by a fixture in the build path's order; the verdict; since 0.7.30 the word's kind; since 0.7.40 a run of orders priced as placing them in turn would be; and since 0.7.41 served. The cards have not been seen rendered.

Both halves of a price are sourced now

A unit of construction material was $2,000 and is $18,000, defined by the House: ten units against a real house's $180,000 of structure. Every template's split was re-derived at a real material share — 40% for a building, 25% for a plant, 60% for a road — with every total held to the dollar. It also found that the builders' material import bill had never been paid: $2.75bn crossed the border over 4,000 months and the expense struck at zero, so Construction ended every run holding the money and the balance of payments never saw a unit move. Range runs from a Home Daycare with two staff to a Rail Terminal with 1,482 jobs, the Regional Medical Centre's 996 the largest the city pays for, and the terminal's 5,500,000 square feet the largest footprint in the game, ahead of the Livestock Farm and a Municipal Cemetery's 2,000,000.

20

Utilities & roads

Power and water

The grid starts with 10,000 kW free and the reservoir with 8,000 units of 10,000 gallons. Both are gated by the same utility workforce. Power is $10 a kilowatt a month and water $50 a unit — the model's .01 and .05, in thousands, as the Services books print them, and about $5 per thousand gallons. Since 0.7.28 power reads in kW, MW and GW (Money.power()) on Services and Build, and the console's utilities report in kW, where earlier editions and the report said watts.

Only businesses are invoiced. Houses, depots, roads, the water plant's own 900 kW and the city's own buildings all draw power nobody pays for, and since 0.7.28 that unbilled draw is split into the homes' (UtilitiesHandler.getHomesElectricityDraw(), getHomesWaterDraw()) and the city's own buildings'. Booking revenue on the whole city's draw was money from nowhere scaling with the housing stock — 73% of it, measured. The utility books exactly what its customers' statements charged them, not a recomputation at the end-of-month ratio. The plants are the city's own: what they sell less their wages goes into the city's cash each month, and a loss is paid as the city's services promise (TreasuryLine.CITY_SERVICES). Since 0.7.41 both read served, supply over demand, unclamped, under one verdict: green at 100% or more and off NEEDS YOU's list, which for the networks means past 133%, the inverse of the 75% the dashboard has always called tight; red at or under 100%; amber, tight, between (the screen). A city drawing 43% of its plant reads 233% served, enough, and one at 124% of capacity 80% served, short.

Roads

The city starts with 400 units of capacity — the streets that already exist. Traffic flows freely to 90% of capacity and degrades after that, floored at 35%. Three roads, and the land ladder was in the catalogue before anybody pointed at it: a gravel road is 500 square feet per unit of capacity, a paved road 208, an elevated highway 42 — twelve times the capacity per acre for two and a half times the money. Since 16 September the load is three streams and the network can be relieved by a highway, a tram or a train, but never deleted; that is transport, and this is the baseline it is measured against.

throughput = 1 if load ≤ 0.9 × cap // FREE_FLOW .9 = max(0.35, 0.9/util) otherwise // MIN_THROUGHPUT .35 served = cap / load the curve reads // since 0.7.41: flow is 1 from 111% served, and 0.9 × served under it

The road is written as one pair everywhere since 0.7.29, in whole per cents, and since 0.7.41 as served rather than full: 62% served · 56% flow where it read 161% full · 56% flow, the same fraction the other way up. Its red line is the curve's own, 111% served, where the flow starts to fall, so a road 100–111% served is red and tight where the networks go red at 100%. Since 0.7.29 the room before it slows and its spare capacity read the load the curve reads, where they read the raw trips: 585 trips of room on a road 161% full.

Congestion slowing the sites is the pointed part: a jammed city builds the roads that would unjam it more slowly, and the floor is what keeps that from being a trap.

Fresh water has a limit, and the sea can be drunk

Since 0.7.59 the Water Treatment Plants together treat no more than the city's lakes and river yield: 121,600 units a month a km² of fresh water the city owns (UtilitiesHandler.FRESH_UNITS_PER_KM2), plus its water rights. The yield is the world's renewable river runoff, about 42,700 km³ a year (Shiklomanov), over the world's river and stream area, 773,000 km² (Allen & Pavelsky 2018): 4.6 million m³ a month a km², which is 121,600 units of 37.854 m³. So a water plant's 60,000 units need about half a km² of lake or river. The reservoir's 8,000 units are groundwater, the old wells', and stand outside the limit. Past it the rest of the fresh plants' nameplate idles and the water is rationed through the same ratio as any shortage, which throttles the businesses that draw it; under it, what the plants make is the old formula to the bit.

the limit 121,600 × owned fresh km² + the city's water rights water made (8,000 + desalination's nameplate) × fill + min(the fresh plants' nameplate × fill, the limit) // fill: the utility workforce's, as before; at no fill at all the wells still give their 8,000

The Services page says so in amber under the water bar — "fresh water limit: 62% of the plants' nameplate idle · buy lake or river, or desalinate" (CityNeeds.freshLimitLine()) — and holds its at full staff to the limit, and the build advice values a fresh plant at the water left under it, so a capped city is offered desalination. The Desalination Plant (id 73, "source": "SEA") makes what a water plant makes, with the same crew and ground, at twice its cost, the low end of seawater reverse osmosis's two to five times a conventional plant; and it draws 10,880 kW, 3.5 kWh a cubic metre against the water plant's own 0.29, the biggest single new draw on the grid, 1.34 wind farms of it. The limit does not reach it, and it needs the sea: an order with no owned sea is refused, No sea to draw (BuildResult.NO_COAST), and the refusal's page buys the cheapest offer with sea (land). Rights keep the old cities whole: a save from before the limit is given what its standing fresh plants already pump less what its lakes yield, so that loading idles nothing; a new city has none. A new default city owns about 0.011 km² of lake, a limit of 1,324 units, so its first water plant would idle 98% until it bought lake or river; at 0.7.59 the playtest's city had 1.1 km² of it by its first shortage, bought with its ground, and at 0.7.64 it was never offered a move past the limit, ending with 16.68 km² of lake and river, two water plants and none for the sea. The playtest's player orders a water plant only while the water left under the limit covers it, and then desalination on owned sea, the offer with the most lake or river a dollar (LandMarket.bestFresh()), or the cheapest with sea.

Construction output

round(working capacity × construction fill rate × road ratio × health work ratio) working capacity = the city's own 400 points + the depots' capacity × the share of their posts offered // since 0.7.17

One definition, used by the screen, by the sites, and by what the sector is paid for. The three had to be the same number: a copy that drifts books revenue for work nobody did, and very nearly did when roads arrived. What reaches the sites is what is left once the standing stock has had its repairs — about 40% of the total, permanently — and since 0.7.17 it is shared among them by the crew each building can use (the builders).
