The depots count at the share of their posts the builders kept on that month, and the city's own works department, which has no posts, counts whole; whoever is weighing an order reads the same figure with every post offered (getConstructionOutputAtEveryPost()), because an order is work and work hires the crews back. Since 0.7.22 a rushed site's crews work longer hours on top of it, more points in their first two months and fewer from the third, and the builders count them beside the output.

21

Transport & vehicles

Until 16 September the entire transport model was one scalar: a road network with a capacity and a load, one throughput ratio between 0.35 and 1, and three road types that were the same road at three prices. Jerus: "decomposing the existing import/export bands into world price + transportation cost… then later, transportation efficiency can make that freight component smaller or larger." Nine batches followed in three days under the same version number, each one there to make the next possible: the band decomposes, the load splits, the modes and the fare, the railway, the vehicles, and the screen that finally shows a player any of it.

The band was a freight bill

Every traded good carried two constants and the gap between them was the whole local market — and nothing in the game said why the gap was the width it was. Each good carries a third number now, the cost of moving one unit, and the two ends split into four:

worldImportPrice = worldBuyPrice + baseFreight // the world's own ask, plus getting it here worldExportPrice = worldSellPrice − baseFreight // the world's own bid, less getting it there

Three quarters of the wedge is freight and one quarter the world's margin, and the split is a game decision rather than a measurement: it sets how much of the band good logistics can ever win back. At three quarters a 23% band bottoms out near 6%. The price table was already a freight model and nobody designed it that way: freight as a share of the world price sits at 21–25% for fifteen of the seventeen goods that had both ends, and the two outside that band are iron at 49% and materials at 43% — the two lowest-value-density things in the game, where real freight genuinely is half the delivered cost. The delivered prices are the stored literals and the world band is derived, which is the opposite of how it reads and the only way round that is exact: storing the world band and adding the freight back gives 0.8470000000000002 for steel and 0.27999999999999997 for crops, and an ulp in this codebase is never nothing. Not one price moved on the day it shipped, and eight seeds over 333 years came back byte-identical; TradeCostCheck asserts every delivered price against a table of what it was that morning, because a test that reads its own subject proves nothing.

One road load is three

Traffic { COMMUTERS, GOODS, BULK }, and which mode can carry what falls out of the classes rather than needing a table of exceptions: commuters can ride a tram and nothing else can; goods — a shop's bread and milk — are a van at a back door however many rails a city lays, and take the train only across the boundary; bulk — ore, steel, crops, grain, aggregate, beams, machines, a locomotive on a low-loader — is what a railway is for. The shares were fitted rather than guessed, by least squares against the catalogue itself:

roadLoad = 0.726 × jobs + 0.878 × homes + 0.0386 × tonnes a month // R² = 0.74 over the 56 templates that loaded a road when the fit was struck; seventy do today

The implied rate is one worker per nineteen tonnes a month — a truckload — and nobody set out to encode that. The residuals are all explainable and all one way: an Iron Mine 280 over the fit (heavy vehicles), a Penitentiary 288 under and a Long-Term Care Complex 136 under (their occupants do not commute), a University 258 over (students travel and the fit counts only wages). None of it matters, because the fit supplies the share and the stored road load supplies the magnitude: a mine keeps its 420 and learns that 68% of it is ore.

Commuters / goods / bulk, as the fit reads the catalogue
BuildingRoad loadSplit
Steel Mini-Mill50017% / 0% / 83%
Grain Farm1814% / 0% / 86%
Iron Mine42032% / 0% / 68%
Bakery4053% / 16% / 31%
Small Grocery5587% / 12% / 1%
University520100% / 0% / 0%
A played city—86% / 3% / 11%

A real city is 86% people, which is what urban traffic actually is. And the ensemble found a defect whose fix is the better design: the first version summed the three streams to get the network's total, and eight seeds came back subtly different from the build before them — a thousand dollars in three trillion, one person in forty-eight thousand — because every building's three loads add to its own road load to the bit, and what does not survive is adding them up in a different order. So the total keeps the exact sweep it always had and the breakdown is read alongside it. The demand did not change; only the ability to see what it is made of.

The modes, and a fare that is a price

The land ladder, per unit of capacity — a journey a month for the transit lines
ModeCapacityLandSq ft a unitCash a unit
Gravel Road900450,000500$2,584
Paved Road1,200250,000208$4,500
Elevated Highway1,50063,00042$6,400
Bus Network2,50020,0008$4,800
Light Rail Line8,00045,0006$8,000
Metro Line30,00030,0001$10,667

A metro is five hundred times a gravel road's capacity per acre for four times the price a journey. A city that has run out of ground and not out of money builds one; a city with ground to spare never should — which is the first real answer this game has had to its own late-game wall. Every mode is a relief on an unchanged baseline, and that is the design rather than an implementation detail: a city with no highway and no transit computes exactly the load it computed yesterday and degrades on exactly the curve it degraded on. A highway relieves freight, not by carrying more but because a tonne of ore on a grade-separated road does not share a junction with a school run — bulk by 40%, goods by 15%, commuters not at all. Transit takes people off the road entirely, and three things stop it being the answer to everything: a city's lines reach 65% of each group of commuters, the car-less and the car owners alike since 0.7.49 (TRANSIT_MAX_SHARE), so no more than that ever ride, whatever is built; what transit can carry is capped at twice the road capacity under it, because buses are road vehicles and a metro station needs a street outside it — a city that builds a metro and no streets gets a metro nobody can reach; and it never touches freight. Measured on a load of 10,000 in the played mix: 5,590 riders with room to spare, 4,800 with fewer streets, 800 with none, and with the best of everything the road still carries 39% of what it would. The road can be halved and it can never be deleted. It is also why the stream split matters to a business: an office and a mill on the same jammed road read 74% and 47% against the road's own 42%, because the mill beats the road by exactly as much of its own traffic as rides the tram, and a lorry in a jam is in a jam. A sector with nothing standing reads the city's own blend rather than a clean road, because the planner costs a first plant at the sector's operating rate and a congested city was cheerfully building plants a 38% road said it could not afford.

A fare was a price, not a charge, from 16 September to 0.7.48. Nobody chooses to be policed and a patient in a clinic is not shopping; the person on a bus chose to be, and can choose a car instead. So the ridership ceiling was what a free system would carry and the fare walked it down on a straight line to nobody at the maximum: free, 5,590 riders; at the default, 5,311; at the ceiling, none. And the two lines moved against each other, which was the whole decision — six times the fare took $58,695 a month instead of $13,276 and put 1,398 more cars on the road. Jerus asked for a dial that can be made profitable "by alot"; it can, at a ridership the city may not want. The fare is money, so a reform moves it — the twenty-third of that family and the first caught in the same edit that created the field — and since 0.7.47 the curve reads the dial in founding money through the currency's unit, so a reform moves nobody's mind about the bus; until then it did, by about 5% of the riders in the 2,400-month city divided by a thousand, and its cap is the founding cap in the new unit (TaxPolicy.maxTransitFare()). Since 0.7.42 the fare is real: the dial is set in founding money and charged at the price level the city expects (TaxPolicy.chargedFare()), so it keeps its worth, and the screens show it in today's money. Two things were found on the way: nothing in the infrastructure category had ever had a job, so nobody had written a line to pay one, and a Metro Line is 828 posts; and the fare was charged once a month against a constant that says a single journey, so a monthly pass cost $2.50 against a $3,460 wage, seven hundredths of one percent, and the buses could not have paid for themselves at any fare a player would set. That was the transit loss the list had carried as a balance problem. It is 40 journeys a month now — out and back, twenty days — a pass is $100 or 2.9% of an unskilled wage, and the dial stays per ride because that is the number a person on a platform recognises.

Since 0.7.49 it is a price to car owners and a charge to everyone else. Jerus: "households shouldnt check the transit price alone to determine if they use it, they should determine the cost of their own transportation ... if one household category has 3k workers and 2k cars, then well, 1k has to take transit cause they have no choice ... but if they already have car they look at oil costs". So the commuters are split by whether they have a car of their own, counted per worker in the working cells (HouseholdBalance.captiveShare()): a household holds one car at most, so a couple's second earner and four of five flatmates have none. The car-less ride whatever the fare, if a line reaches them and there is a seat: 65% of them are in reach, the seats go to them first, and the rest walk, at no money cost but on the street at today's car factor, so the jam is what it costs them. A dearer fare takes more of their money and does not empty the buses. The owners weigh a ride, the dial at the expected level, against a journey's fuel, $2.00 in the world's money at today's rate (Motoring.CAR_FUEL_PER_JOURNEY) — since 0.7.62 its 1.2 litres at the price fuel lands at, the refiners' while they have it on offer (Motoring.journeyFuel()) — and a cell splits rather than flips: all of it on the bus at half the drive's cost, all of it in the car at twice it, the straight line between (InfrastructureManager.transitChosen()), because a cell is thousands of households with commutes of every length. The jam still puts more of them on the bus, walked down by the old fare curve, so an owner who chose the car rides exactly as before; 65% of the owners are in reach too, and they take the seats the car-less left. The ceiling did not move — the car-less and the owners in reach are never more than 65% of the commuters, and the stock and the road under it cap both — and the road reads what is not on a bus at the car factor, as it did. Because the dial is anchored and the fuel is today's, a weak currency puts owners on the bus. In the 2,400-month city 73% of workers have no car of their own; the old rule, which read cars per household, had left 21,000 seats empty there while the streets jammed, and at 0.7.49 56,775 ride: 48,716 car-less, every one in reach, and 8,059 owners who chose the bus, while 26,232 car-less walk and 19,498 drive. Raising the fare loses owners and keeps the car-less, so revenue rises almost in line with it: at $50 a ride, the dial's cap, the riders bottom out at the car-less, 48,716, and the fares at D$97.6M a month, a pass 27.6% of an unskilled household's take-home. It is a tax on people who cannot leave, and the fare card says who is paying it. The fares fall on the households' riding rows since 0.7.49, and the fuel, a fourth fee, on their driving rows; the retired, the out of work and the students pay neither (people).

The fare was collected from nobody

From the day transit was built until 16 September the city credited itself the fare and debited no household. Nobody saw it because nothing had ever built a bus: the playtest's advisor answered every jam with tarmac, so four thousand months a seed, eight seeds a run, not one city ever carried a passenger, and every fixture that needed a road bought a road. The first ensemble in which the advisor bought a Bus Network failed the money audit on all eight seeds, and the residual was the fare to six decimals — 2,186 riders at $2.50 is $5.47, and the audit said 5.465207. A harness cannot check a thing nobody does. MoneyCheck's own header says every money bug this codebase has had was a flow with one side, and the twenty-ninth harness could not see this one because its city had no buses. It builds three now. Fixing it found its sibling at once: setTransit was missing from the load path, the fifth sighting of that shape, invisible until the fare became real money.

The bill was paid by nobody

Transit's wages and upkeep were struck every month and paid by nobody. The crews drew their pay and the lines their upkeep, the fares came in, and no line of the treasury, the budget or the audit carried the bill — D$23.96M a month in the 2,400-month city against D$4.12M of fares, measured at 0.7.47. The Government screen's bridge had found it in 0.7.31 as a bill on no budget line that, as far as the bridge and the audit could show, nobody paid, and it went on the list. Since 0.7.49 the treasury pays it as a promise, after the police (TreasuryLine.TRANSIT), and the budget, EARNED, the audit and G carry it, the fares a budget line beside it (tax, policy and GDP); the bill is carried across a save. Paying it taught the playtest's player to count it: its advisor had 25 bus networks at month 1,000 in a city of 15,740, a post for every ten seats, and at the design study's checkpoints their bill, unpaid, came to as much as 54% of a month's output. Since 0.7.49 it buys a line only while the output the road gives back with it standing and the fares of the riders it adds pay its crews (LongPlaytest.linesThatPay()) — the police station's rule, with the fares counted — and with that rule the playtest's treasury never fell below its own earlier low.

The railway

Rail is the twelfth sector, and the business that wants to be paid the freight every price in this game has always had inside it. Jerus: "the freight cant reach zero cause freight needs profit, its its own sector… it wants and will do everything possible to stay profitable and maximize profits. freight money goes to that sector." There were two ways to write that and only one keeps the audit honest. Paying the railway out of the band would have a payment the books record as going abroad in fact going to a company in the city. So the band carries only what the lorries still move, and the railway bills the shipper at home, like any other supplier of a service — the invoice goes on the shipper's input line, where freight belongs in anybody's accounts, the money never leaves the city, and the VAT falls out for free because the ledger already credits a purchase at the supplier's own rate.

what a shipper pays a unit = (1 − share) × baseFreight // still to the lorries, inside the band + share × quote × baseFreight // to the railway, on its invoice allowed = what a month costs to run + 1.2% of the track it has sunk quote = clamp(allowed / what it would bill at the lorry rate × scarcity, 0.30, 1)

A shop's pricing rule cannot price a utility, and the measurement is what said so. Written first as Retail's shelf — cost plus a quarter — it quoted 32% of the lorry rate, earned $4.5m a month on ten billion of track, and was written down six times: a railway's running cost is a fraction of its capital, so a mark-up on running costs allows a return of nothing on the track. It prices like a regulated network now, running costs plus 1.2% a month on the rate base, floored at 30% of the lorry rate because rail is cheaper than a lorry and not free, capped at one because nobody ships by rail at more than the lorry charges, lifted up to 1.6× by what it cannot carry. It settles at 38–43%. Building too much rail does not make freight cheaper; it makes a railway that cannot pay for itself, and RailCheck asserts that a network with four times the track it needs pins at the lorries' price and earns less on four times the capital. The link that falls out of it is real and welcome: a railway earning 1.2% a month on money that costs it 1% is barely a business, so dear money is what keeps a city's trade band wide. And the band alone was lying to the sectors: at a 92% quote on 92% of the traffic it said steel's wedge had fallen from 52% to 11% when the shipper had saved eight per cent, because the railway's invoice was not in it. Every place that asks is this worth exporting reads a net price now; every place that settles a trade reads the band; with no railway the two are the same number to the bit.

Track is half a railway. Capacity is the lesser of the track laid and the wagon sets to run it, at 2,500 tonnes a set and a twenty-year life, so track with no locomotives carries nothing: a Rail Spur is twenty sets, $48m of rolling stock on a $140m spur, and a mature city's million tonnes of track is 420 sets and about 1¾ sets a month of replacement for ever, which is half a Locomotive Works running. Rolling stock is a pantry rather than an input — that one line is what makes a bought set accumulate into a fleet instead of vanishing into a month's production — and a save from before trains has trains, because it was hauling, so it had locomotives. The railway will not lay a line under 60% used, Materials' lesson relearned the afternoon a spur went up in a town of four hundred houses and a bank failed over it. It relieves 75% of the bulk it carries off the road, never all of it, because the last mile off a siding is a lorry and a spur reads as 94% trucks; bulk goes before goods, which decides almost nothing today because bulk is 99.7% of the tonnage crossing the boundary. Every tonne it hauls burns $30 of fuel in the world's money, a real import before there was any oil good, so that the day oil became a good nothing else had to change; and since 0.7.62 the railway draws FUEL as a buyer, eighteen litres a tonne (Rail.FUEL_LITRES_PER_TONNE, the $30 over fuel's import price), off the refiners' tanks first, keeping among its extras what came from abroad, where it had booked an imported service with no good (Sector.bookImportedService(), gone). Since 0.7.29 the lorry bill is told three ways — billed at home, paid abroad and kept — and what the railway was allowed to bill and what went abroad are kept across a save among its extras, with no format change; a save from before reads them as not known until a month runs, bar a railway that billed nothing at home, which sent the whole bill abroad. Since 0.7.46 the month's trade in units is saved too, so a reloaded railway bills its first month as the city that never reloaded does, where it had billed almost nothing and its quote had jumped (saves).

What a railway is worth

Eight seeds, four thousand months each: population 113,178 → 178,975 (+58%), GDP $416.8M → $711.2M a month (+71%), per seed 32–91% and 14–113%. On the default seed steel's net wedge goes from 51.6% of the world price to 25.9%. And it is a healthy business in all eight: zero write-downs, zero loss months, the best credit rate in the game, and in seven of eight no debt at all. Three rungs — 50,000, 200,000 and 500,000 tonnes a month at $140m, $520m and $1.24bn — $2,800 of capital per tonne a month on a spur and $2,480 on a terminal, and a terminal nearly three times a Coal Power Plant on the ground, which is the land trade Jerus asked for.

The vehicles

Automotive is the thirteenth sector, and it is not for the cars. Ore becomes steel, steel becomes fabricated steel and machinery, and until 16 September both left, because nothing in the city bought a beam or a machine. An assembly plant is the first thing that does — the fourth link of a chain that stopped at three — and the chain cannot be shortcut: fabricated steel is deliberately not importable, so a city with money and no fabrication shop cannot be in the car business, it can only buy cars. Machinery can come off a ship, at the ceiling its own header had predicted for the day something here bought one, because a car is three and a half tonnes of fabricated steel and four hundred kilos of machinery and the bulky input is the one a city must make. Cars export; vans and locomotives do not, from three measurements of one failure: allowed to sell wagon sets to the world, the playtest built 19 Locomotive Works, 9,880 posts in a city with 25,102 jobs; allowed to sell vans, 240 Commercial Vehicle Plants shipping 36,000 vans a month; and it was already building 179 Fabrication Works and zero Machine Works. An unbounded export market at a fixed floor means the single best export is built for ever and nothing else ever is; bounded world demand is the general fix and is still its own batch. A plant may draw at most a quarter of what the city fabricates, because the first supply gate asked the mills' question — is there spare local supply — and nothing fabricated is ever spare when the world will take all of it; a $172m Assembly Plant sank in month seventeen of a fixture that was measuring tuition, and the harness was right. Eight seeds: GDP $711.2M → $758.8M a month, up in six of eight; seed 0's mature city runs 84 Assembly Plants on 23% of its fabrication.

The cars are the fourth mode, and the only one that costs — the road, and since 0.7.49 their owners, who buy $2.00 of the world's fuel a journey, forty a month, an import, and since 0.7.62 draw the journey's 1.2 litres off the refiners' tanks before they import. Highways, transit and rail are all reliefs on an unchanged baseline; a car is the opposite, and it has to be, because the baseline this network always computed was an unmotorised city, unlabelled. It is named now rather than moved:

onRoad = (commuters − riders) × (1 + ownership × (3.0 − 1)) // exactly 1 at zero ownership, to the bit car-less = min(ceiling, commuters × captive share × 0.65) // at any fare, since 0.7.49 owners = min(ceiling − car-less, commuters × (1 − captive share) × 0.65 × ride) ride = s + (1 − s) × 0.75 × jam × ridership(dial) // s = transitChosen(a journey's fuel, the dial at the expected level); jam a memory of the commute, a quarter a month transitChosen = clamp(½ + (drive − ride) / (drive + ride) × 1.5, 0, 1) // 1 at half the drive's cost, 0 at twice it // until 0.7.49: willingToRide = (1 − ownership) + ownership × 0.75 × jam, read off cars per household

Only the people still driving pay it, so a city that motorises and builds transit is not charged twice for one journey, and that is what closes Jerus's loop: "people drive until the road is full, then take the tram." A car owner rides nothing on a clear morning and three quarters of them ride at a standstill — motorise, nobody rides, it jams, they ride, it clears — read off a memory of the commute rather than last month's road, or it is a two-month oscillator. Transit is a ceiling on ownership, not a brake on adoption, and the difference is the whole of whether a player has a move: on the rate it only delays, on the level a well-served city tops out at half a car per household for ever. Since 0.7.49 that ceiling is scaled by who would rather ride: a household without a car weighs a month's pass against a car's full monthly cost — the showroom's price over its 180-month life at the household rate, and forty journeys of fuel (Motoring.carPayment()) — on the same split, and the ceiling is one less half the transit cover times the share that prefers the bus, so half a car holds where a pass beats a car's full cost and at twice that cost transit deters nobody (people). What motorising costs, eight seeds against the same build with adoption off: 191,194 people and $848.3M a month at 80% road, against 176,228 and $695.7M at 73%, cars in 60% of households and 37% of commuters riding — and the chain is not congestion alone: a worse road costs every new plant at a lower operating rate, marginal projects stop clearing the interest test, business borrowing collapses ($6.6bn lent against $18.5bn, the bank at 45% of capacity instead of 77%), Manufacturing's assets fall from $23.4bn to $6.3bn, exports fall 26%. A six-point road ratio, compounded over three centuries. CAR_LOAD_AT_SATURATION = 3.0 is the one constant here that is a game decision rather than a measured one. The advisor learned to buy a bus the same day, and what decides between six candidates is the catalogue: each is costed in trips taken off the road per dollar — a gravel road 900 for $2,326k against a bus network 2,500 for $12,000k times the car factor — so a bus loses outright in a city where nobody drives and wins once about half the households own a car. Nobody chose that crossover.

The vans are the fifth throttle, and the first one a business buys. Energy, water, road and health all arrive; a lorry is the sector's own capital, sized from a measurement: a city of 106,612 moves 1,169,640 tonnes a month at nameplate, both sides of the door, which at 120 tonnes a vehicle is a fleet of about ten thousand, one commercial vehicle per eleven people, and eighty a month of replacement for ever. Built once without teeth, every ratio in the measured city read 100.0%, because vans are importable and a sector asking for a fleet got one in a month; you cannot put a thousand lorries on the road in a month, so a fleet is built up at an eighth a month and a firm short of its own lorries hires haulage — slowed to 60%, not stopped. Re-measured: 132 sector-months short, worst 65%, every growing sector hitting it and none stopped. A save from before vans owns a fleet, on the railway's own flag: it was moving steel, so it had lorries. And it is what finally gives the Commercial Vehicle Plant a customer.

What the whole vehicles arc costs a mature city — eight seeds, 4,000 months each
BuildPopulationGDP a monthRoads
No cars, no vans191,194$848.3M80%
Cars176,228$695.7M73%
Cars and vans165,914$673.3M75%

About 13% of population and 21% of output against a city where nobody owns anything to move with — for two industries and three decisions it did not have. The households' side of the cars, who buys one and who borrows for it and who sells it last, is in people.

The instrument panel

Nine batches of transport shipped before any of it was on a screen: the road carried three streams and showed one number, the fare was a real dial with a real elasticity and nothing in the game could turn it, the railway repriced itself every month and told nobody, and a mill running at 65% because it had not taken delivery of its lorries looked exactly like a mill running at 65% for no reason. Infrastructure has been a tab of its own since 0.6.5, the rail's twelfth, put after Services because that is where the eye goes looking for it, with four pages that each answer a question somebody actually asks; since 0.7.28 it is drawn by its own class (InfrastructureScreen, moved verbatim from ServicesScreen), and Services' road row and Build's road and transit rings open it on Roads or Transit. Since 0.7.29 it is drawn in Build's style, a fixed frame over a page that scrolls: Infrastructure with a door to Build's Roads & transit, on the ring the page explains, and one to City History on road throughput alone; five figures, each a door — SERVED (FULL until 0.7.41) and FLOW in NEEDS YOU's road colour, the only verdicts in the strip, FLOW with its change on last month, then ON TRANSIT, CARS and BY RAIL; and the four pages as chips with icons. Roads answers why the flow is what it is: the model's own flow curve with the city's dot on it and a hollow dot where the road sites on site would leave it, drawn since 0.7.41 against what the road serves, 0 to 200%, red, amber and green from the left and better to the right (throughputAtServed()), where it had been a banded meter of how full; beside it FROM TRIPS TO THE ROAD, the trips the city makes by stream, less transit, plus what the cars add, less what the railway and the highways take off, with the capacity and the free-flow line through every row (roadBreakdown()); then a card a stream — the trips made and on the road, what gets through, what a trip costs the road — and the network in four figures. Transit is a funnel from the commuters through the three ceilings, the lowest tagged, to the riders, with the line that reconciles Build's ring, room on the stock, with who rides, and since 0.7.49 the riders by reason under it (below), then the books and the fare card. The railway is the month's lorry bill as one bar split three ways — billed at home, paid abroad, kept (getPaidAbroad(), getKept()) — and the quote on a gauge between its floor and a lorry; track and trains, or N sets short, or the replacement sets a month; what it hauls; and its business, with a bigger than its city chip where a box used to be. Freight is a bar a good — the world's margin, the freight still paid abroad, the railway's charge, and a mark where it would be by lorry — the month's freight bill in five figures, and the lorries in one line while nobody is short; the old page split the bill into the half billed at home and the half that leaves inside the band, and that half was the shippers' saving when the railway carried everything. Every table is behind details and every paragraph behind an (i). Nothing on the screen builds anything; a second place to buy a building is a second place to maintain. Cars show in both places, on People beside the household's other assets and on Roads beside the congestion number they explain.

The fare card. The fare is the one control, and since 0.7.38 it is a dial card as Policy's are: the bus, the reading ($2.50 a ride, or free), the ladder, and under it, always and following the thumb, WHAT IT WOULD DO — riders, fares collected a month, what it costs the city net a month and the trips back onto the road, and since 0.7.49 the car owners on the bus — each the model's own read at that fare (InfrastructureManager.ridersAt(), faresAt(), backOnTheRoadAt(), EconomyManager.transitNetAt()), where the page had multiplied riders by fares itself. It still says plainly what it cannot preview, the second round, where a worse road puts some of them back on the tram, behind its caveat's (i), and Apply sits at its foot once a fare is staged. Since 0.7.49 its status says the anchor and what the fare is weighed against: $100 a month for 40 journeys · set at $2.50 at founding prices, ×1.001 the prices people expect, and a drive's fuel: $1.42 a journey at today's exchange rate · a month's pass is 1.4% of an unskilled household's take-home in the 2,400-month city. Riders by reason, since 0.7.49, sit under the ceilings: those with no car of their own, how many a line reaches and how many ride, and how many walk; those with one, how many are in reach and how many chose the bus; those who drive; and the riders — in the 2,400-month city 74,948 workers with no car, 73%, of whom 48,716 are in reach and riding and 26,232 walk; 27,557 with one, 17,912 of them in reach and 8,059 on the bus; 19,498 driving; and 56,775 riders. The screen shipped without anyone looking at it — the only JavaFX natives here are the Windows build and a Java window cannot be put in the screenshot allowlist — so InfrastructureCheck gained a ninth section that walks every accessor the four pages call, in the four city states that break screens, and asks the two questions a renderer cannot survive the wrong answer to: is it finite, and is it inside the range the label claims. Its tenth (0.7.29) holds the curve, the walk and the wedge as the redrawn tab draws them — the dot on the curve, the walk adding to the load, the funnel's last step the riders, the room on the effective load — and its eleventh (0.7.49) who rides by what they pay. None of the redrawn tab has been seen rendered.

22

The screen

Jerus played 0.7.19 for its interface alone, and four batches rebuilt the screen from what that found (0.7.20 to 0.7.23); then from 0.7.24 to 0.7.39 every screen on the rail was redrawn in Build's style, a screen a batch, and 0.7.40 and 0.7.41 fixed what playing them found. None of them moved the model in a city whose player leaves 0.7.22's new hand on the queue alone, and after each the default playtest wrote 0.7.19's eight trace files to the byte. The window is a header, a rail and a page. Twelve destinations sit on the rail at the window's left edge — Build, Land office, People, Services, Infrastructure, Sectors, Government, Finances, Bank, Trade, Policy, History — with Menu at its foot where the gear was; the header carries the clock, the money block and five headline numbers, then the Needs you chip; since 0.7.24 the City overview is a drawer behind that chip and Under construction folds to a tab at the stage's right edge (the builders); and since 0.7.21 there is a main menu again, drawn over a skyline the game draws. A new or loaded city opens on Build's Overview, the one screen a player who does not yet know what they are doing can do something on. Pressing a rail button you are already on takes you back to that rail's own front page rather than doing nothing, so there is always a way out of a sub-page without knowing which one you came in by; Build is the exception since 0.7.20, and keeps the category it was left on for the session, though a new city or a load sets it back to its Overview.

The play-through, and what it asked for

Jerus asked for a play-through on the PC, the interface and not the numbers: "take notes about the ui, any issues, any thoughts, any ideas, things that should be better... dont change anything". On 30 September a new Standard city was played for thirteen months, and his slot-10 city of 1.05 million from month 1,792 to 1,814, his own autosave copied off first and written back byte for byte. It found a founding screen that promised a village the game does not build, City History opening scrolled to its foot, Esc doing nothing on the Build tab, a price card still saying no year yet at month 13, Quit with no question, a building on site invisible on its own build card, a build quote that drifted from five months to nine, tooltips with no background, Died −0.0 and Moved in 8.9, revenue at 169.7% of annual GDP in month 7, and a centre column that jumped under the pointer whenever its height changed. What worked: the tax dial's staging, the Needs you rows that go straight to the fix, the order line — "$33.6M with $5.0M sales tax, back to the treasury as it is built · ~5 mo" — and the land office's cards. Jerus: "ok yes, thats good, all you listed is good", and he added the textbook problem, everything "worded very long, like the player is basically... welcome to the textbook?", and asked for colour, "gdp on the main screen at the top always visible with a tiny little chart", charts that move with their crises named, a chart that can fill the screen, and the construction panel. Four batches were agreed: the bugs and the layout (0.7.20); colour, the header, the menu and the founding screen (0.7.21); the construction panel (0.7.22, the builders); and the charts (0.7.23). On the mockups for the last three, Jerus: "that is damn pretty, go for it".

The page stays put

0.7.20 shipped nineteen fixes. City History opens at the top: Write the year book, the page's only control that takes the focus, took it on arrival and scrolled the page down to itself. Esc and P reach the menu with a tooltip showing, because a showing tooltip hid itself on Esc and swallowed the key, and on the Build tab the pointer is nearly always over one; P still types in a text box. The centre is top-aligned on every screen, the build hint and the receipt's dot keep their space whether or not they show, and a scrolled page ends with room under it. A build card reads N on site · ~M mo at today's queue for anybody's order, a Needs you line whose fix is already on site adds N on the way, ~M mo and fell from red to amber until 0.7.41, when the served rows kept their verdict's colour and their sites came to change only the listing and the order (below), and the order line, the card and the right panel read the quote's one wait (BuildingManager.waitOnSite()). Tooltips have a panel. Flows are whole people, under 1 below one, and no shared formatter prints a negative zero. Quit asks first, "Quit to the desktop? This city is autosaved.", and while it is up Esc closes it and every other key goes to its buttons. Urgent notices are toasts at the bottom right: three at most, each fading after eight seconds and held while the pointer is on it, a click opening the inbox at that notice. Text wraps; Build remembers its category for the session; the receipt is a popover with the last five purchases, each with its total and its sales tax; trillions print $3.1T (and since 0.7.54 quadrillions $26.0Q, grouped past a thousand of them) and the load list Autosave · month 13; and a chart with nothing recorded yet says from the end of the first year rather than drawing an empty frame.

It was checked by eye on Jerus's PC the same night, a new Standard city to month 13 and then a cold start, with his autosave backed up first and restored byte for byte, and that check found two more, fixed the same night: the right panel's site line was squeezed back to 371 af… once the build log under it needed the room, and Resume on a cold start opened the empty world the game builds at start-up rather than the autosave — older than 0.7.20, and that world would have autosaved over the city the player meant to resume. Esc could not be checked, because the remote session's Escape never reaches the game; it is Jerus's to try by hand.

Colour, type, the header and the menu

One palette since 0.7.21, the mockups', in ui/Palette: near-black blue grounds, three greys for text, four area colours that say where you are and never how it is going — people teal, money blue, business violet, building pink — on the rail, as a swatch before every page title, on the header's tiles and in one-line charts; and three verdict colours, green, amber and red, for good, watch and bad and nothing else. So a labour shortage's 0% out of work reads amber, jobs going unfilled, and not red; a sector earning nothing draws grey; the treasury's cash is white unless it is overdrawn; the build tabs carry a dot for who builds them — blue for the city alone, violet where investors build too — with a key, where amber words were, and since 0.7.24 Build's strip reads ‹ Overview, the city's five with blue dots, a rule and the market's nine with violet; and the Government's rings take five colours that can be told apart. The redrawn screens kept to it and took out the thresholds they had made up: since 0.7.32 Finances' import cover has one tick, the model's COMFORTABLE_COVER, its stress bars are plain and a price under par is not painted green; since 0.7.33 the Bank's families, its interest and its coupons are series, not verdicts; and since 0.7.37 a line's move on City History is in neutral ink, where a rise had been green and a fall amber whatever the line. Words are IBM Plex Sans and every figure IBM Plex Mono, bundled with the SIL Open Font License, where about fifty hard-coded Courier New faces were; a face that fails to load leaves the platform's, and the log says which.

The header is one bar where the date bar, the floating time controls and the net-income bubble were. The clock is docked at its left — a green play button, the date, the month and ‹ 10× ›. Since 0.7.24 the money is a block of its own beside it: a coin in a blue square, TREASURY in the money blue with its (i), and the cash at 28 pixels in the short form, white and red only when overdrawn; under it the month's net income, what the budget earned (Game.getIncome()), green, red when negative and grey at nothing, its tooltip saying it is not the change in the cash and giving that, and worded since 0.7.31 +$1.5B earned a month, its (i) naming EARNED, SURPLUS and BANKED and the steps between them (what the treasury actually did); a click opens Finances. Then five tiles, each a label and a 72 × 16 sparkline of the last ten years on its top row, the figure at 15 pixels and a line of change across it: POPULATION, with the month's net; GDP, annualised from month 1, with real growth over twelve months once there are two years to compare, and since 0.7.31 a loaded city's year its own at once; INFLATION, against the player's target, or rate in ~N mo before the first rate, and since 0.7.45 what people expect and how far they trust the bank under it (prices); OUT OF WORK; and RATE · D$, the dial with what a US dollar costs, on the one parity rule since 0.7.35. TREASURY was a sixth tile until 0.7.24. A change line too long for its tile takes a shorter wording, so that no label, figure or change line is ever cut, and a tile with under 30 pixels spare drops its sparkline; the header is laid out for 1,280, 1,389 and 1,920 pixels, with the date at 17, and was seen at 1,389 on Jerus's PC with nothing cut, where at 0.7.21 a big city's first three tiles drew no sparkline at 1,366. A click on a tile opens City History on its lines over their last ten years, landing at the chart, and the (i)s are on INFLATION, RATE and the money block. Then the Needs you chip: Needs you and a count, red if any row is red and amber if only ambers, and ✓ Nothing needs you with none; a hover lists up to eight rows, and a click opens the City overview on NEEDS YOU whatever it was last left on (below). At the right are the credit rating's chip, green from AAA to BBB, amber at BB and B and red below, and the inbox's envelope with its unread count.

The main menu is drawn over the whole window on a still skyline the game draws, the city's line rising behind it: CityBuilderSim, "A city, its money, and everyone in it."; Continue, saying which city and month it goes back to — the city in play, or the autosave on a cold start; Save while a city is open; New city, which opens the founding panel (founding a city); Load a city; Settings; Quit; and YOUR CITIES, the three slots saved most recently with their name, people and month. Esc, P or Menu on the rail open the same menu over the city, and since 0.7.22 Settings, Load and Save on a cold start are drawn over its backdrop rather than over an empty city's header.

Text in three layers. On the screen, the number, a label of two to four words and at most one short line; one click away, behind a shared (i), the full explanation in a popover that closes on a click elsewhere or on Esc; and in this manual, the history — nothing on screen carries how the code used to work. Since 0.7.21 the (i) is on the build tabs, Construction's How it bills, the tax dial, the Government's last row, the founding screen, the menu and three of the header's tiles; since 0.7.22 on five more of the longest paragraphs the game had — the bank's lending table, the central bank rolling its paper, buying bonds, the households who skipped their care bills, and the preferred shares' terms. The short lines dropped some clauses the long paragraphs had, which is on the list. Every screen redrawn since 0.7.26 puts every paragraph behind an (i) and every table behind details.

Build opens on an Overview

Jerus: "when you start the game you start in residential so the player without reading thinks he needs to building houses". Since 0.7.24 Build opens on an Overview, and a new city or a load sets it back there. It is three rows. THE CITY'S JOB is five tiles, the city's categories, each ringed by its worst NEEDS YOU row. WHAT WOULD HELP MOST is up to three orders by BuildAdvice's rule, each the quote its order charges, with Show and Order. The rule takes NEEDS YOU's rows in their order, one order a need, and skips a need that what is on site already keeps ahead; it prefers a building that can keep the need ahead to one that cannot, one whose count fits the ground the cards before it leave to one that does not, and then the lowest price a unit at today's staffing with its ground, roads and transit compared on the trips they take off the strained line through the model's own curve; it orders the least that keeps the need ahead after what is on site, or else the least that reaches the best that building makes, as far as Xs go; and its before and after are NEEDS YOU's own figure and the model's arithmetic, which a twin built with the order agrees with to every printed digit. THE MARKET BUILDS THESE is nine quieter tiles. The city's five categories open on rings, one a measure, with cards that say what a building does in a verb for the measure, seats or puts N officers on the street, a cost bar a unit served, the posts the city could not fill per 10,000 served, tags, and an order bar summing the page's steppers. Since 0.7.34 the order bar's Build says the whole order's total (BuildAdvice.quoteTotal()), the model's since 0.7.38; a press is charged the run's invoice, which can be a little over it when an earlier order's yard material makes a later one dearer. Every building is one card (the catalogue). BuildAdviceCheck holds the rule in thirteen sections: the fourteen categories, NEEDS YOU's needs in order, the count and the quote, the cash, before and after in a twin, every measure, the staffing weighting, nothing suggested when nothing is needed, and since 0.7.51 the land counted, the slack built, the schools' pipeline, the projection and the run (how it's checked).

Since 0.7.51 the advice is priced with its land and sized ahead. Jerus, of Build all three: "the building ideas is flawed, it doesnt take into account land price, and it doesnt build any slack … make it so that alot of its ideas also take into account the same as businesses do, aka a projection". The count had been the least that took a row off the list today, and no more — forty clinics left the 2,400-month city's general care at 81%, and at 80% the month they opened — and the price a unit never saw the ground, so a Wind Farm's 1.74 million square feet for 8,100 kW beat a Coal Power Plant's 2 million for 280,000. Now each building's ground is valued at what the land office charges (LandManager.getOfficePricePerSqFt(), its best plot without ore at today's rate, and since 0.7.57 its best value, the most dry ground a dollar, a square foot of that ground; free ground at that or at what a business pays the city, whichever is less), on the ground the cards before it leave; a card's price is still its quote, because the city builds on its own ground. An order is sized the way the businesses size a plant: to the demand when it opens — its own wait, held to their twelve months — plus their six-month horizon, grown by their growthFactor(), which planMaker() reads too, with 5% to spare on top, their headroom (BuildAdvice.SLACK); a served gauge has to reach 100% of that, green and not just off the list. A school above the ladder is wanted only for the students the city would get and hire (labour and schools). And the cash no longer cuts a count: a card is the count that keeps its need ahead, on credit when the cash the cards before it leave is short of its quote, and by how much. A card says it in three lines — what it does; when it opens and what it is sized for, Opens ~18 mo; sized 18 mo out: no growth on the trend, 5% to spare; and its ground, Land 4,950,000 sq ft ≈ $6.5M, with what is to buy first and, for a count above one, why so many, 133 of them: the cheapest per child with its land that keeps it ahead, at 8 children each — the cheapest of its rank, because one that can keep the need ahead outranks one that cannot at any price, and one that fits the land left one that does not; the ranking behind the choice is in the land line's tooltip. The 600-month city's roads went from 2 Bus Networks to 11 Gravel Roads, the buses at $3,431 a trip with their land against the gravel's $8,200 but unable to hold the road with 5% spare, and its childcare from 3 Childcare Centres to 133 Home Daycares; the 2,400-month city's power from 14 Wind Farms to 1 Coal Power Plant; Jerus's roads from 63 Gravel Roads to 54 Elevated Highways; and the playtest's month-9 university is gone. Over the cards, all three ≈ $X is what placing the run charges (Game.buildRunInvoice()), where it had added the cards' quotes — $85.5M against the $98.4M the run charged in the playtest city at month 24 — and Build all three reads the run the model will place, where each card's own look had said GO for runs the model stopped: held where Game.buildRunAhead() stops it, it stops at the first: short of land - buy it at the land office first; on credit by the run's own gap, short $X: Build offers a loan; else GO.

Every screen in Build's style

Jerus, after Build was redone: "the others are still full of text and the design could be more intuitive and fun". From 0.7.26 to 0.7.37 the rail's other eleven screens were redrawn, one a batch, most of them overnight or while Jerus was at work, each to one shape: a fixed frame — the screen's name, a strip of four or five figures that are each a door, and its pages as chips with icons — over a page that alone scrolls; every page led by one picture; every paragraph behind an (i) and every table and old statement behind details, verbatim; colour for categories, and the verdict colours only on verdicts; and every figure a model read, worked out in a method that draws nothing, so that a probe without the toolkit can read every word on a played city. They are built from shared pieces in Pieces — a page head, chips, a ring as a card, a waterfall, a bullet bar, a bridge, a cause bar, a supply bar, a funnel, rows on one scale, two bars on one scale, a band bar, a rate ladder, a status banner, a split ring, ranked bars and a range bar — and the icons in Icons. The land office was the first (0.7.26, land).

People (0.7.27) is one scrolling page of pictures: the pyramid with its settled ghost, the month as a waterfall with a year view, the bridge, Build's care rings, the homes gauge, the household mosaic, tiles for the people outside the families and the pay ladder as bars. Household money is a page of its own: each tier's live wage at its head, the books beside the grid at 1,200 pixels and wider, a verdict that counts every row, the rows outside the families measured against a basket and the retired on their statement, and INCOME PER RESIDENT where a per-worker figure had divided the population by the workforce (people). Since 0.7.45 People has WHO GOES SHORT, AND WHY, and since 0.7.49 a fuel step in its waterfall, a fuel line on the statement and an opened cell's row fares and fuel. Services (0.7.28) opens each system on an Overview with one picture — the sick rate as a bar of its causes over the four care cards, the schools as a pipeline with each school's gate, power, water and the road as capacity rows, now, at full staff and asked, with who draws each, and since 0.7.59 the fresh water limit in amber under the water bar when it binds (utilities), and crime as a bar of its reasons beside Canada's — with Books the last chip, cost against what came back; the Infrastructure tab moved to a class of its own then and was redrawn the next day (0.7.29, transport). Sectors (0.7.30) is fifteen cards, seventeen since 0.7.62, in three columns in Build's market order, under four figures — KEPT with its move, LOSING with the two worst named, WORKERS against posts, and RUNNING AT as a range with a door to the Build category that relieves the thinnest of power, the road and health — each card its icon, its Build group, what it kept and its move, the 24 months' line, its workers, its running-at bar with what cuts it most and its investors' word in a kind; while no business has a word for the month, after a load or a founding, the list says so once over the cards (0.7.34). A business is inputs, plant and outputs, the plant a ring with the six throttles as a cascade whose last step is the operating rate (SectorFlow), over ITS OWN FIGURES; its income a waterfall over the statement; its balance sheet two bars on one scale with the owners card; its cash and debt a bridge with the rate in its parts; and its investors one line, each building's first gate, the rules as chips and four doors (work and output). Since 0.7.45 Retail draws THE SHELF and the kitchens and boutiques WHAT IT CHARGES (goods and markets); since 0.7.46 Construction reads its struck materials (the builders) and since 0.7.47 Automotive the month's cars made, sold and shipped. Government (0.7.31) is four pages under five figures: the two rings with THE BALANCE between them, the bridge FROM EARNED TO BANKED, Revenue and Spending as ranked bars that open into who pays, and Output led by the GDP layers; since 0.7.47 its of GDP reads the trailing year and since 0.7.49 it lists Transit fares and Transit as budget lines (tax, policy and GDP). Finances (0.7.32) is the debt's dashboard (the city's borrowing), and the Bank (0.7.33) answers whether it is healthy and what it charges (the bank).

Trade (0.7.35) is five pages — Overview, The month, What we trade, The currency, The reserves — under SOLD ABROAD, BOUGHT ABROAD, THE MONTH, THE CURRENCY and IN THE VAULT, with action cards at the top of every page when the money is leaving, cover is under three months, hot money is barely backed or the city owes more than it holds. The Overview draws mirrored bars a good, bought left and sold right, off the businesses' own books (Sectors.tradeByGood()), footing to the balance of payments every month, beside the currency on a small ten-year chart and three gauges; The month is steps from exports to the month's balance, with the river one toggle away, which now carries the income from abroad it used to drop — a month of +D$183.7M had drawn a D$120.5M deficit; What we trade is by good or by business, a popover a good, ten years and the record since founding as a waterfall; The currency is City History's own chart beside parity, with the forces (the currency); and The reserves is the vault. After a founding, or since 0.7.46 a load from an older save, the month's flows read not counted yet until a month ends, where until then every load did; and since 0.7.49 the households' fuel is among the imports, and since 0.7.62 among the goods, as fuel, the households and the railway its buyers. Policy (0.7.36) is a hub of four areas over WHAT IS BITING and RECENT DECISIONS, every dial a dial card whose before and after are the model's own (PolicyPreview), and the staged changes in a tray with THE BUDGET (setting them). Its Money › The policy rate draws THE RATE LINE, every rate on one scale with named ticks — inflation, the target, the dial, the rule, savers, the city, prime, the world — and a staged dial's as ghosts; OVER THE WORLD and REAL, AGAINST THE WORLD; THE DIAL, with its chips, do what the rule says, the hand and its effects, savers' next month by the bank's own rule; THE RULE; THE CENTRAL BANK; PRICES with a range bar and THE BASKET's five parts, each keyed with its own year's inflation; and since 0.7.45 THE ANCHOR beside THE CURRENCY'S DRIFT (the anchor). The dial's (i) says the city's paper is priced from the higher of the dial and the bank's cost of funds. Promises has had a Food page since 0.7.45, WHO GOES WITHOUT over the food assistance dial. City History (0.7.37) is below, and the city's fund (0.7.39) is a brokerage under Finances — Portfolio, Search, Activity, Rules & cash, and a page a security with its order ticket — whose Rules & cash opens since 0.7.48 on THE WITHDRAWAL, a dial card the page's width (the city's fund).

The loose ends (0.7.38) were eleven places where a screen disagreed with the model or with another screen, each made to agree: the small charts draw the decisions they are handed, and the founding month's decisions stand on the first month of every lane; import cover is said through one formatter; the floor reads in today's money on People, as on Policy and in the drawer, and City History's line is named Minimum wage, founding money; the fare is a dial card; a sector shut out of credit is named red on the Bank's ladder; the Sectors rate bar draws the parts its rate was struck from; the drawer's vs parity is on the one parity rule; the pension's (i) names no method; Build's total is the model's; and nine members with no caller went. Of the redrawn screens only 0.7.24's frame and Build, and 0.7.32's Finances and 0.7.33's Bank, have been seen on Jerus's PC; every word on the rest was composed by the implementers' probes on a 600-month and a 2,400-month city, through a save and a load, and is on the list to look at.

Buttons that ask to be pressed

Jerus, after playing 0.7.31: "everywhere you have build, like the build button, it should be more intuitive aka like an actual button that is basically asking to be pressed, cause currently its a tiny text". Since 0.7.34 Pieces has two pieces for it. The action button is 40 pixels tall, 32 beside a heading, its icon at the left and its label composed from the model's verdict and quote: GO filled in the building pink's darker step, CHOOSE and CREDIT outlined, HELD grey with its reason. The door pill is 30 pixels, outlined, its icon and ›. Every Build and Buy is an action button — a card's (the catalogue), Build's order bar, Build 21 · $X for the whole order, WHAT WOULD HELP MOST's cards and Build all three, Show, a plot's and the land office's (land), and the funding pages' offers (the city's borrowing) — and every door to Build or the land office is a pill, on Services, Infrastructure, Sectors, People and the Bank. Left as they were: a figure's door in a strip or a cell, a whole card, the navigation chips, and the confirmations that buy paper on Finances, Trade, Policy and the Bank. A double-click is one press. Build and the land office share the building pink, and neither piece takes the keyboard's focus: Enter still builds the page.

Served: one rule for every gauge

Jerus, playing 0.7.39: "some of the build stuff shows check no issues yet if you click general care is at 90%, additionally roads are at 180% bad, while general care 90% is bad, so its confusing to the player". A gauge was bad over 100% or under it, depending on the gauge, and of the rules put to him he chose "Served %, higher = better". Since 0.7.41 every power, water, road, transit, care and school gauge reads served, supply over demand as a per cent, unclamped, 100% meaning enough: power and water were % used, roads % full and care % covered, and each is now the same figure turned over or renamed, so a road at 161% full · 56% flow reads 62% served · 56% flow. The figure is the model's (CityNeeds.servedShare() and its owners' getters), and so is the verdict (CityNeeds.verdict()), on the lines NEEDS YOU already had, turned over: green with the ✓ at 100% or more and off the list, which for the networks means past 133% and for the road past 118%; red at or under the red line — 100% for power and water, 111% for the road, where its flow starts to fall, and care's and the schools' own lines; and amber between, worded short under 100% and tight from it. Transit, and a school NEEDS YOU does not list — under a class, or since 0.7.51 with no seats of its kind and short of half the smallest school — are judged by nothing and read blue, served alone. So it reads on Build's rings, tiles and suggestions, on Services, in the drawer, on Infrastructure, in People's (i) and in NEEDS YOU, and a served row takes the verdict's colour wherever it is drawn: where 0.7.20 had made a row whose fix was on site fall from red to amber, its sites now change only the listing and the order. NEEDS YOU's list, its order and its chip are as they were, and City History keeps its series. Left alone, not being supply over demand: the police against Canada's crime, cells, the unburied, burial plots, crematoria, land, the bank's % full, Trade's backing, pensions and the courses. Two figures for care can now disagree: Build's rings and NEEDS YOU read staffed places over people, unclamped, where Services' cards keep the month's coverage, which the model holds at 100%. BuildCardCheck's tenth section holds the verdict against NEEDS YOU's level at every load from 25% to 300%, and every served row against its owner's getter to the bit.

The price model on the screens

0.7.45 put 0.7.42 to 0.7.44 on the screens and moved nothing in the month: the anchor under the header's INFLATION tile, a PRICES row in NEEDS YOU, and THE ANCHOR beside THE CURRENCY'S DRIFT on Policy › Money (the anchor); THE BASKET on the same page; THE SHELF on Retail and WHAT IT CHARGES on the kitchens and the boutiques (goods and markets); WHO GOES WITHOUT on Promises' Food page, where the food assistance dial moved, and WHO GOES SHORT, AND WHY on People; 23 new series on City History, with the 7 it had never offered and the basket's links drawn as marks; and the fare, the grant, the pension and the bank's paid-in shown in today's money. The model fixes of 0.7.46 to 0.7.48 each made a screen say what the model does. Trade, care's fees by kind, the Food page's count and Construction's materials read the same after a load as before it (saves); City History records the bank's leverage ratio, graduates as gains and Health's outbreaks, and names an epidemic only while an outbreak runs (health); the Bank's capital chart reads the leverage ratio while it binds; Policy previews the savers' real rate as the month strikes it; and the fund has its WITHDRAWAL card, its mark at fair value for a share a year untraded, and its ticket's words for the rule's bid at the ask (the city's fund). Transit's riders by reason and its fare card's fuel are 0.7.49's (transport).

The inbox, and the money on the screen

The four red banners became an inbox: one live notice per condition, raised from the city's own state rather than restored from a record, carrying this month's figures and one button that goes to the screen that fixes it. A resolved notice gets no button. Since 0.7.8 it says when businesses are going bust and opens the bank's Lending page, since 0.7.14 the bank's request for preferred shares waits in it with Answer the bank, and pauses a running clock if the player has asked the game to pause on events, and since 0.7.22 it says when a rush is about to cost more than it gains and when a demolition is done. Since 0.7.20 an urgent notice is also a toast, and since 0.7.21 the envelope is the header's, red while something urgent is unread. Services carries a Safety area beside the utilities. City History plots the city's recorded series, with one heading per group — including The dead, which plots the running totals by band since the city was founded, the orphans and the people with no home among them, and the killed — on an axis that is the window rather than forced through zero, bar a per-cent axis with nothing negative on it, which starts at zero since 0.7.23 (below). The goods on City History are thirty-one rows, since 0.7.37 behind PRICES THIS MONTH's details (below): what a unit costs here this month, the world's band around it, and what crossed the border. There are keyboard shortcuts, the window's own full screen on F11, and since 0.7.20's check by eye Continue, Resume as it was, loads the autosave on a cold start; earlier editions said Resume did, and it opened the empty start-up world. Since 0.7.5, on a build page, Enter places every card with a quantity in the page's own order and stops at the first refusal with the rest still pending — since 0.7.40 as one run, funded whole through Build › Funding when it costs more than the cash (the city's borrowing), every order not placed staying on its card, the refused one among them, which had lost its count — and Backspace or Delete takes every quantity back to none (Jerus: "when you have let's say 3 ready to build, i want to be able to press enter to build"); the Build button's tooltip says so, a caption under the grid says so while anything is pending, and the Settings screen's list of keys, which had named three, names all six, Esc first leaving the full-screen chart since 0.7.23; since 0.7.24 it then closes the inbox's list and the City overview, pinned or not, before it opens the menu, and the inbox's list closes on a screen change and on the main menu, where it had stayed down across screens.

The money came onto the screen with it. Prices and the exchange rate sat beside the cash from 0.6.10, and the price of money from 0.7.4, on a strip that the header's tiles replaced in 0.7.21 (the currency). The Money page under Finances is the central bank's balance sheet in the player's words — issued and retired this month, printed since founding, the remittance, what the defence has spent, and M0 and M2 — drawn as pictures since 0.7.32 (the central bank). The monetary page under Policy, Money › The policy rate since 0.7.36, carries the dial and its chips, what the rule would set and where the dial stops, the autopilot, since 0.7.4 the target the rule aims at, the holdings dial, the advances ceiling, the real rate and what savers earn in real terms (above). The borrow pages draw the curve a column a term since 0.7.32, and the rollover and what happens when the bank fails, on both borrow pages from 0.7.13 and 0.7.14, are said once since, on the Finances hub (the city's borrowing); who holds the city's paper is Home & abroad's WHO HOLDS IT bar and each piece card's holders on The book; Finances has the businesses' bond market since 0.7.12 and the city's fund since 0.7.14; and Trade's currency page draws what moves the rate (the currency). The Government tab's bridge has every journal line as a step since 0.7.31, its arrears are rows of the CENTRAL BANK card, and the bank's desk foots; the Bank tab was rebuilt in 0.7.9 and redrawn in 0.7.33 (the bank). Since 0.7.4 the Sector economy list draws on each card the last 24 months of its net income, with its workers under it, and a Show more chip opens every card to revenue, margin, cash, what it owes the bank and posts filled against posts offered — Jerus had asked for "a little graph of its net income, perhaps how much workers it employs total"; the line was green or red on the latest month, and since 0.7.21 and 0.7.23 it is the business violet, grey for a month that made nothing, with a faint mark at each January; since 0.7.30 the list is fifteen cards under four figures (above), Show more opening revenue, margin, cash, what it owes its lenders and posts filled. Since 0.7.21 every sector page prints its money in the short form Construction has used since 0.7.20, as Jerus chose. And the People tab's EI paid this month reads what the treasury paid since 0.7.3, after the docs pass found it reading next month's bill, while the People and Summary screens read the care coverage the month actually used rather than one worked out from the beds.

City History's chart

The page was redrawn in 0.7.5. Jerus: "two graphs always displayed on the graph rail, the real gdp yearly figure, and the other is the population, and then the third one, the bigger one, the one where you set the stuff, it defaults to the borrowing rate, price level and inflation year on year". Two small charts sit at the top, real GDP over the rolling year and the population until the player pins others — a preference that follows the player from city to city and never enters a save; under them the big chart, with the presets, clear all and a log switch in a row above it, seeded on a first visit with What money costs, the city's rate, the price level and inflation. One unit is one real axis, two units are two real axes, and only a third brings back each line squashed to its own range. Recessions are shaded on all three charts, and from 0.7.5 to 0.7.22 the year book's episodes were named under the big chart's axis — Jerus's "little name generator": bank equity under zero is a Financial crisis of 2045, the rolling year of real output under the year before a Recession of, two years of it a Depression of, and so on through currency crises, inflations and deflations, epidemics, the treasury under zero and slumps, with runs under three months dropped and runs under six months apart joined (YearBook.episodes()), the same lines as the year book's WHAT HAPPENED, so the chart and the file cannot disagree. The picker folded into its groups under a filter box until 0.7.37 (below).

Since 0.7.23 the chart moves. Jerus: "in teh graphs you should be able to pan the chart just like yahoo finance does if you get what i mean, and it actually have interactive graph, and like also the crisis labels and all, dont you think that it should be more clear". City History's charts are drawn on a canvas (ui/TimeChart), with their arithmetic in ChartModel, which a harness can call without a screen. Drag to pan, wheel to zoom about the pointer, double-click to go back; 1Y · 5Y · 10Y · 50Y · All, opening on ten years (ChartModel.DEFAULT_RANGE), and a window left alone keeps to its range as the city grows. Under the plot an overview strip of the whole history carries the window, to drag, stretch at its edges or click outside. Years on every axis, and months once there is room for them; a crosshair card with every line's value in its own unit; legend chips that hide and show lines and say each line's unit and, with two axes, which side. Lines take their area's colour and never a verdict's. Each axis takes its own scale over the window, and a per-cent axis starts at zero when nothing on it is negative, which is what lifts a new city's flat rate off its flat price level: they sat on each other, and sit 136 pixels apart now. The second chart is no longer laid transparent over the first, and the two small charts follow the big chart's window.

Crises are named on the chart. Each recession band carries its recession's name; a hover on it gives the month, every line, the rule that named it and the band's depth, and a click, or a hover on the name, a card with the rule, the band's months and depth and the whole recession's span and worst, all off the year book's own records. Under the plot an episode lane draws every episode as a labelled span, overlapping ones on rows of their own, crises and depressions in red and the rest in amber — verdict colours, as the mockup drew them, a call made while Jerus slept. Under that a lane marked you carries the player's decisions as flags in their area's colour, because a decision is not a verdict: several in one month are one flag with a count, flags too close to tell apart merge, a hover lists them and a click pins the list, ten lines and a count of the rest. The names under the axis are gone, because the lane carries every one.

Full screen. Jerus: "let the player click fullscreen on the graph so the whole screen concentrates on the graph". The chart's Full screen lays it over the whole window, with City History's name and the clock's line in its toolbar; Esc or the button comes back, P leaves it and opens the menu, and any other screen closes it. The clock runs as it was, and each month redraws the chart in place; toasts and the inbox are under it, and a notice that pauses the clock shows as paused in its toolbar. F11 is still the window's own full screen. The game's other charts gained years under them too — at 0.7.23 the Bank tab's six, Finances' two and a company's share price — and their red, amber and lilac lines moved to the palette's colours; a sector card's sparkline and the header's tiles mark each January. The docs pass found a young city's chart that never narrowed again once it had opened on a short history, a grant change written to the log twice, a timer that could draw History over the next screen, a drag that could outlive full screen and a violet under the palette's own 4:1 contrast, and all five were fixed before it shipped.

Since 0.7.37 the page is in Build's style, the last of the rail's screens redrawn. Its frame is a head — City History with its (i), and Write the year book on 0.7.34's button at its right, its result a card under the head until × or the next write (the year book) — and a strip about the city: THE CITY, its age and the months recorded; HARD TIMES, every named episode since founding and how many touch the view; RUNNING NOW, what the history has not closed, a crisis before a watch and the newest first, an episode of ten years or more (CHRONIC_MONTHS) last and leading only alone, in the chart's verdict colour, a door that moves the chart there; and YOUR DECISIONS, the log's size, how many are in view and the last. Under it is a page at the stage's width, no longer the 0.7.5 column of 760 pixels: the two pins as cards, a click on a pin's name drawing that line alone on the big chart, 120 pixels tall so that the big plot ends above the fold at 1,389 × 868; the big chart at the page's width; and since 0.7.40 every chart follows the window, reading the viewport rather than its own width, and is cut at its edge, because a chart sized to a page it had itself widened crept right for ever. WHAT EACH LINE DID is a card a line, three across: the figure, its move in neutral ink with an arrow, a range bar from where the view starts to where it ends with the low and the high on hover, the figures counting on as a month lands, and a true minus on every figure. HARD TIMES AND YOUR DECISIONS lists the episodes touching the view, at most five, and the decisions in it, at most eight, each row moving the chart there, and behind details every episode since founding by kind on one scale of the city's months. PICK WHAT TO DRAW has an icon a group and every series the history keeps, 217 lines where it offered 148: GDP's four parts, M0, M2, the reserves, the advances and the remittance, the bank's provisions, dividends, allowance, capital ratio, target and return on equity, each band's dead a month, a SECTORS group with each sector's net income and workers, and each company's fair value beside its shares; since 0.7.35 a Parity line beside the exchange rate (fxParity, empty in an older history), since 0.7.45 23 more with the basket's links drawn as dashed marks, and since 0.7.46 the bank's leverage ratio and its target, graduates as gains and Health's outbreak. PRICES THIS MONTH counts the goods by how each is priced — with the world, at what it charges, between, at what it pays, open on one side, set by a seller — over every good on its band behind details, a seller-priced good naming its seller as a door to Sectors. The episode lane's colours read YearBook.isSevere(), which RUNNING NOW's order reads too, and since 0.7.46 an epidemic is named only while an outbreak runs, so the 2,213-month epidemic of the 2,400-month city is gone. The founding month's decisions, older than the history's first month, stand on that month with each decision's own month on its card (ChartModel.onAxis()).

The other charts are City History's own since the redraws: Finances' OWED AND THE RATE, the public debt and the borrowing rate on two axes with the borrowing decisions as flags (0.7.32), and Money's M2 with M0 and the advances; the Bank's ITS RATES and its six small charts (0.7.33), which show the last ten years and do not pan, Over the years opening City History; Trade's currency beside parity, with the currency's crises and the city's currency decisions as flags and no full-screen button (0.7.35, TimeChart.withoutFullScreen()); and the fund's worth on Portfolio and a security's price, with the fund's decisions as flags (0.7.39), the fund's three series kept but not on the picker. Since 0.7.38 a small chart draws the decisions it is handed on a smaller lane under its years, a hover giving the flag's card, and the founding month's decisions stand on the first month of every lane.

The decision log

The plan Jerus agreed asked for the player's own decisions on the timeline — "taxes to 17%", "central bank cut to 0%", "bank rescued" — and nothing in the game kept them: a tax rate is saved as what it is, not as when it moved, so the month a player cut it could be guessed from a step in a line and the reason for the step could not be. A decision is a flow, and a flow cannot be reconstructed from the state a month ended in, so since 0.7.23 it is recorded as it happens and saved (DecisionLog): a month, a kind and a line — Taxes to 17%, Central bank rate to 0.00%, Bank rescued for its shares, Rushed University. It is written in the one place each policy is applied, never in the interface, so a decision made from any screen or by a harness is recorded the same way, and a value set to what it already was writes nothing. What is logged: every tax and the farmland relief; every promise — the wage floor, the pension's two dials, EI's two, tuition, the grant and the student loan's rate, both health dials, the fare, a standing subsidy and since 0.7.43 food assistance; the central bank's dials and the hand taking or handing back the rate; the bank — a rescue, marked (automatic) when the treasury's setting made it, the preferred offer and the rescue setting; the fund's dial, since 0.7.48 its withdrawal, what is paid into it or drawn from it, and each order of its hand, since 0.7.39 written as the order it is, Order: buy D$4.0M of Construction shares at D$111,490, and a cancel as Cancelled: …, where it had read Fund to buy … of an order that might buy nothing — its fills and lapses are the fund's Activity, not decisions; an issue or a buyback of the city's paper, the rollover's setting and a default chosen abroad; a currency reform, reserves bought or sold and how land is paid for; and every hand on the construction queue. A proposal of several dials applied together is one flag with its count. What is not: ordinary build orders and land purchases, which a city places every month and which would smear the lane — a big one the treasury cannot pay for shows as the borrowing that paid for it; the rule moving the rate each month; the rollover's own issues, a foreign default the treasury could not avoid and the reserves a dollar issue buys as part of itself; and anything while a city is founded or loaded. Flags are coloured by their area — money blue, promises teal, the queue pink. A label is fixed text in the money of the day, and a later reform does not rewrite it; the log has no cap and is saved whole under one key. It was shown nowhere but the chart until the redraws; since, it is on City History's lane and in its lists, on the smaller lanes of the Bank's rates, Finances' OWED AND THE RATE and Trade's currency chart, each with its own kinds, and in Policy's RECENT DECISIONS.

The interface is a package

Until 18 September every screen was one class: UserInterface.java, 25,098 lines, 492 methods, a hundred banner sections, and a session working on the bank had to carry the policy tab's text in the same window. It was nineteen files in ui/ then and is twenty-six now, with Ladder (0.7.6), FoundingScreen (0.7.10), ConstructionScreen (0.7.22), TimeChart (0.7.23), InfrastructureScreen (0.7.28), FundScreen (0.7.39) and MapView (0.7.61, land), and the model never imports it. UserInterface is the window — the stage and its theme, the header with the clock, the money block, the five tiles, the Needs you chip, the rating and the inbox, the rail, the City overview's drawer and the construction panel's tab, the main menu and its backdrop, the panels and the dialogs, the time-skip dialog, the scroller every screen draws into — at about 6,100 lines, where it was about 4,000 at the split; its debt bar went in 0.7.24.
