Every tab is a class of its own, BankScreen, PolicyScreen, PeopleScreen and so on, its banners moved verbatim with a scripted ui. prefix wherever it reaches the window, the Infrastructure tab drawn by ServicesScreen until 0.7.28, and the City overview's content — the summary, the dashboard, the problem list — is SummaryScreen, though since 0.7.24 the problem list is measured in the model (CityNeeds). What every screen shares is four toolkit classes of stateless helpers, Money, Statement, Pieces and Levers, reached through an import static so that not one of their call sites changed, with Palette, Icons, Ladder and TimeChart beside them; the redraws put their shared parts in Pieces, the dial card in Levers (0.7.36), and the marks, the tracking and the idle words in Ladder. Twelve model members and two imports were the whole cost of the boundary. The move was mechanical — a dry run prints the couplings and the compiler catches what the script missed — and the one thing the compiler cannot check is the standing rule of this interface: the month arrives on a clock, so every panel is rebuilt under the player and must restore its own scroll position. Opening every tab on the split found three small things: the fare dial carried the player to the Policy tab the moment it was let go, because the staged-lever slider redrew the policy screen by name; the Build tab scrolled twice as fast as every other, because it is the one screen whose content sits straight in the page and a wheel floor meant for a notch was raised on every delta of a burst; and the Reports tab, City History now, moved under the pointer when a line was ticked, because the legend grows a block per line, so that one page keeps its position as pixels from the bottom.

The statement opens

Jerus: "can you make it so in the UI when you click on revenue or cogs, it expands and shows the individual items." One figure that is really ten is a figure a player can read and cannot act on. Four lines on every sector's income statement open now — Revenue by good, split city and abroad; the cost of sales by good, split city and imported; Wages by pay tier, with posts; and Sales tax remitted, charged, at the border and credited — and underneath them is a Sector.Split per good, booked from the trade rather than reconstructed from units times price, because a local sale and an export clear at different prices in the same month. The first thing it showed was that the city's own bakeries were shipping 62% of what they baked at the world's floor while the shops bought imported meat, fruit and fish by the half-million. A disclosure that discloses nothing is worse than none: a line with one good and nothing shipped does not open, because the caret would promise a breakdown and then repeat the figure above it — and the rule about how many rows there are has to count the rows, since Construction sells no goods at all and fell straight through a rule about goods to draw a caret onto one row that repeated its total. That other revenue turned out to be two businesses under one label, work recognised off the order book and this month's repairs billed to every standing building, and a city whose building work has stopped and whose repair income is still growing is a city that has finished growing. Since 0.7.19 the material escalation the owners paid is a third part of it, and since 0.7.22 the overtime on rushed sites a fourth. The parts are frozen into the statement when it is struck, like the per-good money, because a screen reading a working field late describes a month the statement above it is not about.

The same disclosure reached two more statements on 19 September. The Government tab's bridge from the budget to the treasury's cash opened its last row into the treasury's journal, and since 0.7.31 every journal line is a step of the bridge, with what the journal cannot explain printed under it as Not accounted for (tax, policy and GDP); and the bank's trading desk, whose opened lines had never added up to its total because the re-mark was left out, foots (the owners and the exchange). A row with nothing to disclose stays a plain row. Because the panel is rebuilt every month under the player, a line drawn closed every time snapped shut each month while the player was reading it; since 0.7.9 a screen can keep a set of the lines it has open (Statement.opens()), and the Bank tab was the first that did. Since 0.7.30 the Sectors screen keeps each business's opened lines, its waterfall's Revenue, Bought in, Wages and Sales tax bars opening their lines, and since 0.7.31 the Government tab keeps its opened lines, folds and bridge columns through a month.

The summary is a problem list

The summary panel used to be six fixed rows — economy, tax, labour, health, land, people. Of the sixteen problems a player can actually build their way out of it could show exactly one, health, and only as a sick rate. A city with no water, no cells and nowhere to bury its dead read exactly like a city with none of those problems.

It is two lists now, and since 0.7.24 a drawer, the City overview, behind the header's Needs you chip: the chip opens it on NEEDS YOU, Pin keeps it across screens and outside clicks, and Esc closes it pinned or not. Since 0.7.24 the list is measured in the model (CityNeeds), so the chip's count, the drawer and Build's Overview read one list. NEEDS YOU carries the conditions that have a lever — twenty-one with 0.7.24's FALLS DUE, and twenty-two with 0.7.45's PRICES — each row a door to the screen that owns the fix, and each disappearing when it is solved; when it is empty it names the condition closest to its yellow line instead, so it is never blank. FALLS DUE is red whenever any of the city's paper matures within three months, $X in N mo, the rollover's, or by hand with the rollover manual, its door Finances: the old strip's red maturity, kept red on Jerus's word. An amber TREASURY row reads $X, under a month's tax ($Y), where it read in hand; PRICES says whether the city still believes its central bank (the anchor); and since 0.7.32 the three Finances rows land where they say, whatever page the tab was left on. Since 0.7.20 a row whose fix is already a building on site, for anybody's order, says so and how long it will take. The served rows — power, water, the road, care and the schools — read supply over demand since 0.7.41, 233% served or 62% served · 56% flow, the road as one pair since 0.7.29; they are listed on the same lines as before, power and water from 75% of capacity, but coloured by one verdict, and keep it while their sites are on the way (above), and the drawer's RESOURCES line counts 1 short · 1 tight and can be red. HOW THE CITY IS carries seven readings with no dial — out of work, off sick, hungry, people a month, doubled up, ground used, the currency — always present, coloured when bad, and its door goes to the screen that diagnoses rather than the one that fixes: OFF SICK to Services' health overview since 0.7.28, on the model's lines (CityNeeds.SICK_YELLOW 6%, SICK_RED 12%), where it had opened whatever Services last showed; hungry the share of people since 0.7.27, where it had read Health's points beside a page showing the other figure; and the currency on the one parity rule since 0.7.35, its door opening The currency. Since 0.7.20 the Dashboard's GDP, and the ratios read off it, are annualised before a year is recorded; it had summed the months there were and called them a year.

Writing it turned up a model bug that no harness had: getEnergyRatio() and getWaterRatio() both return min(supply / demand, 1), so a row keyed off them could not appear until the lights were already out. They read load — demand over supply — now, against the 75% the dashboard has called "tight" all along, and since 0.7.41 the rows print it the other way up, served.

23

Time, saves & logs

There is no next month button: the month arrives on a clock, a save has to be the city it was to the cent, and everything the game says goes to a file.

Time runs on its own

The city runs on a clock at five seconds a month (SECONDS_PER_MONTH) and a speed the player picks off a snapping ladder — 0.1× · 0.25× · 0.5× · 1× · 2× · 5× · 10× · 20× · 50× — with the space bar as play/pause and the arrow keys moving the ladder; since 0.7.21 the ladder is the header clock's ‹ and › rather than a slider. The day inside the month is painted so the city looks alive between the ticks, even though nothing in the model moves until the month turns.

Three things that only matter once the month arrives unasked. A frame that takes a long time is clamped at a quarter second, so a stalled window cannot swallow thirty months while it was away — the worst a single frame can advance is 2.5 months. Redraw is capped at one in every 120ms, because at 50× a month lands every 100ms and the screen cannot usefully be redrawn faster than it can be read; and since 0.7.40 it waits while a mouse button is down and is drawn after the release (UserInterface.holdPresses()), because a click needs the press and the release on the same node, at 20× a month lands every 250ms, and a rebuild between the two swallowed it — Jerus had to click a button several times to have it go through. The clock runs the months meanwhile and marks the redraw pending, and a slider or a chart drag is no longer rebuilt under the hand. And a panel is now rebuilt while the player is still looking at it, so every scrolling page has to hold its own position across the rebuild rather than snapping to the top — which took seven attempts and a trace file to get right, and is written up on its own.

The game can pause itself when something happens, and by default it does not: the setting exists, and it is off, because a clock that stops without being asked is worse than a notice that waits. Since 0.7.22 the clock stands still while any question is up — a cancel, a demolition, a buy-out, the Quit dialog — so that nothing changes under a decision being made.

The calendar

Month 1 is January 2000. Purely presentational, with deliberately no reverse function — nothing should derive a month from a date string.

Skipping time

A skip takes a snapshot before, samples every month during, and diffs a snapshot after. Episodes cannot be reconstructed from endpoints: a city that starved for fifty months and recovered looks identical at both ends to one that never had a problem. The report counts months short of power, water, land and road capacity, months nothing was built, epidemics and their worst month, and the peak unburied — then writes plain sentences ordered by how much each should worry you. Since 0.7.8 it says what lenders wrote off, amber only past a sound book's loss, and since 0.7.15 a skip no longer stops at an empty treasury: it stops only when a month throws, and says what the central bank advanced over it and what is owed at the end. On the default seed on 1 October no skip refused to run, so the playtest never had to step a month by itself instead, and on 5 October, at 0.7.49, none did either.

The year book, as text and as CSV

Jerus: "csv if its cheaper token wise to read than xlsx, also keep the txt". Since 0.7.16 the export writes six files: year-book.txt and decade-book.txt as before, the same bytes 0.7.15 wrote bar the version on their first line, and beside each its main table and its WITHIN table, each year's worst and best month, as CSV — year-book.csv, year-book-within.csv, decade-book.csv and decade-book-within.csv, in the game's folder beside the log (GameFiles.yearBookCsv()). A CSV is plain text, so it is never dearer to read than an xlsx, which is zipped XML; Excel opens it as columns without an import step; and it needs no library. Each table is built once (YearBook.Table), and the text's block and the CSV are both written from it, so the two files cannot disagree. The format is RFC 4180: CRLF line ends, a field quoted only for a comma, a quote or a line break, the text's own column names as the header, the text's own strings in the cells — three significant figures and a point for the decimal whatever the locale — a blank left empty, and no BOM. Since 0.7.37 the button is in City History's head and its result a card under it — Written to the folder, a line of file names a book, anything that failed in red, and a × — where a message named the folder once and then each book's three files. The books have gained columns since: fxParity (0.7.35); the fund's worth and what was put in and taken out (0.7.39); expected inflation and credibility (0.7.42); the food assistance paid and the baskets it bought (0.7.43); 0.7.45's expected level, each of the index's five parts with its own level and weight, the month the basket in force was struck, the shelf price and its floor, the kitchens' and counters' margins with their targets, the baskets asked for and handed over, the households assisted, and the hunger with its priced-out share; and 0.7.46's bankLeverageRatio, bankLeverageTarget and outbreak, with graduates counted as gains. Since 0.7.46 the books name an epidemic on Health's outbreaks, where they had read the sick rate, and so named one that lasted 2,213 months (health). Its first locale test passed a writer that formatted by locale, because it wrote the CSV after restoring the locale, and was fixed. Left small for Jerus: an episodes CSV, full precision, a friendlier message when a CSV is open in Excel, and an Excel set to a comma decimal, which opens a comma CSV as one column.

History past five hundred years

A history keeps its newest six thousand months, five hundred years, a month to an entry (HistorySave.MONTHLY_KEPT), and since 0.7.55 a calendar year all of whose months are older than that folds into one entry at its last month, each series by the year book's own rule for it (YearBook.kindOf()), so that a folded year is exactly the book's row: a flow added — GDP and its parts, the births and deaths, revenue and every tax — a level taken at the year's end — cash, debt, the population, the money stock — and a rate averaged — the policy rate, the exchange rate, the price index. The history keeps how many months each entry holds, and its readers weigh by it: a folded flow reads a month at a time as its monthly average (aligned()), totals and running totals read what was stored, a rolling year counts twelve months and not twelve entries, the year book's rows and the fund's return weigh each entry by its months, and City History's crosshair names a folded entry the year Y, kept whole. The first fold comes at month 6,012 of a history begun at month 1, and the newest six thousand months are always kept one to an entry; only the months inside the old years are given up. Where nothing has folded every reader is what it was to the bit, and the default playtest's 4,005 months never fold. An older history reads all its entries as months, and HistoryCheck grows one to 6,041 months and holds the fold, the totals, the save and every reader across the seam.

Saves

Ten numbered slots plus an autosave, under %APPDATA% on Windows and the platform equivalent elsewhere — chosen because Steam Cloud syncs from known roots and a bare home folder is not one. Writes go to a temp file, back up the existing save, then atomically move; the worst case is a stray temp file and a save one autosave old. History graphs are per slot. Autosave fires every twelve months, before any skip, and on quit, and since 0.7.20 Quit asks first. Since 0.7.52 the twelve-monthly one is written at the month's end, once the month is recorded. It was written at the top, after the calendar turned and before anything ran, so the file said month N on a city that had finished N − 1, and a city loaded from it never ran N — Jerus's, saved by 0.7.49 at month 1,851, held its history to 1,850 and went on to 1,852. An autosave written before 0.7.52 still loads that way, its month gone and a one-month gap left in its history, because its month's top had run and would run again; the next one, twelve months on, holds a whole month. The two others were always written between the presses. SaveSlotCheck loads all three and plays on. The load list names each city and slot and the autosave once, Autosave · month 13, and since 0.7.21 the main menu's YOUR CITIES reads the three newest slots' headers.

The save format is at 31, and its changelog is the best history the project has. Older saves load, back to format 21; what is refused is a save from a newer build and one from before the sector template. Nineteen carried the household balance sheet, twenty the repriced unit of material — an old yard read at its old value — and twenty-one the sector template, which was a clean break. Twenty-two was business services, twenty-three manufacturing, twenty-four the farms, twenty-five the bank's own cost of funds, twenty-six the price index's water-marks and twenty-seven the sixth age band — which also made every band-indexed array save its band names beside its values, so the day a seventh band arrives no existing file is read at the wrong offsets. Twenty-eight is the player's hand on the queue (0.7.22), under one key: a format-27 build handed such a save would load it wrongly, not merely incompletely, because a stopped shell and a demolition on site hold ground and are off their stacks, and an older build would come back with the ground free, the work gone and the money spent. Twenty-nine is the decision log (0.7.23), under one key: a format-28 build would load the city, play on, and write it back without its decisions at its next autosave, and a decision is a flow nothing else in the save can give back. Both are safe the other way: a 27 save loads with no order set and nothing rushed, stopped or being demolished, and a 28 with an empty log, which is what each city had. Thirty is the month the People page draws (0.7.27): Migration's last month — the draw and its two halves, the pulls, the arrivals and the departures and who made them up, the mixes and the licences — after its wage history, the pyramid's dead by cause after its flows, and the share the shops handed over and the hungry at full shelves after the households' row array. A format-29 build handed such a save would load it wrongly: each of the three arrays is a length it refuses whole, so it would come back with no wage history, nobody able to leave for a year, and a pyramid of nobody. A 29 save loads with those figures at nothing until a month runs, which is what a reloaded People page showed before. Thirty-one is the land on the world (0.7.57): the city's centre, its forty lanes, every purchase in its order, the forty offers standing, what has been taken out of its ground, and the world's totals and sea level. A format-30 build handed such a save would load it wrongly, with no ore under a city whose mines stand on it and nine fresh parcels; the other way, a format-30 save is converted onto its world once, as it loads (land).

The thirteen foods went in without a bump, because goods have been saved by name since the sector template: a good this build does not know is dropped and says so in the log, which is what happens to an old city's warehouse of FOOD. And so did everything the batches of two editions ago added — five sectors, the freight band, the three streams, the modes and the fare, the railway's quote and its locomotives, every sector's fleet, the households' cars, their investment income, the luxury baseline and the dinners they ate — because a sector is keyed by name, a market is keyed by name, and every new figure in a household cell is appended to the end of an array whose reader checks its length. A city saved before them opened with no cars, no fleets, no kitchens, and the rolling stock its track implies, which is exactly what that city was; an eleven-sector build was proved to load a ten-sector save to the cent before the first of the five shipped. The central bank went in without a bump too, and so did everything around it: the treasury's journal for both months and its raised counter; the two health dials, the coverage each month used and a cell slot for the share of a cell's people who could pay for care; the grant's basis and amount, the loan's rate and the tuition scale on the policy array's tail; the vault in dollars and its revaluation (slots 22 and 23 of the foreign accounts), the debt's revaluation (24), the defence (25–27) and the real rate differential with its two inflations (28–30); what the bank still owes for paper sold between the presses, under two keys; the central bank's books, the arrears, the autopilot, the policy rate boxed so that a 0% dial reloads at 0%, the holdings dial and the advances ceiling, each under its own key; who holds every bond, saved with the bond; the households' paper as the thirty-first slot of a cell, with its book ratio; and a buyback's holders outside the pools. The fold and the spend factor added nothing: the first is a rule about counts, and the second is struck again on load. A city saved by 0.6.7 opened in 0.7.3 with an empty central bank, the vault it had bought if it bought one, the bank holding all of its paper and nothing in arrears — exactly what that city was — and a city saved by 0.7.2 pays one month's EI twice on its first month, once. Between them the long sick, the people outside the families, the household record and the whole crime system went in without a bump, deliberately: Gson leaves a missing key alone, and a format-21 city loads with each of them empty, which is correct for that city. A big saved city comes back with only its founding constabulary and shows the crime notice until it builds a station.

The batches from 0.7.4 to 0.7.15 went in without a bump as well, each batch's new figures under keys of their own or on the end of an array whose reader knows its older widths: the inflation target's key, the three tax bases and then a tuition scale for each of the nine kinds of school on the policy array's tail, each school kind's month on the end of the schools' array; the history's new series — two a sector, GDP's four parts, the dial, the bank's prime, deposit rate and fees, and six of its capital — with an older history's strain premium skipped on load; the land office's choice of how to pay, its listing behind a marker that says its prices are dollars, the office's dollar ground price as a fourth slot, and the land's dollars as slots 31–36 of the foreign accounts; the bank's pricing record and its late profit, its allowance, its capital record, its month's lines and a year of them, its year of balance sheets and its equity's two parts; every borrower's last quarter, the builders' salvage and a statement's stock paid for earlier; the households' account fee on the end of their statement; the eight keys of the founding record; the mortgages, saved among the business debts under their own type with the insurance's three counters and two lines on the government's month; the bond market, each cell's bonds as the thirty-second slot and bond by bond by name, the exchange's books, and the interim loans under a type of their own; the rollover's setting, ledger and record; the city's fund, the bank's preferred and its record, three slots a company on the register for the city's holdings and a city holder on every bond, and the four inputs the debt market's rate was struck from; and what the central bank has paid the households for their paper and paid at issue rolling its own. A 0.7.15 save with the holdings dial past half, opened in 0.7.14, loads at half.

And from 0.7.16 to 0.7.21 nothing needed one. 0.7.16, 0.7.18, 0.7.20 and 0.7.21 put nothing in the save: the year book's CSV is a file it writes, the best-paid fill is a function of the posts, the workers and the licences, all saved already, and the remembered build category, the receipts, the toasts and the fonts are the window's. 0.7.17 put the builders' share of their posts, its need and the fill it was struck on in their sector's extras; 0.7.19 each payer's contract on site with the material its quote allowed for and the share of the tax it gets back, the sectors' capital purchases in the month's ledger, the builders' escalation in their extras, the bank's customers and its operating cost in its month's lines with two slots on the end of its last month, and the grant's marker that its fixed amount is real on the end of the tax policy's state. The pins on City History are a preference in settings.json, not the city's. A city saved by the build the last edition described, 0.7.15 at format 27, opens in this one as the city it was, bar the construction points it had parked on stacks with nothing left to build, which are dropped on load and move no money (7,801,561 in Jerus's city): its builders offering every post until its first month strikes their crews, each contract on site read as its owner's with the escalation starting at the load, its grant on the basis it was played on, no order set on the queue and nothing rushed, stopped or coming down, and an empty decision log. From its first month this build's rules apply to it.

Nor has anything since. 0.7.24 to 0.7.26 put nothing in the save, and from 0.7.28 to 0.7.52 every figure went under a key of its own, among a sector's extras, or on the end of an array whose reader knows its older widths, and a missing key or slot seeds. 0.7.29 put the railway's allowed bill and what went abroad in its extras, written only when known; 0.7.31 puts the last ten years of GDP back from the history on a load, so a loaded city's year is its own, where every load had reset it to one month; 0.7.39 the fund's ledger and the hand's posted orders inside the fund's own state, and three series on the history, an older save's ledger seeded at its load with one row saying so. The price model went in the same way: 0.7.42 the anchor under its own key, a save from before it seeded at a credibility of 0.80 and an expected level of 1.0 because its constants are still at founding money; 0.7.43 the chained basket behind a marker an older array lacks, the food assistance dial on the policy's tail and the shops' extras; 0.7.44 the suppliers' credit among the shops' extras; and 0.7.45 each part's own chained level. Then the model fixes saved what a reloaded city had read as nothing or struck again: 0.7.46 the month's trade in units, the balance of payments' month on the foreign accounts' slots 37–44, care's fees by kind, each cell's income after its fixed bills as a cell slot, and Construction's struck materials; 0.7.48 the fund's withdrawal and what it sells for and paid late; and 0.7.49 transit's bill, the share of workers with no car of their own, a journey's fuel and the households' fuel by row; and 0.7.52 how strictly the rule holds the target, by name under its own key. A save from before each loads as the city it was: Trade's month not counted yet and care's fees unsplit until a month runs, the fund at Norway's 3%, transit's bill derived at the load, the rule at Standard. 0.7.53 and 0.7.54 put nothing new in it, and 0.7.53 widened what was there: the population, in the save and in its header, the workforce, the businesses' population trend and ten of the history's series — the posts, the workforce, the out of work, the population, the births, deaths, arrivals and departures, the homes and the builders' capacity — can pass 2,147,483,647, which a JSON number does not notice, so an older save loads into them as it did, and the save of a city past it is one an older build marks unreadable rather than loading it wrong. ScaleCheck saves one of five billion and loads it back figure for figure. The fixes moved no trace on their own: 0.7.46's eight traces were 0.7.45's to the byte. A newer save is not safe the other way: a 0.7.45 build handed a 0.7.49 save refuses its cells, its care and the households' statement whole, at widths it does not know, which is the downgrade the number guards and is on the list.

From 0.7.55 to 0.7.64 one thing needed a bump, the land. 0.7.55 put the land price's parts — the crowding, the premium and the world's price level — on the end of the office's price state, an older save reading its premium back from its price, and the months each folded entry of the history holds, an older history having none; 0.7.56 the world's seed under a key of its own, a save without it deriving one; and 0.7.57 the land on the world, which is format 31 (above), the old listing, iron pool and tonnes read only to convert. Then nothing needed one again. 0.7.58 put the goods the sectors hold on the end of the national accounts' slots, an older save's first month booking no change in them; 0.7.59 the water rights, a save without them given what its plants already pump; 0.7.60 the map's stamp, the map itself a file of its own beside the save, slot-NN-map.bin, written through the same swap, whose format 2 since 0.7.64 has an older sidecar drawn again once; 0.7.62 the drivers' fuel as 6d drew it — the bill, its imported part and the litres — a save without it importing every litre, as it struck, and the railway's imported fuel among its extras; and 0.7.63 the graduates waiting for the next census to carry their loans and the four ratios each sector's month was run at, two figures only the month sets: without them Jerus's city came back 7,447 units short a month after a load, its graduates' loans left with the students, and city2400, short of power, billed its saved month 0.02% dear. A save from before 0.7.46's units has them derived on load (Sector.deriveCarriedTrade()), and every save writes them now, empty or not. 0.7.61 put nothing in it. Jerus's two 0.7.63 saves, his autosave at month 416 and slot 3 at month 212, load in 0.7.64 with every key of the file read, play twelve months, and save and reload to the bit in every pool the money audit reads and in their people. A city saved by 0.7.54, at format 30, the build the last edition described, opens in this one on its own world: its seed derived, its ground a centre and forty offers listed round it, its water rights what its plants pump, its drivers' fuel all imported until its first month draws it, and its map drawn when the land office first asks for it.

A city saved by 0.7.23, at format 29, the build an earlier edition described, opens in this one as the city it was, bar four things: its People page's month reads nothing until a month runs; its anchor is seeded and its basket waits a month to link, the first month stepping the year's rate as the shelf turns sticky; its fixed grant, new-home rebates and account fee come back about 10% lower, because they are struck at an expected level of 1.0 where the old city had indexed them, 1.11 in the 2,400-month city; and its fund draws at Norway's 3% and its treasury pays transit's bill from the first month. And a load since 0.7.25 hands the investors' planner the bank, as a month does: until then the planner had no bank for the first month after every load, and its word read Holding: no bank.

Logs

Every println in the game already goes to a rotating log file, because a logging system that needs a thousand edits does not get adopted. In a packaged build there is no console, so all of it previously went nowhere. Reports and graphs to the log default off, so the 2 MB log holds the crash rather than three centuries of ASCII graphs. The decision log prints nothing: it is in the save and on the chart.

24

How it's checked

Seventy-nine self-hosted harnesses plus a 4,005-month playtest that audits every month, asserts money is conserved to the cent, and reloads the city repeatedly to prove a saved game and a played one agree. AllChecks runs all eighty, one JVM each, in about nine minutes — 318 seconds on 5 October, 381 at 0.7.52, 21 of them CentralBankCheck's with its new probe city, 408 at 0.7.54, 28 of them OrderSearchCheck's and 7 ScaleCheck's, 628 at 0.7.62 and 546 at 0.7.64, 94 of them the playtest's, 24 ScaleCheck's and 12 MapCheck's, in a cloud session where the one that needs JavaFX, BuildMenuCheck, is skipped: seventy-eight green and one red, HealthCheck. There were sixty-six at the edition of 0.7.23; the thirteen since are BuildAdviceCheck (0.7.24), BuildCardCheck (0.7.25), SectorFlowCheck (0.7.30), PolicyPreviewCheck (0.7.36), FundLedgerCheck (0.7.39), ExpectationsCheck (0.7.42), GroceryCheck (0.7.43), SupplierCreditCheck (0.7.44), ScaleCheck (0.7.53), OrderSearchCheck (0.7.54), WorldCheck (0.7.56), MapCheck (0.7.60) and OilCheck (0.7.62). BuildMenuCheck had failed 73 checks since 0.7.19, because its price check was older than the builders' sales tax; since 0.7.21 it checks the taxed price, worked from the price rule's own inputs rather than read back off the quote (Jerus: "Update to the taxed price"), and it runs in the cloud on its own against the Windows JavaFX jars, where at 0.7.23, at 0.7.49, at 0.7.51, at 0.7.52 and again at 0.7.54 it read PASS, 73 of 73, and since the catalogue grew, 74 of 74 at 0.7.59 and 76 of 76 at 0.7.62 and at 0.7.64. HealthCheck's red line reads the hunger of a city charging fifteen times the founding fees for care against a free one's over their last year, and since partway through 0.7.12 the dear city has been hungrier by more than the price of care — 8.4 points against a tolerance of five on 28 September, 40.0 on 1 October, and on 5 October, at 0.7.49, with hunger counted in baskets since 0.7.43, 10.1: 2.2% against 12.3% (health); it shipped red on Jerus's word, "Ship with it red, flagged", and has a batch of its own on the list. The line that stood here before it, InfrastructureCheck's "and its shops can actually be supplied", which Jerus put down to roads — "its fine, we will balance later." — went green by accident on one build of 0.7.2 and red again on the next, and has passed on every suite since 0.7.7; the fixture is sensitive to the young city's rates, so it stays on the list. Every count and constant on this page is struck from the tree at 0.7.49, in the passages 0.7.50 and 0.7.51 changed, at 0.7.51, in 0.7.52's at 0.7.52, and in 0.7.53's and 0.7.54's at 0.7.54, and in every passage 0.7.55 to 0.7.64 changed at 0.7.64; every measurement carries its date or its batch, and "the default seed on 5 October" is the plain playtest at 0.7.49, as "1 October" was 0.7.23's and "28 September" 0.7.15's. RealismCheck, a probe outside the build, is the rebalance's instrument and the thing to run first if any real-world figure is questioned. And since 18 September the playtest is the gate for a refactor as well as for a mechanic: it is deterministic, two baseline runs are byte-identical, and every move out of Game and every comment-only pass was proved by a run that compared equal to the morning's, bar its wall-clock line. The four interface batches proved the same way that they left the model's default run alone: the default playtest's eight trace files — the month, the borrowing, the houses, the people, the bank, the bonds, the building and the labour — came out byte-identical to 0.7.19's at 0.7.20, 0.7.21, 0.7.22 and 0.7.23, so a reading of the default seed taken at 0.7.19 still held at 0.7.23, and so they did at every batch to 0.7.41, the redraws among them. The price model was the first to move them on purpose: they were re-baselined at 0.7.42, 0.7.43 and 0.7.44, each harness premise the new design made wrong listed for Jerus and changed, and no tolerance loosened; 0.7.45 and 0.7.46 left them byte-identical; and they were re-baselined again at 0.7.47 for the floor in today's money, at 0.7.48 for the fund and at 0.7.49 for transit, each new assertion shown to fail on the build before; 0.7.50 to 0.7.54 — the chart crash, the build advice, the autosave's month and the bank's strictness, the counts that can pass 2.1 billion, and the order searches — left them byte-identical. On the world's land they moved again, each move explained before the next batch began: re-baselined at 0.7.55 for the crowding premium, at 0.7.57 for the land on the world, twice at 0.7.58 for early iron and then for a test player that keeps its ground ahead, at 0.7.59 for the water limit, at 0.7.62 for fuel and at 0.7.64 for whole fields; 0.7.56's world, 0.7.60's map, 0.7.61's view and 0.7.63's save fixes left them byte-identical, and since 0.7.60 the playtest keeps its city's map up every month and reads it back the same at every reload. The test player plays by rules of its own that moved with them: since 0.7.58 it keeps about 5% of its dry ground free ahead of six months' growth, the build advice's own line, spending at most a tenth of its cash a look (LongPlaytest.keepGroundAhead()), because on the world's smaller offers a player that bought only when it was full grew a city half the size; since 0.7.59 it orders water plants only for the water its lakes yield; and since 0.7.64 it buys its iron a whole field at a time (land). Since 0.7.42 a change to the model is also scored on an ensemble, sixteen seeds under five policies against criteria written as code before the first number was seen (the anchor). MoneyAudit.tolerance() is a cent, or a line's own tighter floor, while the figures compared are under $10T, and past that a part in a trillion of them where that is more; seven harnesses read through it the lines a ten-billion city's own rounding missed, and in every harness city it is the bar it was. What it printed and did not assert until 0.7.55 was the land premium's doing: linear in the city's size, at five and ten billion it priced land so dear that the businesses borrowed to pay its property tax, the treasury swelled and the copies lost people every month — Jerus's call, from the scale study, which made the premium crowding. Since 0.7.57 a copy's land is drawn again to hold K times the ground.
